\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.63307pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.63307pt\raisebox{-7.75671pt}{\makebox[10.63307pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{r^{\prime}}$}}}\hskip-10.63307pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[10.63307pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}$}}}B

(i.e., the number of bits required to write down the proof). In our applications, the bit complexity of all
sum-of-squares proofs will be
n^{O(\ell)}
(assuming that all numbers in the input have bit complexity
n^{O(1)}
).
This bound suffices in order to argue about pseudo-distributions that satisfy polynomial constraints approximately.

The following fact shows that every property of low-level pseudo-distributions can be derived by low-degree sum-of-squares proofs.

Fact 2.18

(Completeness)
.

Suppose that
d\geqslant r^{\prime}\geqslant r
and
\mathcal{A}
is a collection of polynomial constraints with degree at most
r
,
and
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.00003pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.00003pt\raisebox{-1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.00003pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-8.00003pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.00003pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}$}}}\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{2}\leqslant B\}
for some finite
B
.
Let
\{g\geqslant 0\}
be a polynomial constraint. If every degree-
d
pseudo-distribution that satisfies
D\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-1.23135pt]{7.92819pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-7.92819pt\rule[0.75348pt]{7.92819pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-7.92819pt\raisebox{-5.96742pt}{\makebox[7.92819pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{r}$}}}\hskip-7.92819pt\raisebox{2.95354pt}{\makebox[7.92819pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}$}}}\mathcal{A}

also satisfies
D\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-1.23135pt]{10.63307pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.63307pt\rule[0.75348pt]{10.63307pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.63307pt\raisebox{-8.74913pt}{\makebox[10.63307pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{r^{\prime}}$}}}\hskip-10.63307pt\raisebox{2.95354pt}{\makebox[10.63307pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}$}}}\{g\geqslant 0\}
, then for every
\varepsilon>0
,
there is a sum-of-squares proof
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.16281pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.16281pt\raisebox{-6.82222pt}{\makebox[8.16281pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{d}$}}}\hskip-8.16281pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.16281pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}$}}}\{g\geqslant-\varepsilon\}
.

Basic Sum-of-Squares Proofs.

We will require the following basic SoS proofs.

Fact 2.19

(Operator norm Bound)
.

Let
A
be a symmetric
d\times d
matrix and
v
be a vector in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then, we have that

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.25113pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.25113pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[8.25113pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-8.25113pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.25113pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\left\{v^{\top}Av\leqslant\|A\|_{2}\|v\|^{2}_{2}\right\}\;.

Fact 2.20

(SoS Hölder’s Inequality)
.

Let
f_{i},g_{i}
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant s
, be scalar-valued indeterminates.
Let
p
be an even positive integer.
Then,

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{14.66982pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-14.66982pt\raisebox{-8.20114pt}{\makebox[14.66982pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{p^{2}}$}}}\hskip-14.66982pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[14.66982pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f,g}$}}}$}}}\left\{\left(\frac{1}{s}\sum_{i=1}^{s}f_{i}g_{i}^{p-1}\right)^{p}\leqslant\left(\frac{1}{s}\sum_{i=1}^{s}f_{i}^{p}\right)\left(\frac{1}{s}\sum_{i=1}^{s}g_{i}^{p}\right)^{p-1}\right\}\,.

Observe that using
p=2
above yields the SoS Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.

Fact 2.21

(SoS Almost Triangle Inequality)
.

Let
f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{r}
be indeterminates. Then, we have that

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{44.17969pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-44.17969pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[44.17969pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-44.17969pt\raisebox{3.96112pt}{\makebox[44.17969pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{r}}$}}}$}}}\left\{\left(\sum_{i\leqslant r}f_{i}\right)^{2t}\leqslant r^{2t-1}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{r}f_{i}^{2t}\right)\right\}\,.

Fact 2.22

(SoS AM-GM Inequality, see Appendix A of
[
BKS15
]
)
.

Let
f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{m}
be indeterminates. Then, we have that

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{46.78969pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-46.78969pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[46.78969pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{m}$}}}\hskip-46.78969pt\raisebox{3.96112pt}{\makebox[46.78969pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{m}}$}}}$}}}\left\{\left(\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}\right)^{m}\geqslant\Pi_{i\leqslant m}f_{i}\right\}\,.

We defer the proofs of the two subsequent lemmas to Appendix
A
.

Lemma 2.23

(Spectral SoS Proofs)
.

Let
A
be a
d\times d
matrix. Then for
d
-dimensional vector-valued indeterminate
v
, we have:

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.25113pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.25113pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[8.25113pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-8.25113pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.25113pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\left\{v^{\top}Av\leqslant\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right\}\,.

Fact 2.24

(Cancellation within SoS, Lemma 9.2
[
BK20b
]
)
.

Let
a,C
be scalar-valued indeterminates. Then,

\left\{a\geqslant 0\right\}\cup\left\{a^{t}\leqslant Ca^{t-1}\right\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{16.82747pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-16.82747pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[16.82747pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-16.82747pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[16.82747pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{a,C}$}}}$}}}\left\{a^{2t}\leqslant C^{2t}\right\}\,.

Lemma 2.25

(Frobenius Norms of Products of Matrices)
.

Let
B
be a
d\times d
matrix valued indeterminate for some
d\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, for any
0\preceq A\preceq I
,

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.38293pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.38293pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.38293pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.38293pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[10.38293pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{B}$}}}$}}}\left\{\left\lVert AB\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leqslant\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\right\}\,,

and,

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.38293pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.38293pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.38293pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.38293pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[10.38293pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{B}$}}}$}}}\left\{\left\lVert BA\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leqslant\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\right\}\,,

2.3
Analytic Properties of Gaussian Distributions

The following definitions and results describe the analytic properties of Gaussian distributoins that we will use. We also state the guarantees of known robust estimation algorithms for estimating the mean, covariance and moment tensors of Gaussian mixtures here.

Certifiable Subgaussianity.

We will make essential use of the following definition.

Definition 2.26

(Certifiable Subgaussianity (Definition 5.1 in
[
KS17b
]
))
.

For
t\in\mathbb{N}
and an absolute constant
C>0
, a distribution
\mathcal{D}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is said to be
t
-certifiably
C
-subgaussian
if for every even
t^{\prime}\leqslant t
, we have that

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{9.72566pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-9.72566pt\raisebox{-7.75671pt}{\makebox[9.72566pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t^{\prime}}$}}}\hskip-9.72566pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[9.72566pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\left\{\E_{\mathcal{D}}\langle x,v\rangle^{t^{\prime}}\leqslant(Ct^{\prime})^{t^{\prime}/2}\left(\E_{\mathcal{D}}\langle x,v\rangle^{2}\right)^{t^{\prime}/2}\right\}\,.

Fact 2.27

(Mixtures of Certifiably Subgaussian Distributions, Analogous to Lemma 5.4 in
[
KS17b
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{q}
be
t
-certifiably
C
-subgaussian distributions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Let
p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{q}
be non-negative weights such that
\sum_{i}p_{i}=1
and
p=\min_{i\leqslant q}p_{i}
.
Then, the mixture
\sum_{i}p_{i}\mathcal{D}_{i}
is
t
-certifiably
C/p
-subgaussian.

Certifiable Anti-Concentration.

The first is
certifiable anti-concentration
— an SoS formulation of classical anti-concentration inequalities — that was introduced in
[
KKK19
,
RY20a
]
.

In order to formulate certifiable anti-concentration, we start with a univariate even polynomial
p
that serves as a uniform approximation to the delta function at
0
in an interval around
0
. Such polynomials are constructed
in
[
KKK19
,
RY20a
]
(see also
[
DGJ+10
]
).
Let
q_{\delta,\Sigma}(x,v)
be a multivariate (in
v
) polynomial defined by

q_{\delta,\Sigma}(x,v)=\left(v^{\top}\Sigma v\right)^{2s}p_{\delta,\Sigma}\left(\frac{\langle x,v\rangle}{\sqrt{v^{\top}\Sigma v}}\right)
.
Since
p_{\delta,\Sigma}
is an even polynomial,
q_{\delta,\Sigma}
is a polynomial in
v
.

Definition 2.28

(Certifiable Anti-Concentration)
.

A mean-
0
distribution
D
with covariance
\Sigma
is
2s
-certifiably
(\delta,C\delta)
-anti-concentrated
if for
q_{\delta,\Sigma}(x,v)
defined above, there exists a degree-
2s
sum-of-squares proof of the following
two unconstrained polynomial inequalities in indeterminate
v
:

\left\{\langle x,v\rangle^{2s}+\delta^{2s}q_{\delta,\Sigma}(x,v)^{2}\geqslant\delta^{2s}\left(v^{\top}\Sigma v\right)^{2s}\right\}\text{ , }\left\{\E_{x\sim D}q_{\delta,\Sigma}(x,v)^{2}\leqslant C\delta\left(v^{\top}\Sigma v\right)^{2s}\right\}\,.

An isotropic subset
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is
2s
-certifiably
(\delta,C\delta)
-anti-concentrated
if the uniform distribution on
X
is
2s
-certifiably
(\delta,C\delta)
-anti-concentrated.

Remark 2.29
.

The function
s(\delta)
can be taken to be
O(\frac{1}{\delta^{2}})
for standard Gaussian distribution and the uniform distribution on the unit sphere (see
[
KKK19
]
and
[
BK20a
]
).

Certifiable Hypercontractivity.

Next, we define
certifiable hypercontractivity
of degree-
2
polynomials that formulates (within SoS)
the fact that higher moments of degree-
2
polynomials of distributions (such as Gaussians)
can be bounded in terms of appropriate powers of their 2nd moment.

Definition 2.30

(Certifiable Hypercontractivity)
.

An isotropic distribution
\mathcal{D}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is said to be
h
-certifiably
C
-hypercontractive
if there is a degree-
h
sum-of-squares proof of the following unconstrained polynomial inequality
in
d\times d
matrix-valued indeterminate
Q
:

\E_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}(x^{\top}Qx)^{h}\leqslant(Ch)^{h}\left(\E_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}(x^{\top}Qx)^{2}\right)^{h/2}\,.

A set of points
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is said to be
C
-certifiably hypercontractive
if the uniform distribution on
X
is
h
-certifiably
C
-hypercontractive.

Hypercontractivity is an important notion in high-dimensional probability and analysis on product spaces
[
O’D14
]
. Kauers, O’Donnell, Tan and Zhou
[
KOTZ14
]
showed certifiable hypercontractivity of Gaussians and more generally product distributions with subgaussian marginals. Certifiable hypercontractivity strictly generalizes the better known
certifiable subgaussianity
property (studied first in
[
KS17b
]
) that controls higher moments of linear polynomials.

Observe that the definition above is affine invariant. In particular, we immediately obtain:

Fact 2.31
.

Given
t\in\mathbb{N}
, if a random variable
x
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
has
t
-certifiable
C
-hypercontractive degree-
2
polynomials, then so does
Ax
for any
A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
.

As observed in
[
KS17b
]
, the Gaussian distribution is
t
-certifiably
1
-subgaussian and
t
-certifiable

1
-hypercontractive for every
t
. Next, we establish certifiable hypercontractivity for mixtures of Gaussians. We defer the proofs to Appendix
A
.

Lemma 2.32

(Shifts Cannot Decrease Variance)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
Q
be a
d\times d
matrix-valued indeterminate, and
C
be a scalar-valued indeterminate.
Then, we have that

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{18.7876pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-18.7876pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[18.7876pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-18.7876pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[18.7876pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q,C}$}}}$}}}\left\{\E_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\left(Q(x)-\E_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}[Q(x)]\right)^{2}\right]\leqslant\E_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\left(Q(x)-C\right)^{2}\right]\right\}\,.

Lemma 2.33

(Shifts of Certifiably Hypercontractive Distributions)
.

Let
x
be a mean-
0
random variable with distribution
\mathcal{D}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
t
-certifiably
C
-hypercontractive degree-
2
polynomials. Then, for any fixed constant vector
c\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the random variable
x+c
also has
t
-certifiable
4C
-hypercontractive degree-
2
polynomials.

Lemma 2.34

(Mixtures of Certifiably Hypercontractive Distributions)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{k}
have
t
-certifiable
C
-hypercontractive degree-
2
polynomials on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
for some fixed constant
C
. Then, any mixture
\mathcal{D}=\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathcal{D}_{i}
also has
t
-certifiably
(C/\alpha)
-hypercontractive
degree-
2
polynomials for
\alpha=\min_{i\leqslant k,w_{i}>0}w_{i}
.

Corollary 2.35

(Certifiable Hypercontractivity of Mixtures of
k
Gaussians)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a
k
-mixture of Gaussians
\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
with weights
w_{i}\geqslant\alpha
for every
i\in[k]
.
Then, for all
t\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{D}
has
t
-certifiably
4/\alpha
-hypercontractive degree-
2
polynomials.

We will use the following robust mean estimation algorithm for bounded covariance distributions
[
DKK+17
]
:

Fact 2.36

(Robust Mean Estimation for Bounded Covariance Distributions)
.

There is a
\poly(n)
time algorithm that takes input an
\varepsilon
-corruption
Y
of a collection
of
n
points
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and outputs an estimate
\hat{\mu}
satisfying

\left\lVert\E_{x\sim_{u}X}x-\hat{\mu}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant O(\sqrt{\varepsilon})\left\lVert\E_{x\sim_{u}X}(x-\E_{x\sim_{u}X}x)(x-\E_{x\sim_{u}X}x)^{\top}\right\rVert_{2}
.

We will use the following robust covariance estimation algorithm from
[
KS17b
]
:

Fact 2.37

(Robust Covariance Estimation,
[
KS17b
]
)
.

For every
C>0,\varepsilon>0
and even
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
Ck\varepsilon^{1-2/k}\leqslant c
for some small enough absolute constant
c
, there exists a polynomial-time algorithm that given an (corrupted) sample
S
outputs an estimate of the covariance
\hat{\Sigma}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
with the following guarantee: there
exists
n_{0}\geqslant(C+d)^{O(k)}/\varepsilon
such that if
S
is an
\varepsilon
-corrupted sample with size
|S|\geqslant n_{0}
of a
k
-certifiably
C
-subgaussian distribution
D
over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with mean
\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

and covariance
\Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, then with high probability:

(1-\delta)\Sigma\succeq\hat{\Sigma}\succeq(1+\delta)\Sigma

for
\delta\leqslant O(Ck)\varepsilon^{1-2/k}
.

We will also require the following robust estimation algorithm with Frobenius distance guarantees proven
for certifiably hypercontractive distributions in
[
BK20b
]
. Since we obtain estimates to the true covariance in Lowner ordering, we can obtain the subspace spanned by the inliers exactly, project on to this subspace and apply Theorem 7.1 in
[
BK20b
]
.

Fact 2.38

(Robust Mean and Covariance Estimation for Certifiably Hypercontractive Distributions,
Theorem 7.1 in
[
BK20b
]
)
.

Given
t\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small so that
Ct\varepsilon^{1-4/t}\ll 1
6
6

6

This notation means that we needed
Ct\varepsilon^{1-2/t}
to be at most
c_{0}
for some absolute constant
c_{0}>0
.
, for some absolute constant
C>0
.
Then, there is an algorithm that takes input
Y
, an
\varepsilon
-corruption of a sample
X
of size
n

with mean
\mu_{*}
, covariance
\Sigma_{*}
, and
2t
-certifiably
C
-hypercontractive degree-
2

polynomials, runs in time
n^{O(t)}
, and outputs an estimate
\hat{\mu}
and
\hat{\Sigma}
satisfying:

1.

\left\lVert\Sigma^{\dagger/2}_{*}(\mu_{*}-\hat{\mu})\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant O(Ct)^{1/2}\varepsilon^{1-1/t}
,

2.

(1-\eta)\Sigma_{*}\preceq\hat{\Sigma}\preceq(1+\eta)\Sigma_{*}
for
\eta\leqslant O(Ck)\varepsilon^{1-2/t}
, and,

3.

\left\lVert\Sigma_{*}^{\dagger/2}\left(\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma_{*}\right)\Sigma_{*}^{\dagger/2}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant(C^{\prime}t)O(\varepsilon^{1-1/t})
,

where
C^{\prime}=\max\left\{C,B\right\}
for the smallest possible
B>0
such that for
d\times d
-matrix-valued indeterminate
Q
,
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\left\{\E_{\mathcal{D}}\left(x^{\top}Qx-\E_{\mathcal{D}}x^{\top}Qx\right)^{2}\leqslant B\left\lVert\Sigma_{*}^{1/2}Q\Sigma_{*}^{1/2}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\right\}
.
7
7

7

The first two guarantees here hold for the larger class of certifiably subgaussian distributions and were proven in
[
KS17b
]
(see Theorem 1.2). Gaussian distribution (with arbitrary mean and covariance) are
t
-certifiably
1
-subgaussian for all
t
and their mixtures (similar to Lemma
2.34
and explicitly proven in Lemma 5.4 of
[
KS17b
]
) are
t
-certifiably
O(1/\alpha)
-subgaussian where
\alpha
is the minimum mixing weight.

The last line in the above fact asserts a bound (along with a degree
2
SoS proof) on the variance of degree
2
polynomials in terms of the Frobenius norm of its coefficient matrix. In the next few claims, we verify this property via elementary arguments for the two classes of distributions relevant to this paper. We note that whenever a distribution satisfies the bounded variance property (without an SoS proof), it also satisfies the property via a degree
2
SoS proof using Lemma
2.23
. Thus, asking for an SoS proof of degree
2
in this context poses no additional restrictions on the distribution. Nevertheless, we provide explicit and direct SoS proofs in the following.

We first note that this property of having
certifiable bounded variance
is closed under linear transformations.

Lemma 2.39

(Linear Transformations of Certifiably Bounded-Variance Distributions)
.

For
d\in\mathbb{N}
, let
x
be a random variable with distribution
\mathcal{D}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that for
d\times d
matrix-valued indeterminate
Q
,
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\left\{\E_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}(x^{\top}Qx-\E_{\mathcal{D}}x^{\top}Qx)^{2}\leqslant\left\lVert\Sigma^{1/2}Q\Sigma^{1/2}\right\rVert^{2}_{F}\right\}
. Let
A
be an arbitrary
d\times d
matrix and let
x^{\prime}=Ax
be the random variable with covariance
\Sigma^{\prime}=AA^{\top}
. Then, we have that

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\left\{\E_{x^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}({x^{\prime}}^{\top}Qx^{\prime}-\E_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}{x^{\prime}}^{\top}Qx^{\prime})^{2}\leqslant\left\lVert{\Sigma^{\prime}}^{1/2}Q{\Sigma^{\prime}}^{1/2}\right\rVert^{2}_{F}\right\}\,.

Lemma 2.40

(Variance of Degree-
2
Polynomials of Standard Gaussians)
.

We have that

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\left\{\E_{\mathcal{N}(0,I)}\left(x^{\top}Qx-\E_{\mathcal{N}(0,I)}x^{\top}Qx\right)^{2}\leqslant 3\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\right\}\,.

Remark 2.41
.

As is easy to verify, the same proof more generally holds for any distribution that has the same first four
moments as the zero-mean Gaussian distribution.

As an immediate corollary of the previous two lemmas, we have:

Corollary 2.42

(Variance of Degree-
2
Polynomials of Zero-Mean, Arbitrary Covariance Gaussians)
.

For any
0\preceq\Sigma
, we have that

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\left\{\E_{\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)}\left(x^{\top}Qx-\E_{\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)}x^{\top}Qx\right)^{2}\leqslant 3\left\lVert\Sigma^{1/2}Q\Sigma^{1/2}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\right\}\,.

We next prove that the same property holds for mixtures of Gaussians satisfying certain conditions.

Lemma 2.43

(Variance of Degree-
2
Polynomials of Mixtures)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathcal{D}_{i}
be a
k
-mixture of distributions
\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{k}
with means
\mu_{i}
and covariances
\Sigma_{i}
. Let
\mu=\sum_{i}w_{i}\mu_{i}
be the mean of
\mathcal{M}
. Suppose that each of
\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{k}
have certifiably
C
-bounded-variance i.e. for
Q
: a symmetric
d\times d
matrix-valued indeterminate.

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\left\{\E_{x^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{D}_{i}}({x^{\prime}}^{\top}Qx^{\prime}-\E_{\mathcal{D}_{i}}{x^{\prime}}^{\top}Qx^{\prime})^{2}\leqslant C\left\lVert{\Sigma^{\prime}}^{1/2}Q{\Sigma^{\prime}}^{1/2}\right\rVert^{2}_{F}\right\}\,.

Further, suppose that for some
H>1
,
\left\|\mu_{i}-\mu\right\|_{2}^{2},\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-I\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant H
for every
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
.
Then, we have that

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\left\{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{M}}\left[\left(x^{\top}Qx-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{M}}\left[x^{\top}Qx\right]\right)^{2}\right]\leqslant 100CH^{2}\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\right\}\,.

As an immediate corollary of Lemma
2.39
and Lemma
2.43
,
we obtain:

Lemma 2.44

(Variance of Degree-
2
Polynomials of Mixtures of Gaussians)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
be a
k
-mixture of Gaussians with
w_{i}\geqslant\alpha
,
mean
\mu=\sum_{i}w_{i}\mu_{i}
and covariance
\Sigma=\sum_{i}w_{i}((\mu_{i}-\mu)(\mu_{i}-\mu)^{\top}+\Sigma_{i})
.
Suppose that for some
H>1
,
\left\lVert\Sigma^{\dagger/2}(\Sigma_{i}-\Sigma)\Sigma^{\dagger/2}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant H
for every
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
.
Let
Q
be a symmetric
d\times d
matrix-valued indeterminate. Then for
H^{\prime}=\max\{H,1/\alpha\}
,

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\left\{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{M}}\left[\left(x^{\top}Qx-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{M}}\left[x^{\top}Qx\right]\right)^{2}\right]\leqslant 100{H^{\prime}}^{2}\left\lVert\Sigma^{1/2}Q\Sigma^{1/2}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\right\}\,.

Analytic Properties are Inherited by Samples.

The following lemma can be proven via similar, standard techniques as in several prior works
[
KS17b
,
KKK19
,
BK20a
,
BK20b
]
.

Fact 2.45
.

Let
D
be a distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with mean
\mu
and covariance
\Sigma
. Let
t\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
X
be a sample from
D
such that,
\left\lVert\frac{1}{|X|}\sum_{x\in X}(1,\bar{x})^{\otimes t}-\E_{x\sim D}(1,\bar{x})^{\otimes t}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant d^{-O(t)}
. Here,
\bar{x}=\Sigma^{\dagger/2}(x-\mu_{i})
. Then,

1.

If
D
is
2t
-certifiably
C
-subgaussian, then the uniform distribution on
X
is
t
-certifiably
2C
-subgaussian.

2.

If
D
has
2t
-certifiably
C
-hypercontractive degree
2
polynomials, then the uniform distribution on
X
has
t
-certifiably
2C
-hypercontractive degree
2
polynomials.

3.

If
D
is
2t
-certifiably
C\delta
-anti-concentrated, then the uniform distribution on
X
is
t
-certifiably
2C\delta
-anti-concentrated.

4.

If
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\left\{\E_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}(x^{\top}Qx-\E_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}x^{\top}Qx)^{2}\leqslant C\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\right\}
, then, for the uniform distribution
\mathcal{D}_{X}
on
X
,
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\left\{\E_{x\sim\mathcal{D}_{X}}(x^{\top}Qx-\E_{x\sim\mathcal{D}_{X}}x^{\top}Qx)^{2}\leqslant 2C\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\right\}.

2.4
Deterministic Conditions on the Uncorrupted Samples

In this section, we describe the set of deterministic conditions on the set of uncorrupted samples,
under which our algorithms succeed. We will require the following definition.

Definition 2.46
.

Fix
0<\varepsilon<1/2
.
We say that a multiset
Y
of points in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is an
\varepsilon
-corrupted version (or an
\varepsilon
-corruption)
of a multiset
X
of points in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
if
|X\cap Y|\geqslant\max\{(1-\varepsilon)|X|,(1-\varepsilon)|Y|\}
.

Throughout this paper and unless otherwise specified, we will use
X
to denote a multiset of i.i.d.
samples from the target
k
-mixture
{\cal M}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}G_{i}
, where
G_{i}=\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
.
We will use
X_{i}
for the subset of points in
X
drawn from
G_{i}
, i.e.,
X=\cup_{i=1}^{k}X_{i}
.

We will use
Y
to denote an
\varepsilon
-corrupted version of
X
, as per Definition
2.46
.
In this
strong contamination model
, the adversary can see the clean samples from
X
before
they decide on the
\varepsilon
-corruption
Y
.
The strong contamination model is known to subsume the total variation contamination of
Definition
1.2
(see, e.g., Section 2 of
[
DKK+16
]
). We note that our robust learning
algorithm succeeds in this stronger contamination model, with the additional requirement that we can obtain two sets
of independent
\varepsilon
-corrupted samples from
{\cal M}
. (The second set is needed to run a hypothesis testing
routine after we obtain a small list of candidate hypotheses.)

Our algorithm works for any finite set of points in
\mathbb{R}^{d}

that satisfies a natural set of deterministic conditions.
As we will show later in this section, these deterministic conditions are
satisfied with high probability by a sufficiently large set of i.i.d. samples from any

k
-mixture of Gaussians.

Condition 2.47

(Good Samples)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
be a
k
-mixture of Gaussians in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Let
X
be a set of
n
points in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
We say that
X
satisfies Condition
2.47

with respect to
\mathcal{M}
with parameters
(\gamma,t)
if there is a partition of
X

as
X_{1}\cup X_{2}\cup\ldots\cup X_{k}
such that:

1.

For all
i\in[k]
with
w_{i}\geqslant\gamma
, any positive integer
m\leqslant t
, and any
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{x\in X_{i}}\langle v,x-\mu_{i}\rangle^{m}-w_{i}\E_{x\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})}[\langle v,x-\mu_{i}\rangle^{m}]\right|\leqslant w_{i}\,\gamma\,m!\,(v^{T}\Sigma_{i}v)^{m/2}\;.

2.

For all
i\in[k]
and any halfspace
H\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have that

\left||X_{i}\cap H|/n-w_{i}\ProbOp_{x\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})}[x\in H]\right|\leqslant\gamma.

We will also need the following consequences
of Condition
2.47
.
The first one is immediate.

Lemma 2.48
.

Condition
2.47
is invariant under affine transformations.
In particular, if
A(x):\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}}
is an affine transformation, and if
X
satisfies Condition
2.47

with respect to
\mathcal{M}
with parameters
(\gamma,t)
, then
A(X)
satisfies
Condition
2.47
with respect to
A(\mathcal{M})
with parameters
(\gamma,t)
.

We note that the first part of Condition
2.47

implies that higher moment tensors are close in Frobenius distance.

Lemma 2.49
.

If
X
satisfies Condition
2.47
with respect to
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})

with parameters
(\gamma,t)
, then if
w_{i}\geqslant\gamma
for all
i\in[k]
,
and if for some
B\geqslant 0
we have that
\left\lVert\mu_{i}\right\rVert_{2}^{2},\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant B
for all
i\in[k]
,
then for all
m\leqslant t
, we have that:

\left\lVert\E_{x\in_{u}X}[x^{\otimes m}]-\E_{x\sim M}[x^{\otimes m}]\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leqslant\gamma^{2}m^{O(m)}B^{m}d^{m}\;.

We note that Condition
2.47
also behaves
well with respect to taking submixtures.

Lemma 2.50
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
. Let
S\subset[k]
with
\sum_{i\in S}w_{i}=w
, and let

\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\sum_{i\in S}(w_{i}/w)\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
. Then if
X
satisfies Condition
2.47
with respect to
\mathcal{M}
with parameters
(\gamma,t)
for some
\gamma<1/(2k)

with the corresponding partition being
X=X_{1}\cup X_{2}\cup\ldots\cup X_{k}
,
then
X^{\prime}=\bigcup_{i\in S}X_{i}
satisfies Condition
2.47

with respect to
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
with parameters
(O(k\gamma/w),t)
.

Finally, we show that given sufficiently many i.i.d. samples from a
k
-mixture of Gaussians,
Condition
2.47
holds with high probability.

Lemma 2.51
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
and let
n
be an integer at least
kt^{Ct}d^{t}/\gamma^{3}
,
for a sufficiently large universal constant
C>0
, some
\gamma>0
, and some
t\in\mathbb{N}
.
If
X
consists of
n
i.i.d. samples from
\mathcal{M}
,
then
X
satisfies Condition
2.47

with respect to
\mathcal{M}
with parameters
(\gamma,t)
with high probability.

The proofs of the preceding lemmas can be found in Appendix
A
.

2.5
Hypothesis Selection

Our algorithm will require a procedure to select a hypothesis from a list of candidates
that contains an accurate hypothesis. A number of such procedures are known
in the literature (see, e.g.,
[
Yat85
,
DL01
,
DDS12
,
DK14
,
DDS15
,
DKK+16
]
).
Here we will use the following variant from
[
Kan20
]
, showing that we can efficiently
perform a hypothesis selection (tournament) step with access to
\varepsilon
-corrupted samples.

Fact 2.52

(Robust Tournament,
[
Kan20
]
)
.

Let
X
be an unknown distribution,
\eta\in(0,1)
,
and let
H_{1},\dots,H_{n}
be distributions with explicitly computable probability density functions
that can be efficiently sampled from. Assume furthermore than
\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}(d_{\mathsf{TV}}(X,H_{i}))\leqslant\eta
.
Then there exists an efficient algorithm that given access to
O(\log(n)/\eta^{2})

\varepsilon
-corrupted samples from
X
,
where
\varepsilon\leqslant\eta
, along with
H_{1},\dots,H_{n}
, computes an
m\in[n]
such that with high probability we have that

d_{\mathsf{TV}}(X,H_{m})=O(\eta)\;.

3
List-Recovery of Parameters via Tensor Decomposition

In this section, we give an algorithm that takes samples from a
k
-mixture of Gaussians,
whose component means and covariances are not too far from each other
in natural norms, and outputs a dimension-independent size list of candidate

k
-tuples of parameters (i.e., means and covariances) one of which is
guaranteed to be close to the true target
k
-tuple of parameters. Our approach involves a new
tensor decomposition procedure that works in the absence of any non-degeneracy
conditions on the components.

The goal of this section is to prove the following theorem:

Theorem 3.1

(Recovering Candidate Parameters when Component Covariances
are close in Frobenius Distance)
.

Fix any
\alpha>\varepsilon>0,\Delta>0
.
There is an algorithm that takes input
X
, a sample from a
k
-mixture of Gaussians

\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
satisfying Condition
2.47

with parameters
\gamma=\varepsilon d^{-8k}k^{-Ck}
, for
C
a sufficiently large universal constant,
and
t=8k
, and let
Y
be an
\varepsilon
-corruption of
X
.
If
w_{i}\geqslant\alpha
,
\left\lVert\mu_{i}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\frac{2}{\sqrt{\alpha}}
and
\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-I\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\Delta
for every
i\in[k]
,
then, given
k,Y
and
\varepsilon
, the algorithm outputs a list
L
of at most

{\exp\left(\log(1/\varepsilon)\left(k+1/\alpha+\Delta\right)^{O(k)}/\eta^{2}\right)}

candidate hypotheses (component means and covariances),
such that with probability at least
0.99
there exist
\{\hat{\mu}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}\subseteq L
satisfying

\left\lVert\mu_{i}-\hat{\mu}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\frac{\Delta^{1/2}}{\alpha}\right)\eta^{G(k)}
and

\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-\hat{\Sigma}_{i}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(k^{4}\right)\frac{\Delta^{1/2}}{\alpha}\eta^{G(k)}
for all
i\in[k]
.
Here,
\eta=(2k)^{4k}\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(1/\alpha+\Delta\right)^{4k}\sqrt{\varepsilon}
,
G(k)=\frac{1}{C^{k+1}(k+1)!}
.
The running time of the algorithm is
\poly(|L|,{|Y|,d^{k}})
.

In the body of this section, we establish Theorem
3.1
.
The structure of this section is as follows:
In Section
3.1
, we describe our algorithm, which is then analyzed
in Sections
3.2
-
3.6
.

3.1
List-Decodable Tensor Decomposition Algorithm

In this section, we describe our tensor decomposition algorithm,
which is given in pseudocode below (Algorithm
3.2
).

Algorithm 3.2

(List-Recovery of Candidate Parameters via Tensor Decomposition)
.

Input:

An
\varepsilon
-corruption
Y
of a sample
X
from a
k
-mixture of
Gaussians
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
.

Requirements:

The guarantees of the algorithm hold if the mixture parameters
and the sample
X
satisfy:

1.

w_{i}\geqslant\alpha
for all
i\in[k]
,

2.

\left\lVert\mu_{i}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant 2/\sqrt{\alpha}
for all
i\in[k]
,

3.

\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-I\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\Delta
for all
i\in[k]
.

4.

X
satisfies Condition
2.47
with parameters
(\gamma,t)
,
where
\gamma=\varepsilon d^{-8k}k^{-Ck}
, for
C
a sufficiently large universal constant, and
t=8k
.

Parameters:

\eta=(2k)^{4k}(Ck(1/\alpha+\Delta))^{4k}\sqrt{\varepsilon}
,
D=C(k^{4}/(\alpha\sqrt{\eta}))
,

\delta=2\eta^{1/(C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}
,
\ell^{\prime}=100\log k\left(\eta/\left(k^{5}\left(\Delta^{4}+1/\alpha^{4}\right)\right)\right)^{-4k}
,
for some sufficiently large absolute constant
C>0
,
\lambda=4\eta
,
\phi=10(1+\Delta^{2})/(\sqrt{\eta}\alpha^{5})
.

Output:

A list
L
of hypotheses such that there exists at least one,
\{\hat{\mu}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}_{i}\}_{i\leqslant k}\in L
, satisfying:
\left\lVert\mu_{i}-\hat{\mu}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\frac{\Delta^{1/2}}{\alpha}\right)\eta^{G(k)}
and

\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-\hat{\Sigma}_{i}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(k^{4}\right)\frac{\Delta^{1/2}}{\alpha}\eta^{G(k)}
, where
G(k)=\frac{1}{C^{k+1}(k+1)!}
.

Operation:

1.

Robust Estimation of Hermite Tensors
: For
m\in[4k]
, compute
\hat{T}_{m}
such that
\max_{m\in[4k]}\left\lVert\hat{T}_{m}-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[h_{m}(\mathcal{M})\right]\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\eta
using the robust mean estimation algorithm in Fact
2.36
.

2.

Random Collapsing of Two Modes of
\hat{T}_{4}
: Let
L^{\prime}
be an empty list. Repeat
\ell^{\prime}
times:
For
j\in[4k]
, choose independent standard Gaussians in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, denoted by
x^{(j)},y^{(j)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)
,
and uniform draws
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{t}
from
[-D,D]
. Let
\hat{S}
be a
d\times d
matrix such that
for all
r,s\in[d]
,
\hat{S}(r,s)=\sum_{j\in[4k]}a_{j}\hat{T}_{4}(r,s,x^{(j)},y^{(j)})=\sum_{j\in[4k]}a_{j}\sum_{g,h\in[d]}\hat{T}_{4}(r,s,g,h)x^{(j)}(g)y^{(j)}(h)
.
Add
\hat{S}
to the list
L^{\prime}
.

3.

Construct Low-Dimensional Subspace for Exhaustive Search
: Let
V
be the span of all singular vectors of the natural
d\times d^{m-1}
flattening of
\hat{T}_{m}
with singular values
\geqslant\lambda
for
m\leqslant 4k
. For each
\hat{S}\in L^{\prime}
, let
V^{\prime}_{\hat{S}}
be the span of
V
plus all the singular vectors of
\hat{S}
with singular value larger than
\delta^{1/4}
.

4.

Enumerating Candidates in
V^{\prime}_{\hat{S}}
: Initialize
L
to be the empty list.
For each
\hat{S}\in L^{\prime}
, let
V_{\delta^{1/4}}
be a
\delta^{1/4}
-cover of vectors in
V^{\prime}_{\hat{S}}

with
\ell_{2}
-norm at most
2/\sqrt{\alpha}
. Enumerate over vectors
\hat{\mu}
in
V_{\delta^{1/4}}
.
Let
k^{\prime}=Ck^{2}

and let
\mathcal{C}_{\delta^{1/4}}
be a
\delta^{1/4}
-cover of the interval
[-\phi,\phi]^{k^{\prime}}
.
For
\{\tau_{j}\}_{j\in[k^{\prime}]}\in\mathcal{C}_{\delta^{1/4}}
and for all
\{v_{j}\}_{j\in[k^{\prime}]}\in V_{\delta^{1/4}}
,
let
\hat{Q}=\sum_{j\in[k^{\prime}]}\tau_{j}v_{j}v_{j}^{\top}
. Add
\{\hat{\mu},I+\hat{S}+\hat{Q}\}
to
L
.

3.2
Analysis of Algorithm

We analyze the three main steps of Algorithm
3.2
in the following lemmas.
We will prove the following three propositions in the subsequent subsections that analyze Steps 1, 2 and 3
of Algorithm
3.2
. For Step 1, we show that when
X
satisfies Condition
2.47
, the empirical estimates of the moment tensors obtained
by applying the robust mean estimation algorithm to
X
are sufficiently close to the moment tensors
of the input mixture
\mathcal{M}
.

Proposition 3.3

(Robustly Estimating Hermite Polynomial Tensors)
.

For any integer
m\leqslant{4k}
, and
\Delta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, there exists an algorithm with running time
\poly_{m}(d/\varepsilon)

that takes an
\varepsilon
-corruption
Y
of
X
, a set satisfying Condition
2.47

with respect to
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
with parameters
\gamma=\varepsilon d^{-m}m^{-Cm}
,
for
C
a sufficiently large constant, and
t={2}m
. If
w_{i}\geqslant\alpha
,
\left\lVert\mu_{i}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant 2/\sqrt{\alpha}
, and
\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-I\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\Delta
for each
i\in[k]
, then the algorithm outputs a tensor
\hat{T}_{m}
such that

\left\lVert\hat{T}_{m}-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[h_{m}(\mathcal{M})\right]\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\eta
,
for
\eta=\mathcal{O}\left(m(1+1/\alpha+\Delta)\right)^{m}\sqrt{\varepsilon}
.

The proof of Proposition
3.3
is deferred to
Section
3.3
.

Next, we analyze Step 2 of the algorithm and prove that, with non-negligible probability,
randomly collapsing two modes of
\hat{T}_{4}
yields a matrix
\hat{S}
such that

\hat{S}-(\Sigma_{i}-I)=P_{i}+Q_{i}
, where
P_{i}
has small Frobenius norm
and
Q_{i}
is a rank-
\mathcal{O}(k^{2})
matrix.

Proposition 3.4

(Tensor Decomposition up to Low-Rank Error)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
be a
k
-mixture of Gaussians
satisfying
w_{i}\geqslant\alpha
,
\left\lVert\mu_{i}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant 2/\sqrt{\alpha}
, and
\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-I\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\Delta
for each
i\in[k]
.
For
0<\eta<1
, let
\hat{T}_{4}
be a tensor such that
\left\lVert\E[h_{4}(\mathcal{M})]-\hat{T}_{4}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\eta
,
and let
D
be a sufficiently large constant multiple of
{k^{4}/(\alpha\sqrt{\eta})}
.
For all
j\in[4k]
, let
x^{(j)},y^{(j)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)
be independent
and
a_{j}\sim\mathcal{U}[-D,D]
, where
\mathcal{U}[-D,D]
is the uniform distribution over the interval
[-D,D]
,
and let
\hat{S}=\sum_{j\in[4k]}a_{j}\hat{T}_{4}\left(\cdot,\cdot,x^{(j)},y^{(j)}\right)
.
Then, for each
i\in[k]
, with probability at least
\left(\eta/\left(k^{5}\left(\Delta^{4}+1/\alpha^{4}\right)\right)\right)^{4k}
,
over the choice of
x^{(j)},y^{(j)}
and
a_{j}
, we have that
\hat{S}-(\Sigma_{i}-I)=P_{i}+Q_{i}
,
where
\left\lVert P_{i}\right\rVert_{F}=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\sqrt{\eta/\alpha}\right)
,
\left\lVert Q_{i}\right\rVert_{F}=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\frac{1+\Delta^{2}}{\sqrt{\eta}\alpha^{3}}\right)

and
\mathrm{rank}(Q_{i})=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(k^{2}\right)
.

The proof of Proposition
3.4

is given in Section
3.4
.

Finally, in Step 3, for any
\hat{S}
such that
\hat{S}-(\Sigma_{i}-I)=P_{i}+Q_{i}
,
where
P_{i}
has small Frobenius norm and
Q_{i}
is a rank
\mathcal{O}(k^{2})
matrix,
we find a low-dimensional subspace
V^{\prime}
such that the range space of
Q_{i}
is approximately contained in
V^{\prime}
.
We will use
V^{\prime}
to exhaustively search for
\mathcal{O}(k^{2})
rank matrices to find candidates for
Q_{i}
.

Proposition 3.5

(Low-Dimensional Subspace
V^{\prime}
for Exhaustive Search)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
be a
k
-mixture of Gaussians
satisfying
w_{i}\geqslant\alpha
,
\left\lVert\mu_{i}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant 2/\sqrt{\alpha}
, and
\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-I\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\Delta
for each
i\in[k]
.
Let
\left\lVert\hat{T}_{m}-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[h_{m}(\mathcal{M})\right]\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\eta
, for each
1\leqslant m\leqslant 4k
, and some
\eta>0
.
Let
V
be the span of all the left singular vectors of the
d\times d^{m-1}
matrix
obtained by the natural flattening of
\hat{T}_{m}
with singular values at least
2\eta
.
For each
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
, let
S_{i}=\Sigma_{i}-I
and
\hat{S_{i}}
be a
d\times d
matrix such that
\hat{S}_{i}-S_{i}=P_{i}+Q_{i}
,
where
\left\lVert P_{i}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\sqrt{\eta/\alpha}\right)
,
Q_{i}
has rank
\mathcal{O}(k^{2})
,
and
\left\lVert Q_{i}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\frac{1+\Delta^{2}}{\sqrt{\eta}\alpha^{3}}\right)
.
Let
V^{\prime}
be the span of
V
plus all singular vectors of
\hat{S_{i}}
of singular values at least
\delta
for all
i
.
Then, for
\delta=2\eta^{1/(C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}
with a sufficiently large constant
C>0
, we have that:

1.

\mathrm{dim}(V^{\prime})\leqslant\left(\mathcal{O}\left(k(1+1/\alpha+\Delta)\right)^{4k+5}\right)/\eta^{2}
.

2.

There is a vector
\mu_{i}^{\prime}\in V^{\prime}
such that
\left\lVert\mu_{i}-\mu_{i}^{\prime}\right\rVert^{2}_{2}\leqslant\frac{20}{\alpha^{2}}\sqrt{\delta}\Delta
.

3.

There are
q=\mathcal{O}(k^{2})
unit vectors
v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{q}\in V^{\prime}
and scalars
\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\ldots,\tau_{q}\in\left[-10(1+\Delta^{2})/(\sqrt{\eta}\alpha^{5}),10(1+\Delta^{2})/(\sqrt{\eta}\alpha^{5})\right]

such that
\left\lVert Q_{i}-\sum_{i=1}^{q}\tau_{i}v_{i}v_{i}^{\top}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\frac{k^{2}}{\alpha}\delta^{1/4}\Delta^{1/2}\right)
.

The proof of Proposition
3.5
is given in
Section
3.5
.

We can now use these propositions to complete the proof of Theorem
3.1
.

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.

Using Proposition
3.3
, Step 1 of the algorithm outputs estimates
\hat{T}_{i}
for
i\in[4k]
such that
\max_{m\in[4k]}\left\lVert\hat{T}_{m}-\E h_{m}(\mathcal{M})\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\eta
. Next, by the standard coupon collector analysis, using Proposition
3.4

and repeating Step 2 of the algorithm
\ell^{\prime}=100\log k\left(\eta/\left(k^{5}\left(\Delta^{4}+1/\alpha^{4}\right)\right)\right)^{-4k}
times,
guarantees that with probability at least
1-1/(100k)^{100}
, for every
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
, there are
\hat{S}_{i}\in L
such that
\hat{S}_{i}-(\Sigma_{i}-I)=P_{i}+Q_{i}
for
P_{i},Q_{i}
satisfying
\left\lVert P_{i}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\sqrt{\eta/\alpha}
,
\left\lVert Q_{i}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\frac{1+\Delta^{2}}{\sqrt{\eta}\alpha^{5}}
and
Q_{i}
has rank
\mathcal{O}(k^{2})
.

Next, Proposition
3.5
implies that for every such
\hat{S}_{i}\in L^{\prime}
, we can construct a subspace
V^{\prime}=V^{\prime}_{\hat{S}_{i}}
of dimension
\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left((k(1+1/\alpha+\Delta))^{4k+5}/{\eta^{2}}\right)
such that
V^{\prime}
contains
\mu_{i}^{\prime}
that satisfies
\left\lVert\mu_{i}-\mu_{i}^{\prime}\right\rVert^{2}_{2}\leqslant\frac{\Delta}{\alpha^{2}}\cdot\sqrt{\delta}
, and there is a rank
O(k^{2})
matrix
\hat{Q}_{i}
with range space contained in
V^{\prime}
such that
\left\lVert Q_{i}-\hat{Q}_{i}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\mathcal{O}(\frac{k^{2}}{\alpha}\delta^{1/4}\Delta^{1/2})
.

Now, let
V_{\tau}\subseteq V^{\prime}
be a
\tau=\delta^{1/4}
-cover, in
\ell_{2}
-norm,
of vectors with
\ell_{2}
norm at most
2/\sqrt{\alpha}
in
V^{\prime}
.
Then, since
\left\lVert\mu_{i}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\frac{2}{\sqrt{\alpha}}
, there is a vector
\hat{\mu}_{i}\in V_{\tau}

such that
\left\lVert\mu_{i}-\hat{\mu}_{i}\right\rVert^{2}_{2}\leqslant\tau+\frac{20}{\alpha^{2}}\sqrt{\delta}\Delta\leqslant\frac{40}{\alpha^{2}}\sqrt{\delta}\Delta
.

Further, there exist
\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\ldots\tau_{\mathcal{O}(k^{2})}
in a
\tau
-cover of

[-10(1+\Delta^{2})/(\sqrt{\eta}\alpha^{5}),10(1+\Delta^{2})/(\sqrt{\eta}\alpha^{5})]

and vectors
v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{\mathcal{O}(k^{2})}\in V_{\tau}
such that

\left\lVert\sum_{i=1}^{\mathcal{O}(k^{2})}\tau_{i}v_{i}v_{i}^{\top}-Q_{i}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\mathcal{O}(k^{4}\delta^{1/4}\Delta^{1/2}/\alpha)
.
In particular,
\hat{\Sigma}_{i}=I+\hat{S}-\sum_{i=1}^{\mathcal{O}(k^{2})}\tau_{i}v_{i}v_{i}^{\top}

satisfies

\left\|\hat{\Sigma}_{i}-\Sigma_{i}\right\|_{F}=\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\eta})+\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\frac{k^{4}\delta^{1/4}\Delta^{1/2}}{\alpha}\right)=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\frac{k^{4}\delta^{1/4}\Delta^{1/2}}{\alpha}\right)\;.

(3.1)

The size of this search space for every fixed
\hat{S}\in L^{\prime}
can be bounded above
by
\left(\frac{1+\Delta^{2}}{\delta\alpha^{5}}\right)^{\mathcal{O}(k^{5}\mathrm{dim}(V^{\prime}))}
.
Thus, the size of
L
can be bounded from above by

\begin{split}k^{5}\left(\frac{\Delta^{4}}{\eta}+\frac{1}{\alpha^{4}\eta}\right)^{4k}\cdot\left(\frac{1+\Delta^{2}}{\delta\sqrt{\eta}\alpha^{5}}\right)^{\mathcal{O}(k^{5}\mathrm{dim}(V^{\prime}))}&\leqslant\exp\left(\log(1/\varepsilon)\left(k+1/\alpha+\Delta\right)^{O(k)}/\eta^{2}\right)\;.\end{split}

This completes the proof.
∎

3.3
Robust Estimation of Hermite Tensors

In this section, we will prove Proposition
3.3
.

Proof of Proposition
3.3
.

Consider the uniform distribution on the uncorrupted sample
X
.
We want to analyze the effect of applying the robust mean estimation algorithm (Fact
2.36
)
to the points
h_{m}(x)
, for
x\in X
. In order for us to apply Fact
2.36
,
we need to ensure that the uniform distribution on
\{h_{m}(x)\}_{x\in X}
has bounded covariance.
This step gives us a good approximation
to
\E_{x\sim_{u}X}h_{m}(x)
. In order for us to obtain an approximation to
\E h_{m}(\mathcal{M})
, we need to bound
the difference between
\E h_{m}(\mathcal{M})
and
\E_{x\sim_{u}X}h_{m}(x)
. We will do both these steps below.

The second part is immediate.
By the definition of
h_{m}(X)
, we have that

\left\lVert\frac{1}{|X|}\sum_{x\in X}h_{m}(x)-\E h_{m}(\mathcal{M})\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\sum_{j\leqslant m/2}m^{2j}d^{j}\left\lVert\frac{1}{|X|}\sum_{x\in X}x^{\otimes(m-2j)}-\E\mathcal{M}^{\otimes(m-2j)}\right\rVert_{F}.

By Lemma
2.49
, this is at most
O(1+\Delta+1/\alpha)^{m}m^{O(m)}d^{m/2}\gamma\leqslant\eta/2
.
We note that a similar argument bounds

\left\lVert\frac{1}{|X|}\sum_{x\in X}h_{m}(x){\otimes h_{m}(x)}-\E h_{m}(\mathcal{M}){\otimes h_{m}(\mathcal{M})}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\eta^{2}.

Let us now verify the first part. We proceed via bounding the operator norm of the covariance of
h_{m}(\mathcal{M})
.
We can then use the bound on the Frobenius norm

\left\lVert\frac{1}{|X|}\sum_{x\in X}h_{m}(x){\otimes h_{m}(X)}-\E h_{m}(\mathcal{M}){\otimes h_{m}(\mathcal{M})}\right\rVert_{F}

to get a bound on
\left\lVert\frac{1}{|X|}\sum_{x\in X}h_{m}(x)h_{m}(x)^{\top}\right\rVert_{{\mathrm{op}}}

(the operator norm of the canonical square flattening of the of the
2m
-th empirical Hermite moment tensor of
X
).
This will complete the proof.

Let
G_{i}=\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
be the components of
\mathcal{M}
.
We have that

\begin{split}\texttt{Cov}(h_{m}(\mathcal{M}))=&\sum_{i\in[k]}w_{i}\texttt{Cov}(h_{m}(G_{i}))\\
&+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j\in[k]}w_{i}w_{j}\left(\E[h_{m}(G_{i})]-\E[h_{m}(G_{j})]\right)\left(\E[h_{m}(G_{i})]-\E[h_{m}(G_{j})]\right)^{\top}\;.\end{split}

(3.2)

By Lemma
2.8
, we have that for all
i\in[k]
, it holds

\left\lVert\texttt{Cov}(h_{m}({G}_{i}))\right\rVert_{\textrm{op}}=\mathcal{O}\left(m(1+\left\|\mu_{i}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\Sigma_{i}-I\right\|_{F})\right)^{2m}=\mathcal{O}\left(m(1+2/\sqrt{\alpha}+\Delta)\right)^{2m}\;,

where for any matrix
M
,
\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{\textrm{op}}=\max_{\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{2}=1}\left\lVert Mu\right\rVert_{2}
is the operator norm of the matrix.
Further, for any
i,j\in[k]
,

\begin{split}\left\lVert\left(\E[h_{m}(G_{i})]-\E[h_{m}(G_{j})]\right)\left(\E[h_{m}(G_{i})]-\E[h_{m}(G_{j})]\right)^{\top}\right\rVert_{\textrm{op}}&=\left\lVert\E[h_{m}(G_{i})]-\E[h_{m}(G_{j})]\right\rVert_{2}\\
&=\mathcal{O}\left(m(1+1/\alpha+\Delta)\right)^{2m}\;.\end{split}

(3.3)

This claim follows from the triangle inequality of the operator norm.
∎

3.4
List-Recovery of Covariances up to Low-Rank Error

In this section, we prove Proposition
3.4
.
We first set some useful notation. We will write
S_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathrm{\footnotesize def}}}}{{=}}\Sigma_{i}-I
throughout this section.
We will also use
S_{i}^{\prime}
to denote
S_{i}+\mu_{i}\otimes\mu_{i}
.

We first show that for every
i
, there exists a matrix
P
such that
\left(\sum_{i\in[k]}w_{i}S^{\prime}_{i}\otimes S^{\prime}_{i}\right)\left(\cdot,\cdot,P\right)
is close to
S^{\prime}_{i}
.

Lemma 3.6

(Existence of a
2
-Tensor)
.

Under the hypothesis of Proposition
3.4
, for each
i\in[k]
,
there exists a matrix
P
such that
\left\lVert P\right\rVert_{F}=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(1/(\sqrt{\eta}\alpha)\right)
and

\left\lVert T^{\prime}_{4}\left(\cdot,\cdot,P\right)-S_{i}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{F}=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\sqrt{\eta/\alpha}\right)
,
where
T^{\prime}_{4}=\left(\sum_{i\in[k]}w_{i}S^{\prime}_{i}\otimes S^{\prime}_{i}\right)
.

Note that throughout this section it will be useful to think of
T_{4}^{\prime}
as a
d^{2}\times d^{2}
matrix rather than as a tensor. In this case, we can think of
T_{4}^{\prime}
as
\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}(S^{\prime}_{i})(S^{\prime}_{i})^{T}.
From standard facts about positive semidefinite matrices it follows that
S_{i}^{\prime}
is in the image of
T_{4}^{\prime}
, and Lemma
3.6
is just a slightly robustified version of this (saying that we can find an approximate preimage that it not itself too large).

The proof of this Lemma
3.6
will involve linear programming duality with an infinite system of constraints. As the application of duality with infinitely many constraints has some technical issues, we state below an appropriate version of duality.

Fact 3.7

(Linear Programming Duality for Compact, Convex Constraint Sets)
.

Let
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
be a compact convex set. There exists an
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
so that
(x,1)\cdot z>0
for all
z\in K
if and only if there is no element
(0,0,\ldots,0,a)\in K
for any
a\leqslant 0
.

This fact can be proved by noting that if no such
a
exists, there must be a hyperplane separating
K
from the set of such points
(0,a)
. This separating hyperplane will be of the form
(z,y)\in H
if and only if
y=x\cdot z
for some
x
and this
x
will provide the solution to the linear system.

Proof of Lemma
3.6
.

To show that such a
P
exists for each
i
, we apply linear programming duality.
In particular, the conditions imposed on
P
define a linear program, which has a feasible
solution unless there is a solution to the dual linear program. For sufficiently large constants
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
, consider the following primal in the variable
P
:

\displaystyle\left\langle v,P\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{c_{1}}{\sqrt{\eta}\alpha}\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{F}

\displaystyle\forall\hskip 7.22743ptv\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}

(3.4)

\displaystyle\left\langle u,T^{\prime}_{4}\left(\cdot,\cdot,P\right)-S^{\prime}_{i}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leqslant c_{2}\sqrt{\eta}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{F}

\displaystyle\forall\hskip 7.22743ptu\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}.

(3.5)

It is not hard to see that
\left\lVert P\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\frac{c_{1}}{\sqrt{\eta}\alpha}
if and only if (
3.4
) holds for all
v
and
\left\lVert T^{\prime}_{4}\left(\cdot,\cdot,P\right)-S_{i}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant c_{2}{\sqrt{\eta/\alpha}}
if and only if (
3.5
) holds for all
u
. Throughout the proof, we suggest that the reader think of
u
and
v
as vectors in
d^{2}
-dimensional vector space.

Our goal is to show that there exists a feasible solution
P
such that (
3.4
) and (
3.5
) hold simultaneously for all
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
. We first note that this is equivalent to saying that

\displaystyle\left\langle v,P\right\rangle+\left\langle u,T^{\prime}_{4}\left(\cdot,\cdot,P\right)\right\rangle-\left\langle u,S^{\prime}_{i}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{c_{1}}{\sqrt{\eta}\alpha}\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{F}+c_{2}\sqrt{\eta}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{F}\;,

(3.6)

for all
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
. This is not quite in the form necessary to apply Fact
3.7
, so we note that this is in turn equivalent to saying that

\displaystyle\left\langle v,P\right\rangle+\left\langle u,T^{\prime}_{4}\left(\cdot,\cdot,P\right)\right\rangle-\left\langle u,S^{\prime}_{i}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leqslant 1\;,

(3.7)

for all
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
so that
\frac{c_{1}}{\sqrt{\eta}\alpha}\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{F}+c_{2}\sqrt{\eta}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant 1
, and
u\in\mathrm{span}\{S_{i}^{\prime}\}
. As this is a convex set of linear equations, we have by Fact
3.7
that there exists such a
P
unless there exists such a pair of
u
and
v
so that the coefficient of
P
in Equation (
3.7
) is
0
and so that the resulting inequality of constants is either false or holds with equality. In particular, the coefficient of
P
vanishes if and only if
v=-T^{\prime}_{4}\left(u,\cdot,\cdot\right)
. We then get a contradiction only if for some
u\in\mathrm{span}\{S_{i}^{\prime}\}

\displaystyle-\left\langle u,S^{\prime}_{i}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\geqslant 1\geqslant\frac{c_{1}}{\sqrt{\eta}\alpha}\left\lVert T_{4}^{\prime}\left(u,\cdot,\cdot\right)\right\rVert_{F}+c_{2}\sqrt{\eta}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{F}.

(3.8)

We claim that this is impossible.

In particular, squaring Equation (
3.8
) would give

\begin{split}\left\langle u,S^{\prime}_{i}\right\rangle^{2}&\geqslant\left(\frac{c_{1}}{\sqrt{\eta}\alpha}\left\lVert T^{\prime}_{4}\left(u,\cdot,\cdot\right)\right\rVert_{F}+c_{2}\sqrt{\eta}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{F}\right)^{2}\\
&\geqslant\frac{c}{\alpha}\left\lVert T^{\prime}_{4}\left(u,\cdot,\cdot\right)\right\rVert_{F}\cdot\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{F}\;,\end{split}

(3.9)

for some large enough constant
c>1
, where the last inequality follows from the AM-GM inequality.
However, using the dual characterization of the Frobenius norm, we have

\begin{split}\left\lVert T^{\prime}_{4}\left(u,\cdot,\cdot\right)\right\rVert_{F}&\geqslant\frac{\left\langle u,T^{\prime}_{4}\left(u,\cdot,\cdot\right)\right\rangle}{\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{F}}\geqslant\frac{w_{i}}{\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{F}}\left\langle u,S^{\prime}_{i}\right\rangle^{2}\;,\end{split}

(3.10)

where the last inequality follows from
T^{\prime}_{4}
containing a
w_{i}S_{i}\otimes S_{i}
term, and the other terms contributing non-negatively.
Rearranging Equation (
3.10
), we have

\left\langle u,S^{\prime}_{i}\right\rangle^{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{w_{i}}\left\lVert T^{\prime}_{4}\left(u,\cdot,\cdot\right)\right\rVert_{F}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\frac{1}{\alpha}\left\lVert T^{\prime}_{4}\left(u,\cdot,\cdot\right)\right\rVert_{F}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{F}\;.

This contradicts Equation (
3.9
) unless
T_{4}^{\prime}\left(u,\cdot,\cdot\right)=0
. This therefore suffices to prove the feasibility of the primal.
∎

We have thus shown that there is some matrix
P
so that
T_{4}^{\prime}\left(P,\cdot,\cdot\right)
suffices for our purposes. We need to show that our appropriate random linear combination of
x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}
suffices. In fact, we will show that with reasonably high probability over our choice of
x^{(j)},y^{(j)}
that there is some linear combination of the
x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}
(with coefficients that are not too large) so that their projection onto the space spanned by the
S_{i}^{\prime}
(which is all that matters when applying
T_{4}^{\prime}
) equal to
P
.

For the sake of intuition, we note that if we removed the bound on the coefficients, we would need that the projections of the
x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}
spanned
\mathrm{span}\{S_{i}^{\prime}\}
. Since there are at least
k
of them, this will hold unless there is some
v\in\mathrm{span}\{S_{i}^{\prime}\}
so that
v
is orthogonal to all of the
x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}
. This shouldn’t happen because each
x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}
is very unlikely to be orthogonal to
v
.

To deal with the constraint that the coefficients are not too large, we use linear programming duality to show that there will be a solution unless there is some
v
that is
nearly
orthogonal to all of the
x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}
. Again, this is unlikely to happen for any individual term, and thus, by independence, highly unlikely to happen for all
j
simultaneously. Combining this with a cover argument will give our proof.

Lemma 3.8

(Existence of a Bi-Linear Form)
.

Given the preconditions in Proposition
3.4
,
with probability at least
99/100

over the choice of
x^{(j)},y^{(j)}
,
there exist
b_{j}\in[-D,D]
for
j\in[4k]
, where
D=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(k^{4}/(\sqrt{\eta}\alpha)\right)
,
such that the projection of
\sum_{j=1}^{t}b_{j}x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}
onto the space spanned
by the
S_{i}^{\prime}
is
P
, where
P
satisfies the conclusion of Proposition
3.6
.

Proof.

To prove this lemma, we again use a linear programming based argument.
Consider the following (primal) linear program in the variables
b_{j}
, for
j\in[4k]
:

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in[4k]}b_{j}\langle S^{\prime}_{i},x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle S^{\prime}_{i},P\rangle

\displaystyle\forall\hskip 7.22743pti\in[k]

(3.11)

\displaystyle-D\leqslant b_{j}

\displaystyle\leqslant D

\displaystyle\forall\hskip 7.22743ptj\in[4k]

(3.12)

We note that a set of
b_{j}
satisfying Equation (
3.11
) will have the projection of
\sum_{j\in[4k]}b_{j}x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}
onto the span of the
S_{i}^{\prime}
be the same as the projection of
P
, and that if the
b_{j}
’s satisfy Equation (
3.12
) then we will have
|b_{j}|\leqslant D
for all
j
. Thus, it suffices to show that with high probability over our choice of
x^{(j)}
and
y^{(j)}
that the above system is feasible.

We will show this by linear programming duality (since this is now a finite system of equations, we can use standard results rather than Fact
3.7
). In particular, we have that Equations (
3.11
) and (
3.12
) are simultaneously satisfiable unless there are real numbers
c_{i}
and non-negative real numbers
z_{j},z_{j}^{\prime}
so that

\sum_{i=1}^{k}c_{i}\sum_{j\in[4k]}b_{j}\langle S^{\prime}_{i},x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}\rangle+\sum_{j\in[4k]}(z_{j}-z_{j}^{\prime})b_{j}\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{k}c_{i}\langle S^{\prime}_{i},P\rangle+\sum_{j\in[4k]}(z_{j}+z_{j}^{\prime})D

yields a contradiction. Setting
v=\sum_{i=1}^{k}c_{i}S^{\prime}_{i}
, the above simplifies to

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in[4k]}b_{j}\left(\langle v,x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}\rangle+z_{j}-z_{j}^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\langle v,P\rangle+\sum_{j\in[4k]}(z_{j}+z_{j}^{\prime})D

(3.13)

We note that in order for Equation (
3.13
) to be a contradiction, it must be the case that the coefficients of
b_{j}
are all
0
. In particular, we must have

z_{j}^{\prime}-z_{j}=\langle v,x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}\rangle

for all
j
. In particular, this means that

z_{j}+z_{j}^{\prime}\geqslant\left|\langle v,x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}\rangle\right|.

In such a case, the right hand side of Equation (
3.13
) will be at least

\langle v,P\rangle+\sum_{j\in[4k]}\left|\langle v,x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}\rangle\right|D

Therefore, Equation (
3.13
) can only yield a contradiction if there exists a
v\in\mathrm{span}\{S^{\prime}_{i}\}
so that

\langle v,P\rangle<-\sum_{j\in[4k]}\left|\langle v,x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}\rangle\right|D.

(3.14)

We want to show that with high probability over our choice of
x^{(j)},y^{(j)}
that there is no
v\in\mathrm{span}\{S^{\prime}_{i}\}
satisfying Equation (
3.14
). In fact, we will show that for every such
v
that

\sum_{j\in[4k]}\left|\langle v,x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}\rangle\right|\geqslant\frac{c_{1}}{\sqrt{\eta}\alpha}\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{F}.

We can scale
v
so that
\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{F}=1
, and it suffices to show that

\sum_{j\in[4k]}\left|\left\langle\tilde{v},x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}\right\rangle\right|\geqslant\left(\frac{c_{1}}{\sqrt{\eta}\alpha D}\right)

(3.15)

holds for all unit vectors
v
in
\mathrm{span}\{S_{i}^{\prime}\}
with high probability.

Since we need to show that infinitely many equations all hold with high probability, we will use a cover argument. In particular, we can construct
\mathcal{C}
, a
\tau
-cover for all unit vectors
v
in the span of the
S_{i}^{\prime}
, where we take
\tau=\left(\frac{c^{\prime}_{1}}{k^{2}\sqrt{\eta}\alpha D}\right)
. Since this is a cover of a unit sphere in a
k
-dimensional subspace, we can construct such a cover so that

\left|\mathcal{C}\right|=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(1/\tau\right)^{k}
. Replacing
v
with the closest point in
\mathcal{C}
,
denoted by
v^{\prime}
, it suffices to show that with high probability for all
v
that

\sum_{j\in[4k]}\left|\left\langle{v},x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}\right\rangle\right|\geqslant\sum_{j\in[4k]}\left|\left\langle{v^{\prime}},x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}\right\rangle\right|-\sum_{j\in[4k]}\left|\left\langle{v}-{v}^{\prime},x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}\right\rangle\right|\geqslant\left(\frac{2c_{1}}{\sqrt{\eta}\alpha D}\right).

(3.16)

We begin by bounding the terms

\sum_{j\in[4k]}\left|\left\langle{v}-{v}^{\prime},x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}\right\rangle\right|.

For this we notice by Cauchy-Schwartz that each term is at most
\left\lVert v-v^{\prime}\right\rVert_{F}
times the Frobenius norm of the projection of
x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}
onto the span of the
S_{i}^{\prime}
. We note that for any
k
-dimensional subspace
W
with orthonormal basis
w_{1},\ldots,w_{k}
we have that

\displaystyle\E\left[\left\lVert\mathrm{Proj}_{W}(x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)})\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left|\left\langle w_{i},x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}\right\rangle\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=k.

Therefore, with high probability over the choice of
x^{(j)},y^{(j)}
each of the projections of
x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}
onto the span of the
S_{i}^{\prime}
has Frobenius norm
\tilde{O}(\sqrt{k})
. Therefore, if this condition holds over our choice of
x^{(j)}
and
y^{(j)}
, we can show Equation (
3.16
) if we can show that

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in[4k]}\left|\left\langle{v^{\prime}},x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\geqslant\left(\frac{c_{1}}{\sqrt{\eta}\alpha D}\right)\geqslant\left(\frac{2c_{1}}{\sqrt{\eta}\alpha D}\right)-\tau\tilde{O}(k^{3/2})

(3.17)

for all
v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
.

Each term in
\sum_{j\in[4k]}\left\langle{v}^{\prime},x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}\right\rangle
is a random bi-linear form
given by
z_{j}=\sum_{\ell,p\in[d]}{v}^{\prime}_{\ell,p}x^{(j)}_{\ell}y^{(j)}_{p}
.
Then, we have that
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[z_{j}\right]=0
and

\begin{split}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[z^{2}_{j}\right]=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(\sum_{\ell,p\in[d]}{v}^{\prime}_{\ell,p}x^{(j)}_{\ell}y^{(j)}_{p}\right)^{2}\right]&=\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime},p,p^{\prime}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[{v}^{\prime}_{\ell,p}{v}^{\prime}_{\ell^{\prime},p^{\prime}}x^{(j)}_{\ell}x^{(j)}_{\ell^{\prime}}y^{(j)}_{p}y^{(j)}_{p^{\prime}}\right]\\
&=\sum_{\ell,p\in[d]}\left({v}^{\prime}_{\ell,p}\right)^{2}\cdot\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(x^{(j)}_{\ell}\right)^{2}\right]\cdot\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(y^{(j)}_{p}\right)^{2}\right]\\
&=1\;,\end{split}

where the last equality follows from
{\tilde{v}^{\prime}}_{F}=1
.

Using Lemma
2.10
with
\zeta=\frac{2c_{1}}{\sqrt{\eta}\alpha D}
,

\ProbOp\left[\left|z_{j}\right|\leqslant\frac{c_{1}}{\sqrt{\eta}\alpha D}\right]\leqslant c_{5}\left(\frac{2c_{1}}{\sqrt{\eta}\alpha D}\right)^{1/2}\;.

(3.18)

However, we note that Equation (
3.17
) will hold unless
\left|z_{j}\right|\leqslant\frac{c_{1}}{\sqrt{\eta}\alpha D}
for all
j\in[4k]
.
Since the
z_{j}
’s are independent, we conclude that

\ProbOp\left[\sum_{j\in[4k]}\left|\left\langle{v}^{\prime},x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}\right\rangle\right|\leqslant\frac{c_{1}}{\sqrt{\eta}\alpha D}\right]\leqslant O\left(\frac{c_{1}}{\sqrt{\eta}\alpha D}\right)^{2k}\;.

(3.19)

Since the above argument holds for any
v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
, we can union bound over all elements in the cover
\mathcal{C}
,
and the probability that there exists a
\tilde{v}^{\prime}
in the cover that does not satisfy Equation (
3.17
)
is at most
O\left(k^{2}\sqrt{\eta}\alpha D\right)^{k}\cdot O\left(\frac{c_{1}}{\sqrt{\eta}\alpha D}\right)^{2k}
.
Setting
D
to be a sufficiently large multiple of
(k^{4}/(\sqrt{\eta}\alpha))
suffices to
conclude that with probability at least
1-1/{\poly(k)}
, the primal is feasible.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.4
.

We begin by bounding the Frobenius norm of
\hat{T}_{4}
.
Let
T_{4}=\E[h_{4}(\mathcal{X})]
. It then follows from Lemma
2.6
that

T_{4}=\mathrm{Sym}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}\left(3S_{i}\otimes S_{i}+6S_{i}\otimes\mu_{i}^{\otimes 2}+\mu_{i}^{\otimes 4}\right)\right)\;.

(3.20)

Further,
\left\lVert S_{i}\otimes S_{i}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\left\lVert S_{i}\right\rVert^{2}_{F}\leqslant\Delta^{2}
,

\left\lVert S_{i}\otimes\mu_{i}^{\otimes 2}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\left\lVert S_{i}\right\rVert_{F}\left\lVert\mu_{i}\right\rVert^{2}_{2}\leqslant 4\Delta/\alpha
,
and
\left\lVert\mu_{i}^{\otimes 4}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\left\lVert\mu_{i}\right\rVert^{4}_{2}\leqslant 16/\alpha^{2}
.
Since
T_{4}
is an average of terms of the form
S_{i}^{\otimes 2},S_{i}\otimes\mu_{i}^{\otimes 2}

and
\mu_{i}^{\otimes 4}
, and each such term is upper bounded, we can conclude that

\left\lVert T_{4}\right\rVert_{F}=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\Delta^{2}+1/\alpha^{2}\right)
, and by the triangle inequality that

\left\lVert\hat{T}_{4}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\Delta^{2}+1/\alpha^{2}+\eta\right)
.
Let
S_{i}^{\prime}=S_{i}+\mu_{i}^{\otimes 2}
and let
T_{4}^{\prime}:=\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}\left(S_{i}^{\prime}\otimes S_{i}^{\prime}\right)
.
We can then rewrite Equation (
3.20
) as follows:

T_{4}=\mathrm{Sym}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}\left(3S_{i}^{\prime}\otimes S_{i}^{\prime}-2\mu_{i}^{\otimes 4}\right)\right)\;.

(3.21)

For
j\in[4k]
, let
x^{(j)},y^{(j)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)
. Collapsing two modes of
\hat{T}_{4}
, it follows from Equation (
3.21
) that for any fixed
j
,

\begin{split}\hat{T}_{4}\left(\cdot,\cdot,x^{(j)},y^{(j)}\right)&=\left(\hat{T}_{4}-T_{4}\right)\hskip-4.33601pt\left(\cdot,\cdot,x^{(j)},y^{(j)}\right)+T_{4}\left(\cdot,\cdot,x^{(j)},y^{(j)}\right)\\
&=\left(\hat{T}_{4}-T_{4}\right)\hskip-4.33601pt\left(\cdot,\cdot,x^{(j)},y^{(j)}\right)+\mathrm{Sym}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}\left(3S_{i}^{\prime}\otimes S_{i}^{\prime}-2\mu_{i}^{\otimes 4}\right)\right)\left(\cdot,\cdot,x^{(j)},y^{(j)}\right)\\
&=\left(\hat{T}_{4}-T_{4}+T^{\prime}_{4}\right)\hskip-4.33601pt\left(\cdot,\cdot,x^{(j)},y^{(j)}\right)+\sum_{i\in[k]}w_{i}\left(S_{i}^{\prime}x^{(j)}\right)\otimes\left(S_{i}^{\prime}y^{(j)}\right)\\
&\hskip 14.45377pt+\sum_{i\in[k]}w_{i}\left(S_{i}^{\prime}y^{(j)}\right)\otimes\left(S_{i}^{\prime}x^{(j)}\right)+\sum_{i\in[k]}w_{i}\left(-2\mu_{i}^{\otimes 2}\left\langle\mu_{i},x^{(j)}\right\rangle\left\langle\mu_{i},y^{(j)}\right\rangle\right)\;,\end{split}

(3.22)

where we use that
\mathrm{Sym}(\cdot)
is a linear operator satisfying
\mathrm{Sym}\left(\mu_{i}^{\otimes 4}\right)=\mu_{i}^{\otimes 4}
, and

\mathrm{Sym}\left(S^{\prime}_{i}\otimes S^{\prime}_{i}\right)=\frac{1}{3}S^{\prime}_{i}\otimes S^{\prime}_{i}+\frac{1}{3}S^{\prime}_{i}\oplus S^{\prime}_{i}+\frac{1}{3}S^{\prime}_{i}\ominus S^{\prime}_{i}

where for indices
(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})
,
\left(S^{\prime}_{i}\oplus S^{\prime}_{i}\right)\left(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}\right)=\left(S^{\prime}_{i}\otimes S^{\prime}_{i}\right)\left(i_{1},i_{3},i_{2},i_{4}\right)
and
\left(S^{\prime}_{i}\ominus S^{\prime}_{i}\right)\left(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}\right)=\left(S^{\prime}_{i}\otimes S^{\prime}_{i}\right)\left(i_{1},i_{4},i_{2},i_{3}\right)
.

Next, it follows from Lemma
3.6
that there exists a matrix
\tilde{P}_{i}
such that

\left\lVert\tilde{P}_{i}\right\rVert_{F}=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(1/(\sqrt{\eta}\alpha)\right)
and

\left\lVert T^{\prime}_{4}\left(\cdot,\cdot,\tilde{P}_{i}\right)-S^{\prime}_{i}\right\rVert_{F}=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\sqrt{\eta/\alpha}\right)
.
Furthermore, with probability at least
0.99
, there exists a sequence of
b_{j}\in[-D,D]
,
for
j\in[4k]
, such that
T^{\prime}_{4}\left(\cdot,\cdot,\sum_{j\in[4k]}b_{j}x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}\right)=T^{\prime}_{4}\left(\cdot,\cdot,\tilde{P}_{i}\right)
.

Consider a cover,
\mathcal{C}
, of the interval
[-D,D]
with points spaced at intervals of length

\tau=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\frac{\sqrt{\eta}}{\alpha k\left(\Delta^{4}+1/\alpha^{4}\right)}\right)
. Since we uniformly sample
a_{j}
’s, with probability at least
(\tau/D)^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
, for all
j\in[4k]
,
\left|b_{j}-a_{j}\right|\leqslant\tau
, and we condition on this event. Thus,

\begin{split}\left\lVert T^{\prime}_{4}\left(\cdot,\cdot,\sum_{j\in[4k]}a_{j}x^{(j)}y^{(j)}\right)-S^{\prime}_{i}\right\rVert_{F}&\leqslant\left\lVert T^{\prime}_{4}\left(\cdot,\cdot,\sum_{j\in[4k]}b_{j}x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}\right)-S^{\prime}_{i}\right\rVert_{F}+\left\lVert T^{\prime}_{4}\left(\cdot,\cdot,\sum_{j\in[4k]}(b_{j}-a_{j})x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}\right)\right\rVert_{F}\\
&\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\sqrt{\eta/\alpha}\right)+\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\tau\Delta^{2}\right)\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\sqrt{\eta/\alpha}\right)\;.\end{split}

(3.23)

Taking the linear combinations with coefficients
a_{j}
in Equation (
3.22
), we have

\begin{split}\hat{T}_{4}\left(\cdot,\cdot,\sum_{j\in[4k]}a_{j}x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}\right)-S_{i}&=\left(\hat{T}_{4}-T_{4}+T^{\prime}_{4}\right)\hskip-2.8903pt\left(\cdot,\cdot,\sum_{j\in[4k]}a_{j}x^{(j)}\otimes y^{(j)}\right)-S^{\prime}_{i}-\mu_{i}\otimes\mu_{i}\\
&\hskip 14.45377pt+\sum_{j\in[4k]}a_{j}\sum_{i\in[k]}w_{i}\left(S_{i}^{\prime}x^{(j)}\right)\otimes\left(S_{i}^{\prime}y^{(j)}\right)+\sum_{j\in[4k]}a_{j}\sum_{i\in[k]}w_{i}\left(S_{i}^{\prime}y^{(j)}\right)\otimes\left(S_{i}^{\prime}x^{(j)}\right)\\
&\hskip 14.45377pt+\sum_{j\in[4k]}a_{j}\sum_{i\in[k]}w_{i}\left(-2\mu_{i}^{\otimes 2}\left\langle\mu_{i},x^{(j)}\right\rangle\left\langle\mu_{i},y^{(j)}\right\rangle\right)\;.\end{split}

(3.24)

Setting
P_{i}=\left(\hat{T}_{4}-T_{4}+T^{\prime}_{4}\right)\left(\cdot,\cdot,\sum_{j\in[4k]}a_{j}x^{(j)}y^{(j)}\right)-S^{\prime}_{i}
, it follows from Lemma
2.9
that with probability at least
0.99
,
\left(\hat{T}_{4}-T_{4}\right)\left(\cdot,\cdot,\sum_{j\in[4k]}a_{j}x^{(j)}y^{(j)}\right)
has Frobenius norm
\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(kD\eta\right)
and it follows from Equation (
3.23
) that with probability at least
0.99
,
T^{\prime}_{4}\left(\cdot,\cdot,\sum_{j\in[4k]}a_{j}x^{(j)}y^{(j)}\right)-S^{\prime}_{i}
has Frobenius norm
\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\sqrt{\eta/\alpha}\right)
. Setting the remaining terms to
Q_{i}
, with probability at least
0.99
we can bound their Frobenius norm as follows:

\begin{split}\left\lVert Q_{i}\right\rVert_{F}&\leqslant\left\lVert\mu_{i}\otimes\mu_{i}\right\rVert_{F}+\left\lVert\left(\sum_{i\in[k]}w_{i}S^{\prime}_{i}\oplus S^{\prime}_{i}+w_{i}S^{\prime}_{i}\ominus S^{\prime}_{i}-2w_{i}\mu_{i}^{\otimes 4}\right)\left(\cdot,\cdot,\sum_{j\in[4k]}a_{j}x^{(j)}y^{(j)}\right)\right\rVert_{F}\\
&\leqslant\frac{4}{\alpha}+\left(2\max_{i\in k}\left\lVert S^{\prime}_{i}\right\rVert^{2}_{F}+\frac{32}{\alpha^{2}}+k\tau\right)\cdot\left\lVert\tilde{P}\right\rVert_{F}\\
&\leqslant\frac{4}{\alpha}+\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\eta}\alpha}\left(\Delta+\frac{1}{\alpha}\right)^{2}\right)\\
&\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\frac{1+\Delta^{2}}{\sqrt{\eta}\alpha^{3}}\right)\;,\end{split}

(3.25)

where the first inequality follows from the triangle inequality,
the second follows from our assumptions that
\left\lVert\mu_{i}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant 2/\sqrt{\alpha}
,

\sum_{j\in[4k]}b_{j}x^{(j)}y^{(j)}=\tilde{P}_{i}
in the span of the
S^{\prime}_{i}
, and
\left|a_{j}-b_{j}\right|\leqslant\tau
for all
j\in[4k]
,
and the third inequality follows from the definition of
S^{\prime}_{i}
,
the bound on
\left\lVert\tilde{P}\right\rVert_{F}
and the bound on
\left\lVert S_{i}-I\right\rVert_{F}
.
∎

3.5
Finding a Low-dimensional Subspace for Exhaustive Search

In this subsection, we will prove Proposition
3.5
.

We start by extending Theorem 4 of
[
MV10
]
,
which shows that large parameter distance between pairs of univariate Gaussian mixtures
implies large distance between their low-degree moments.
In the following, we use
M_{j}(F)=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[F^{j}\right]
to denote the

j
-th moment of a distribution
F
. We show:

Lemma 3.9
.

There exists a constant
C>0
such that the following holds: Fix any
D>0
and
0\leqslant\beta\leqslant 1/(2(2k-1)!D^{2k-3})
. Suppose that
F=\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\sigma_{i}^{2})
is a univariate
k
-mixture of Gaussians with
w_{i}\geqslant\beta
, and
|\mu_{i}|,\sigma_{i}\leqslant D
,
for all
i\in[k]
. If
|\mu_{i}|+|\sigma_{i}^{2}-1|\geqslant\beta
for some
i\leqslant k
, then

\max_{j\in[2k]}\left|M_{j}(F)-M_{j}\left(\mathcal{N}(0,1)\right)\right|\geqslant\beta^{C^{k+1}(k+1)!-1}\;.

We give the proof of Lemma
3.9
in Section
3.6
.

Lemma 3.10

(Bounding
\mu_{i}
’s and
S_{i}
’s in non-influential directions for
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[h_{m}(\mathcal{M})\right]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
be a
k
-mixture of Gaussians on
\mathbb{R}^{d}

satisfying
w_{i}\geqslant\alpha
,
\left\lVert\mu_{i}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant 2/\sqrt{\alpha}
, and
\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-I\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\Delta
for every
i\in[k]
.
For some
B\in\mathbb{R}
, let
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a unit vector such that
\left|\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[h_{m}\left(\left\langle\mathcal{M},u\right\rangle\right)\right]\right|\leqslant B

for all
m\in[2k]
. Then, for
\delta=2^{O(k)}B^{1/(C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}
and
S_{i}=\Sigma_{i}-I
, we have that:

1.

for all
i\leqslant k
,
\left|\langle u,\mu_{i}\rangle\right|,\left|u^{\top}(I-\Sigma_{i})u\right|\leqslant\delta
,

2.

\left\lVert S_{i}u\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leqslant 20\delta\Delta/\alpha^{2}+B/\alpha
,

where
C>0
is a fixed universal constant.

Proof.

The 1-D random variable
\langle u,\mathcal{M}\rangle
is a mixture of Gaussians described by

\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\langle\mu_{i},u\rangle,u^{\top}\Sigma_{i}u)
. Towards a contradiction,
assume that there is an
i\in[k]
such that
\left|\langle u,\mu_{i}\rangle\right|+\left|u^{\top}(I-\Sigma_{i})u\right|\geqslant\delta
.
Then, applying Lemma
3.9
, yields that there is a
j\in[2k]
such that

|M_{j}(\langle u,\mathcal{M}\rangle)-M_{j}(\mathcal{N}(0,1))|\geqslant\delta^{C^{k+1}(k+1)!-1}
.
Applying Fact
2.5
implies that there exists an
m\in[2k]
such that

\left|\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[h_{m}(\langle u,\mathcal{M}\rangle)\right]\right|>2^{-O(k)}\delta^{C^{k+1}(k+1)!-1}\gg B\;,

yielding a contradiction.

We can now prove the second part.
Recall that for
S_{i}=\Sigma_{i}-I
for every
i
, we have that

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[h_{4}(\mathcal{M})\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}\textrm{Sym}\left(3\left(S_{i}\otimes S_{i}\right)+6\left(S_{i}\otimes\mu_{i}^{\otimes 2}\right)+\mu_{i}^{\otimes 4}\right)\,.

We consider the
d\times d
matrix obtained by the natural flattening of the
d\times d
tensor

u^{\otimes 2}\cdot\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[h_{4}(\mathcal{M})\right]
.
Then, we can write:

u^{\otimes 2}\cdot\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[h_{4}(\mathcal{M})\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}\Bigl((u^{\top}S_{i}u)S_{i}+2(S_{i}u)(S_{i}u)^{\top}+\langle u,\mu_{i}\rangle^{2}S_{i}\\
+2\langle u,\mu_{i}\rangle\mu_{i}(S_{i}u)^{\top}+2\langle u,\mu_{i}\rangle(S_{i}u)\mu_{i}^{\top}+(u^{\top}S_{i}u)\mu_{i}\mu_{i}^{\top}+\langle u,\mu_{i}\rangle^{2}\mu_{i}\mu_{i}^{\top}\Bigr)\;.

(3.26)

Now, from the first part, we know that for all
i\in[k]
,
|u^{\top}S_{i}u|\leqslant\delta
and the hypothesis of the lemma gives us that
\left\lVert S_{i}\right\rVert_{F}=\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-I\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\Delta
. Thus, for each
i
, the first term in the summation above has Frobenius norm at most
\Delta\delta
. Using that
\langle u,\mu_{i}\rangle_{2}^{2}\leqslant\delta^{2}
from the first part of the lemma,
yields that, for each
i
, the Frobenius norm of the third term is at most
\Delta\delta^{2}
.

Next, using in addition that
\left\lVert\mu_{i}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant 2/\sqrt{\alpha}
yields that, for each
i
,
the Frobenius norm of the 4th and 5th terms are at most
2\delta\Delta/\sqrt{\alpha}

and the Frobenius norm of the 6th and 7th terms are at most
\delta/\alpha
.
Thus, for each
i
and all but the 2nd term in the summation above,
we have an upper bound on the Frobenius norm of
4\delta\Delta/\alpha
.

Now, since
\left|\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[h_{4}\left(\left\langle\mathcal{M},u\right\rangle\right)\right]\right|\leqslant B
, and
u
is a unit vector,
we have that
\left\lVert u^{\otimes 2}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[h_{4}(\mathcal{M})\right]\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant B
.
Thus, combining the aforementioned argument with the triangle inequality, we have
for each
i
,

\begin{split}\left\lVert S_{i}u\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\left\lVert S_{i}u\left(S_{i}u\right)^{\top}\right\rVert_{F}&\leqslant\frac{1}{\alpha}\left\lVert u^{\otimes 2}\cdot\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[h_{4}(\mathcal{M})\right]\right\rVert_{F}+\sum_{i\in[k]}w_{i}\Bigl(\left(u^{\top}S_{i}u+\left\langle u,\mu_{i}\right\rangle^{2}\right)\left\lVert S_{i}\right\rVert_{F}\Bigr)\\
&\hskip 14.45377pt+\sum_{i\in[k]}4w_{i}\Bigl(\left(\left\langle u,\mu_{i}\right\rangle\right)\left\lVert\mu_{i}\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert S_{i}u\right\rVert_{2}\Bigr)+\sum_{i\in[k]}4w_{i}\Bigl(\left(\left\langle u,\mu_{i}\right\rangle^{2}+u^{\top}S_{i}u\right)\left\lVert\mu_{i}\right\rVert^{2}_{2}\Bigr)\\
&\leqslant B/\alpha+15\delta\Delta/\alpha\;,\end{split}

and the claim follows.
∎

Lemma 3.11

(Subspace covering all the means and large singular vectors of
S_{i}=\Sigma_{i}-I
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
be a
k
-mixture of Gaussians on
\mathbb{R}^{d}

satisfying
w_{i}\geqslant\alpha
,
\left\lVert\mu_{i}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant 2/\sqrt{\alpha}
,
and
