\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-I\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\Delta
for all
i\in[k]
. Given
0<\eta<1
,
let
\hat{T}_{m}
satisfy
\left\lVert\hat{T}_{m}-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[h_{m}(\mathcal{M})\right]\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\eta

for every
m\in[4k]
and let
\lambda\geqslant 2\eta
.
Let
V
be the span of all the left singular vectors of the
d\times d^{m-1}
matrix
obtained by the natural flattening of
\hat{T}_{m}
with singular values at least
\lambda
.
Then, for
\delta=2\lambda^{1/(2C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}
, we have that:

1.

\mathrm{dim}(V)\leqslant\left(4k\eta^{2}+k^{O(k)}\right)\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(1+1/\alpha+\Delta\right)^{4k}/\lambda^{2}
,

2.

Let

V_{\inf}=\{\mu_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}\cup\left\{v\mid\exists i\in[k],\text{ s.t. $\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{2}=1$ and $v$ is an eigenvector of $S_{i}$ and }\left\lVert S_{i}v\right\rVert_{2}\geqslant\sqrt{\delta}\right\}_{i\leqslant k}\,.

Then, for every unit vector
v\in V_{\inf}
,
\left\lVert v-\Pi_{V}v\right\rVert^{2}_{2}\leqslant 20\delta^{1/4}\Delta/\alpha^{2}
,
where
\Pi_{V}v
is the projection of
v
onto
V
.

Proof.

From Fact
2.6
, we have that

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[h_{m}(\mathcal{M})\right]=\sum_{i\in[k]}w_{i}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[h_{m}({G_{i}})\right]
,
where
G_{i}=\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
,
and since
\left\lVert\mu_{i}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant 2/\sqrt{\alpha}
and
\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-I\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\Delta
,
it follows that
\left\lVert\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[h_{m}(\mathcal{M})\right]\right\rVert^{2}_{F}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\left(m(1+1/\alpha+\Delta)\right)^{4m}
.
From Proposition
3.3
, we know that

\left\lVert\hat{T}_{m}\right\rVert^{2}_{F}\leqslant 2\left\lVert\hat{T}_{m}-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[h_{m}(\mathcal{M})\right]\right\rVert^{2}_{F}+2\left\lVert\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[h_{m}(\mathcal{M})\right]\right\rVert^{2}_{F}\leqslant\eta^{2}+\mathcal{O}\left(m(1+1/\alpha+\Delta)\right)^{4m}\;.

Thus, the number of singular vectors of the
d\times d^{m-1}
flattening of
\hat{T}_{m}

with a singular value
\geqslant\lambda
is at most

(\eta^{2}+\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(m(1+1/\alpha+\Delta)\right)^{4m})/\lambda^{2}
.
Summing up this bound for all
m\in[4k]
, yields the claimed upper bound on
\mathrm{dim}(V)
.

For the second part, we will first bound
\langle u,v\rangle
for any unit vector
u
orthogonal to the subspace
V
.
Towards this, observe that since
u
is orthogonal to
V
and
\left\|u\right\|_{2}=1
, we have

\left\lVert u\cdot\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[h_{m}(\mathcal{M})\right]\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\left\|u\hat{T}_{m}\right\|_{F}+\left\|\hat{T}_{m}-\E h_{m}(\mathcal{M})\right\|_{F}\\
\leqslant\lambda+\eta\leqslant 2\lambda\;,

where
u\cdot\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[h_{m}(\mathcal{M})\right]
is a matrix-vector product of
u
with
a
d\times d^{m-1}
flattening of
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[h_{m}(\mathcal{M})\right]
.
For
\delta=2\lambda^{1/(C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}
,
applying Lemma
3.10
yields that

\langle\mu_{i},u\rangle^{2}+\left\lVert S_{i}u\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leqslant\delta^{2}+20\delta\Delta/\alpha\leqslant 20\delta\Delta/\alpha^{2}\;.

(3.27)

Now, if
v
is one of the
\mu_{i}
’s, then we immediately get from Equation
3.27

that
\langle v,u\rangle^{2}\leqslant 20\delta\Delta/\alpha^{2}
. Similarly, note that if
v
is a unit length eigenvector
of
S_{i}
satisfying
\left\lVert S_{i}v\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\geqslant\sqrt{\delta}
, then,

\left\langle u,v\right\rangle^{2}=\frac{1}{\left\lVert S_{i}v\right\rVert^{2}_{2}}\left\langle u,S_{i}v\right\rangle^{2}=\frac{1}{\left\lVert S_{i}v\right\rVert^{2}_{2}}\left\langle S_{i}u,v\right\rangle^{2}\leqslant\frac{\left\lVert S_{i}u\right\rVert^{2}_{2}}{\left\lVert S_{i}v\right\rVert^{2}_{2}}\;.

In both cases, setting
u=(v-\Pi_{V}v)/\left\lVert v-\Pi_{V}v\right\rVert_{2}
completes the proof.
∎

We can now complete the proof of Proposition
3.5
:

Proof of Proposition
3.5
.

We know that
\hat{S}_{i}-P_{i}-S_{i}
is a symmetric, rank-
k^{\prime}
matrix such that
k^{\prime}=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(k^{2}\right)
,
described by the eigenvalue decomposition
\sum_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}\tau_{i}v_{i}v_{i}^{\top}
,
where
v_{i}
’s are the eigenvectors and
\tau_{i}
’s are the corresponding eigenvalues.
Since
\left\lVert S_{i}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\Delta
and

\left\lVert\hat{S}_{i}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\left\lVert P_{i}\right\rVert_{F}+\left\lVert Q_{i}\right\rVert_{F}+\left\lVert S_{i}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\sqrt{\eta/\alpha}\right)+\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\frac{1+\Delta^{2}}{\sqrt{\eta}\alpha^{3}}\right)+\Delta=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\frac{1+\Delta^{2}}{\sqrt{\eta}\alpha^{3}}\right)\;,

we have that the number of singular values of
\hat{S}_{i}
that exceed
\delta^{1/4}

is at most
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1+\Delta^{2}}{\sqrt{\eta}\alpha^{3}\sqrt{\delta}}\right)
.
Recall that from Lemma
3.11

it follows that the dimension of the subspace
V
is at most

k^{\mathcal{O}(k)}\mathcal{O}(1+1/\alpha+\Delta)^{4k}/\lambda^{2}
.
Thus, the dimension of
V^{\prime}
is at most

k^{\mathcal{O}(k)}\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\frac{\left(1+1/\alpha+\Delta\right)^{4k}}{\lambda^{2}}\right)+\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\frac{1+\Delta^{2}}{\sqrt{\eta}\alpha^{3}\sqrt{\delta}}\right)=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\frac{k^{\mathcal{O}(k)}\left(1+\frac{1}{\alpha}+\Delta\right)^{4k+5}}{\eta^{2}}\right)\;.

Since
V^{\prime}
contains
V
constructed in Lemma
3.11
,
we immediately obtain that for every
\mu_{i}
,

\left\lVert\mu_{i}-\Pi_{V^{\prime}}\mu_{i}\right\rVert^{2}_{2}\leqslant\frac{20}{\alpha^{2}}\sqrt{\delta}\Delta
.

Next, let
u
be a unit vector orthogonal to
V^{\prime}
.
Then, since
V^{\prime}
contains the
V
described in Lemma
3.11
,
we know that
\left\lVert S_{i}u\right\rVert^{2}_{2}\leqslant\frac{20}{\alpha^{2}}\sqrt{\delta}\Delta
.
Similarly, since
V^{\prime}
contains all eigenvectors of
\hat{S}_{i}
with singular values exceeding
\delta^{1/4}
,
we know that
\left\lVert\hat{S}_{i}u\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leqslant\delta^{1/2}
.
Thus, we can conclude that
\left\lVert(\hat{S}_{i}-S_{i})u\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leqslant\frac{100}{\alpha^{2}}\sqrt{\delta}\Delta
.
Let
Q_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{k^{\prime}}\tau_{j}v_{j}v_{j}^{\top}
with orthonormal
v_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
We know such
\tau_{j}
’s and
v_{j}
’s exist because of the upper bound on
\mathrm{rank}(Q_{i})
.
Therefore, for any
j
,
|v_{j}^{\top}(\hat{S}_{i}-S_{i})u|\leqslant\frac{10}{\alpha}\delta^{1/4}\Delta^{1/2}
.
On the other hand, for any
j
, we have that

v_{j}^{\top}(\hat{S}_{i}-S_{i})u\geqslant\langle v_{j},u\rangle\tau_{j}-\left\lVert P_{i}\right\rVert_{F}=\langle v_{j},u\rangle\tau_{j}-\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\eta})\;.

Combining the two bounds above, yields that whenever
\tau_{j}\geqslant\delta^{1/4}
,

|\langle v_{j},u\rangle|\leqslant\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\eta}/\tau_{j})+\frac{10}{\alpha\tau_{j}}\delta^{1/4}\Delta^{1/2}\leqslant\frac{10}{\alpha}\delta^{1/2}\Delta^{1/2}\;.

Thus, the matrix
\hat{Q}_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{k^{\prime}}\tau_{j}\Pi_{V^{\prime}}v_{j}(\Pi_{V^{\prime}}v_{j})^{\top}

has its range space in
V^{\prime}
and satisfies

\left\lVert\hat{Q}_{i}-Q_{i}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(k^{2}\delta^{1/4}\right)+\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\frac{k^{2}}{\alpha}\delta^{1/2}\Delta^{1/2}\right)=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\frac{k^{2}}{\alpha}\delta^{1/2}\Delta^{1/2}\right)\;.

∎

3.6
Parameter vs Moment Distance for Gaussian Mixtures

In this subsection, we prove Lemma
3.9
.
To that end, we will use the following two results; the second one is from
[
MV10
]
.

Lemma 3.12
.

Suppose
\mathcal{N}(\mu_{1},\sigma_{1}^{2})
and
\mathcal{N}(\mu_{2},\sigma_{2}^{2})
are univariate Gaussians
with
|\mu_{i}|,|\sigma_{i}|\leqslant D
, for some
D\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. If
|\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}|+|\sigma_{1}^{2}-\sigma_{2}^{2}|\leqslant\beta
,
then the distance between raw moments of two Gaussians is

\left|M_{j}(\mathcal{N}(\mu_{1},\sigma_{1}^{2}))-M_{j}(\mathcal{N}(\mu_{2},\sigma_{2}^{2}))\right|\leqslant(j+1)!D^{j-1}\beta.

Proof.

By Proposition
2.3
, the
j
-th raw moment of a Gaussian
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2})

is a sum of monomials in
\mu
and
\sigma^{2}
of degree
j
.
There are at most
(j+1)!
terms in the polynomial. Thus, changing the mean
or the variance by at most
\beta
will change the
j
-th moment by at most
(j+1)!D^{j-1}\beta
.
∎

Theorem 3.13
.

(
[
MV10
]
)
Let
F,F^{\prime}
be two univariate mixtures of Gaussians:

F=\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\sigma_{i}^{2})
and

F^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}w_{i}^{\prime}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i}^{\prime},{\sigma_{i}^{\prime}}^{2}).

There is a constant
c>0
such that, for any
\beta<c
, if
F,F^{\prime}
satisfy:

1.

w_{i},w_{i}^{\prime}\in[\beta,1]

2.

|\mu_{i}|,|\mu_{i}^{\prime}|\leqslant 1/\beta

3.

|\mu_{i}-\mu_{i^{\prime}}|+|\sigma_{i}^{2}-\sigma_{i^{\prime}}^{2}|\geqslant\beta
and
|\mu_{i}^{\prime}-\mu_{i^{\prime}}^{\prime}|+|{\sigma_{i}^{\prime}}^{2}-{\sigma_{i^{\prime}}^{\prime}}^{2}|\geqslant\beta
for all
i\neq i^{\prime}

4.

\beta\leqslant\min_{\pi}\sum_{i}\left(|w_{i}-w_{\pi(i)}^{\prime}|+|\mu_{i}-\mu_{\pi(i)}^{\prime}|+|\sigma^{2}-{\sigma_{\pi(i)}^{\prime}}^{2}|\right)
,
where the minimization is taken over all mappings
\pi:\{1,\dots,k\}\to\{1,\dots,k^{\prime}\}
,

then

\max_{j\in[2(k+k^{\prime}-1)]}|M_{j}(F)-M_{j}(F^{\prime})|\geqslant\beta^{O(k)}\;.

We are now ready to complete the proof of Lemma
3.9
.

Proof of Lemma
3.9
.

We proceed via induction on
k
. Consider the base case, i.e.,
k=1
.
Then, either
\left|\mu_{1}\right|\geqslant\beta/2
or
\left|\sigma_{1}-1\right|\geqslant\beta/2
,
and thus the first or second moment differ by at least
\beta^{2}/4
.
Let the inductive hypothesis be that Lemma
3.9
holds for at most
k
components.

Consider the case where
|\mu_{i}-\mu_{i^{\prime}}|+|\sigma_{i}^{2}-\sigma_{i^{\prime}}^{2}|\geqslant\beta^{C^{k}k!}
for all pairs of components
i,i^{\prime}\in[k]
. Then, by Theorem
3.13
, we have that

\max_{j\in[2k]}|M_{j}(F)-M_{j}(\mathcal{N}(0,1))|\geqslant\beta^{C^{k+1}k!}\geqslant\beta^{C^{k+1}(k+1)!-1}\;,

and the lemma follows.

Otherwise, we know that there exists a pair of components with parameter distance less than
\beta^{C^{k}k!}
.
In this case, we merge these two components and get a
(k-1)
-mixture
F^{\prime}
.
By Lemma
3.12
, the distance between
the
j
-th moments of
F^{\prime}
and
F
is at most
(j+1)!D^{j-1}\beta^{C^{k}k!}
.
Since we still have
|\mu_{i}^{\prime}|+|{\sigma_{i}^{\prime}}^{2}-1|\geqslant\beta-3\beta^{C^{k}k!}
for all components
i
in
F^{\prime}
,
the inductive hypothesis implies that

\max_{j\in[2k-2]}|M_{j}(F^{\prime})-M_{j}(\mathcal{N}(0,1))|\geqslant\left(\beta-3\beta^{C^{k}k!}\right)^{C^{k}(k)!-1}\;.

By the triangle inequality, we can write

\displaystyle\max_{j\in[2k]}|M_{j}(F)-M_{j}(\mathcal{N}(0,1))|

\displaystyle\geqslant\max_{j\in[2k-2]}|M_{j}(F^{\prime})-M_{j}(\mathcal{N}(0,1))|-\max_{j\in[2k-2]}|M_{j}(F)-M_{j}(F^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\geqslant\left(\beta-3\beta^{C^{k}k!}\right)^{C^{k}k!-1}-(2k-1)!D^{2k-3}\beta^{C^{k}k!}

\displaystyle\geqslant\beta^{C^{k+1}(k+1)!-1}\;.

The last inequality follows from the assumption that
\beta\leqslant 1/(2(2k-1)!D^{2k-3})
.
This completes the proof of Lemma
3.9
.
∎

4
Robust Partial Cluster Recovery

In this section, we give two robust
partial clustering
algorithms.
A partial clustering algorithm takes a set of points
X=\cup_{i\leqslant k}X_{i}

with true clusters
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{k}
and outputs a partition of the sample
X=X^{\prime}_{1}\cup X^{\prime}_{2}
such that
X^{\prime}_{1}=\cup_{i\in S}X_{i}
and

X^{\prime}_{2}=\cup_{i\not\in S}X_{i}
,
for some subset
S\subseteq[k]
of size
1\leqslant|S|<k
.
That is, a partial clustering algorithm partitions the sample into two non-empty parts
so that each part is a sample from a “sub-mixture”. This is a weaker guarantee than clustering the entire mixture, which must find each of the original
X_{i}
’s. We show that the relaxed guarantee is feasible even when the mixture as a whole
is not clusterable. In our setting, we will get an approximate (that is, a small fraction of points are misclassified) partial clustering that works for
\varepsilon
-corruptions
Y
of any i.i.d. sample
X

from a mixture of
k
Gaussians, as long as there is a pair of components in the original mixture
that have large total variation distance between them.

A partial clustering algorithm such as above was one of the innovations in
[
BK20b
]

that allowed for a polynomial-time algorithm for clustering all fully clusterable Gaussian mixtures.

In this section, we build on the ideas in
[
BK20b
]
to derive two new partial clustering algorithms
that work even when the original mixture is not fully clusterable. Both upgrade the results of
[
BK20b
]
by handling mixtures with arbitrary weights
w_{i}
s instead of uniform weights and handling mixtures where not all pairs of components are well-separated in TV distance. The first algorithm succeeds under the information-theoretically minimal separation assumption (i.e. separation in total variation distance) but runs in time exponential in the inverse mixing weight. The second algorithm is a key innovation of this paper – it gives an algorithm that runs in polynomial time in the inverse mixing weight at the cost of handling separation only in relative Frobenius distance. This improved running time guarantee (at the cost of strong separation requirement that we mitigate through a novel standalone spectral separation step in Section
5
) is crucial to obtaining the fully polynomial running time in our algorithm.

In order to state the guarantees of our algorithms,
we first formulate a notion of parameter separation
(same as the one employed in
[
BK20b
,
DHKK20
]
) as the next definition.

Definition 4.1

(
\Delta
-Parameter Separation)
.

We say that two Gaussian distributions
\mathcal{N}(\mu_{1},\Sigma_{1})
and
\mathcal{N}(\mu_{2},\Sigma_{2})

are
\Delta
-parameter separated if at least one of the following three conditions hold:

1.

Mean-Separation:

\exists v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

\langle\mu_{1}-\mu_{2},v\rangle^{2}>\Delta^{2}v^{\top}(\Sigma_{1}+\Sigma_{2})v
,

2.

Spectral-Separation:

\exists v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
v^{\top}\Sigma_{1}v>\Delta\,v^{\top}\Sigma_{2}v
,

3.

Relative-Frobenius Separation:

\Sigma_{i}
and
\Sigma_{j}
have the same range space and
\left\lVert\Sigma_{1}^{\dagger/2}(\Sigma_{2}-\Sigma_{1})\Sigma_{1}^{\dagger/2}\right\rVert^{2}_{F}>\Delta^{2}\left\lVert\Sigma_{1}^{\dagger}\Sigma_{2}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}
.

As shown in
[
DHKK20
,
BK20b
]
, if a pair of Gaussians is
(1-\exp(-O(\Delta\log\Delta))
-separated in total variation distance, then, they are
\Delta
-parameter separated.

Our first algorithm succeeds in robust partial clustering whenever there is a pair of component Gaussians that are

\Delta
-parameter separated. The running time of this algorithm grows exponentially
in the reciprocal of the minimum weight in the mixture.

Theorem 4.2

(Robust Partial Clustering in TV Distance)
.

Let
0\leqslant\varepsilon<\alpha\leqslant 1
, and
\eta>0
. There is an algorithm with the following guarantees:
Let
\{\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i}\}_{i\leqslant k}
be means and covariances of
k
unknown Gaussians.
Let
Y
be an
\varepsilon
-corruption of a sample
X
of size
n\geqslant(dk)^{Ct}/\varepsilon
for a large enough constant
C>0
, from
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
satisfying Condition
2.47
with parameters
t=(k/\eta)^{O(k)}
and

\gamma\leqslant\varepsilon d^{-8t}k^{-Ct}
, for a sufficiently large constant
C>0
.
Suppose further that
w_{i}\geqslant\alpha>2\varepsilon
for every
i
and that
there are
i,j
such that
\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
and
\mathcal{N}(\mu_{j},\Sigma_{j})

are
\Delta
-parameter separated for
\Delta=(k/\eta)^{O(k)}
.

Then, the algorithm on input
Y
,
runs in time
n^{(k/\eta)^{O(k)}}
, and with probability at least
2^{-O\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{k}{\eta\alpha}\right)\right)}

over the draw of
X
and the algorithm’s random choices,
the algorithm outputs a partition of
Y
into
Y_{1},Y_{2}
satisfying:

1.

Partition respects clustering:
for each
i
,

\max\{\frac{k}{n}|Y_{1}\cap X_{i}|,\frac{k}{n}|Y_{2}\cap X_{i}|\}\geqslant 1-\eta-O(\varepsilon/\alpha^{4})
, and,

2.

Partition is non-trivial:

\max_{i}\frac{k}{n}|X_{i}\cap Y_{1}|,\max_{i}\frac{k}{n}|X_{i}\cap Y_{2}|\geqslant 1-\eta-O(\varepsilon/\alpha^{4})
.

Our proof of the above theorem is based on a relatively straightforward extension of the ideas of
[
BK20b
]
, albeit
with two key upgrades 1) allowing the input mixtures to have arbitrary mixing weights
(at an exponential cost in the inverse of the minimum weight) and 2) handling mixtures where some pair of components may not be well-separated in TV distance.

In order to get our main result that gives a fully polynomial algorithm (including in the inverse mixing weights),
we will use a incomparable variant of the above partial clustering method that only handles
a weaker notion of parameter separation, but runs in fixed polynomial time.

Theorem 4.3

(Robust Partial Clustering in Relative Frobenius Distance)
.

Let
0\leqslant\varepsilon<\alpha/k\leqslant 1
and
t\in\mathbb{N}
. There is an algorithm with the following guarantees:
Let
\{\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i}\}_{i\leqslant k}
be means and covariances of
k
unknown Gaussians.
Let
Y
be an
\varepsilon
-corruption of a sample
X
of size
n\geqslant(dk)^{Ct}/\varepsilon
for a large enough constant
C>0
, from

\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
that satisfies Condition
2.47

with parameters
2t
and
\gamma\leqslant\varepsilon d^{-8t}k^{-Ck}
, for a large enough constant
C>0
.
Suppose further that
w_{i}\geqslant\alpha>2\varepsilon
for each
i\in[k]
,
and that for some
t\in\mathbb{N}
,
\beta>0
there exist
i,j\leqslant k
such that

\left\lVert\Sigma^{\dagger/2}(\Sigma_{i}-\Sigma_{j})\Sigma^{\dagger/2}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}=\Omega\left((k^{2}t^{4})/(\beta^{2/t}\alpha^{4})\right)
,
where
\Sigma
is the covariance of the mixture
\mathcal{M}
.
Then, the algorithm runs in time
n^{O(t)}
, and with probability at least
2^{-O\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{k}{\eta}\right)\right)}
over the random choices of the algorithm,
outputs a partition
Y=Y_{1}\cup Y_{2}
satisfying:

1.

Partition respects clustering:
for each
i
,
\max\left\{\frac{1}{w_{i}n}|Y_{1}\cap X_{i}|,\frac{1}{w_{i}n}|Y_{2}\cap X_{i}|\right\}\geqslant 1-\beta-O(\varepsilon/\alpha^{4})
, and,

2.

Partition is non-trivial:

\max_{i}\frac{1}{w_{i}n}|X_{i}\cap Y_{1}|,\max_{i}\frac{1}{w_{i}n}|X_{i}\cap Y_{2}|\geqslant 1-\beta-O(\varepsilon/\alpha^{4})
.

The starting point for the proof of the above theorem is the observation that the running time of our first algorithm above is exponential in the inverse mixing weight almost entirely because of dealing with spectral separation
(which requires the use of “certifiable anti-concentration” that we define in the next subsection).
We formulate a variant of relative Frobenius separation (that is directly useful to us) and prove that
whenever the original mixture has a pair of components separated in this notion,
we can in fact obtain a fully polynomial partial clustering algorithm building on the ideas in
[
BK20b
]
.

4.1
Algorithm

Our algorithm will solve SoS relaxations of a polynomial inequality system.
The constraints here use the input
Y
to encode finding a sample
X^{\prime}

(the intended setting being
X^{\prime}=X
, the original uncorrupted sample)
and a cluster
\hat{C}
in
X^{\prime}
of size
=\alpha n
, indicated by
z_{i}
s
(the intended setting is simply the indicator for any of the
k
true clusters)
satisfying properties of Gaussian distribution (certifiable hypercontractivity and anti-concentration).

Covariance constraints introduce a matrix valued indeterminate
\Pi
intended to be the square root of
\hat{\Sigma}
, the sos variable for the covariance of a single component.

\text{Covariance Constraints: $\mathcal{A}_{1}$}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &&\Pi&=UU^{\top}\\
&&\Pi^{2}&=\hat{\Sigma}\\
\end{aligned}\right\}

(4.1)

The intersection constraints force that
X^{\prime}
be
\varepsilon
-close to
Y
(and thus,
2\varepsilon
-close to unknown sample
X
).

\text{Intersection Constraints: $\mathcal{A}_{2}$}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\forall i\in[n],&m_{i}^{2}&=m_{i}\\
&&\textstyle\sum_{i\in[n]}m_{i}&=(1-\varepsilon)n\\
&\forall i\in[n],&m_{i}(y_{i}-x^{\prime}_{i})&=0\end{aligned}\right\}

(4.2)

The subset constraints introduce
z
, which indicates the subset
\hat{C}
intended to be the true clusters of
X^{\prime}
.

\text{Subset Constraints: $\mathcal{A}_{3}$}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\forall i\in[n].&z_{i}^{2}&=z_{i}\\
&&\textstyle\sum_{i\in[n]}z_{i}&=\alpha n\\
\end{aligned}\right\}

(4.3)

Parameter constraints create indeterminates to stand for the covariance
\hat{\Sigma}
and mean
\hat{\mu}
of
\hat{C}
(indicated by
z
).

\text{Parameter Constraints: $\mathcal{A}_{4}$}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &&\frac{1}{\alpha n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}\left(x^{\prime}_{i}-\hat{\mu}\right)\left(x^{\prime}_{i}-\hat{\mu}\right)^{\top}&=\hat{\Sigma}\\
&&\frac{1}{\alpha n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}x^{\prime}_{i}&=\hat{\mu}\\
\end{aligned}\right\}

(4.4)

Certifiable Hypercontractivity :
\mathcal{A}_{4}
=

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\forall t\leqslant 2s&\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}n^{2}}\sum_{i,j\leqslant n}z_{i}z_{j}(Q(x^{\prime}_{i}-x^{\prime}_{j})-\E_{z}Q)^{2t}&\leqslant\left(\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}n^{2}}\sum_{i,j\leqslant n}z_{i}z_{j}(Q(x^{\prime}_{i}-x^{\prime}_{j})-\E_{z}Q)^{2}\right)^{t}\\
&&{\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}n^{2}}\sum_{i,j\leqslant n}z_{i}z_{j}\left(Q(x^{\prime}_{i}-x^{\prime}_{j})-\E_{z}Q\right)^{2}}&{\leqslant\frac{6}{\alpha^{2}}\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}}\end{aligned}\right\}

(4.5)

Here, we used the shorthand
\E_{z}Q=\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}n^{2}}\sum_{i,j\leqslant n}z_{i}z_{j}Q(x^{\prime}_{i}-x^{\prime}_{j})
.

In the constraint system for our first algorithm, we will use the following certifiable anti-concentration constraints on
\hat{C}
for
\delta=\alpha^{-\poly(k)}
and
\tau=\alpha/\poly(k)
and
s(u)=1/u^{2}
for every
u
.

\text{Certifiable Anti-Concentration : $\mathcal{A}_{5}$}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &&\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}n^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}z_{i}z_{j}q_{\delta,\Sigma}^{2}\left(\left(x^{\prime}_{i}-x^{\prime}_{j}\right),v\right)&\leqslant 2^{s(\delta)}C\delta\left(v^{\top}\Sigma v\right)^{s(\delta)}\\
&&\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}n^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}z_{i}z_{j}q_{\tau,\Sigma}^{2}\left(\left(x^{\prime}_{i}-x^{\prime}_{j}\right),v\right)&\leqslant 2^{s(\tau)}C\tau\left(v^{\top}\Sigma v\right)^{s(\eta)}\\
\end{aligned}\right\}

(4.6)

We note that the constraint system for our second algorithm (running in fixed polynomial time), we will not use
\mathcal{A}_{5}
.
Towards proving Theorems
4.3
and
4.2
we use the following algorithm that differs only in the degree of the pseudo-distribution computed and the constraint system that the pseudo-distribution satisfies.

Algorithm 4.4

(Partial Clustering)
.

Given:

A sample
Y
of size
n
. An outlier parameter
\varepsilon>0
and an accuracy parameter
\eta>0
.

Output:

A partition of
Y
into partial clustering
Y_{1}\cup Y_{2}
.

Operation:

1.

SDP Solving:
Find a pseudo-distribution
\tilde{\zeta}
satisfying
\cup_{i=1}^{5}\mathcal{A}_{i}
(
\cup_{i=1}^{4}\mathcal{A}_{i}
for Theorem
4.3
) such that
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}z_{i}\leqslant\alpha+o_{d}(1)
for every
i
. If no such pseudo-distribution exists, output fail.

2.

Rounding:
Let
M=\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{z\sim\tilde{\zeta}}[zz^{\top}]
.

(a)

Choose
\ell=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}\log(k/\eta)\right)
rows of
M
uniformly at random and independently.

(b)

For each
i\leqslant\ell
, let
\hat{C}_{i}
be the indices of the columns
j
such that
M(i,j)\geqslant\eta^{2}\alpha^{5}/k
.

(c)

Choose a uniformly random
S\subseteq[\ell]
and output
Y_{1}=\cup_{i\in S}\hat{C}_{i}
and
Y_{2}=Y\setminus Y_{1}
.

4.2
Analysis

Simultaneous Intersection Bounds.

The key observation for proving the first theorem is the following lemma
that gives a sum-of-squares proof that no
z
that satisfies the constraints
\cup_{i=1}^{5}\mathcal{A}_{i}

can have simultaneously large intersections with the
\Delta
-parameter
separated component Gaussians.

Lemma 4.5

(Simultaneous Intersection Bounds for TV-separated case)
.

Let
Y
be an
\varepsilon
-corruption of a sample
X
of size
n\geqslant(dk)^{Ct}/\varepsilon
for a large enough constant
C>0
, from
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
satisfying Condition
2.47
with parameters
t=(k/\eta)^{O(k)}
and

\gamma\leqslant\varepsilon d^{-8t}k^{-Ct}
, for a sufficiently large constant
C>0
.
Suppose further that
w_{i}\geqslant\alpha>2\varepsilon
for every
i
and that
there are
i,j
such that
\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
and
\mathcal{N}(\mu_{j},\Sigma_{j})

are
\Delta
-parameter separated for
\Delta=(k/\eta)^{O(k)}
.
Then, there exists a partition of
[k]
into
S\cup L
such that,
|S|,|L|<k

and for
z(X_{r})=\frac{1}{w_{r}n}\sum_{i\in X_{r}}z_{i}
,

\left\{\cup_{i=1}^{5}\mathcal{A}_{i}\right\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{56.08414pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-56.08414pt\raisebox{-11.92337pt}{\makebox[56.08414pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{(k/\eta\alpha)}^{\poly(k)}}$}}}\hskip-56.08414pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[56.08414pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{z}$}}}$}}}\left\{\sum_{i\in S,j\in L}z(X_{i})z(X_{j})\leqslant O(k^{2}\varepsilon/\alpha)+\eta/\alpha\right\}\,.

The proof of Lemma
4.5
is given in
Section
4.3
.

For the second theorem, we use the following version that strengthens the separation assumption
and lowers the degree of the sum-of-squares proof (and consequently the running time of the algorithm) as a result.

Lemma 4.6

(Simultaneous Intersection Bounds for Frobenius Separated Case)
.

Let
X
be a sample of size
n\geqslant(dk)^{Ct}/\varepsilon
for a large enough constant
C>0
, from

\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
that satisfies Condition
2.47

with parameters
2t
and
\gamma\leqslant\varepsilon d^{-8t}k^{-Ck}
, for a large enough constant
C>0
.
Suppose further that
w_{i}\geqslant\alpha>2\varepsilon
for each
i\in[k]
,
and that for some
t\in\mathbb{N}
,
\beta>0
there exist
i,j\leqslant k
such that

\left\lVert\Sigma^{\dagger/2}(\Sigma_{i}-\Sigma_{j})\Sigma^{\dagger/2}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}=\Omega\left((k^{2}t^{4})/(\beta^{2/t}\alpha^{2})\right)
,
where
\Sigma
is the covariance of the mixture
\mathcal{M}
.
Then, for any
\varepsilon
-corruption
Y
of
X
, there exists a partition of
[k]=S\cup T
such that

\left\{\cup_{i=1}^{4}\mathcal{A}_{i}\right\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{z}$}}}$}}}\left\{\sum_{i\in S}\sum_{j\in T}z(X_{i})z(X_{j})\leqslant O(k^{2})\beta+O(k^{2})\varepsilon/\alpha\right\}\,.

Here,
z(X_{r})=\frac{1}{w_{r}n}\sum_{i\in X_{r}}z_{i}
for every
r
.

The proof of Lemma
4.6
is given in
Section
4.4
.

Notice that the main difference between the above two lemmas
is the constraint systems they use. Specifically, the second lemma does
not
enforce
certifiable anti-concentration constraints. As a result, there is a difference in
the degree of the sum-of-squares proofs they claim; the degree of the SoS proof in the second lemma
does not depend on the inverse minimum mixture weight.

First, we complete the proof of the Theorem
4.2
.
The proof of Theorem
4.3
is exactly the same except
for the use of Lemma
4.6

(and thus has the exponent in the running time independent of
1/\alpha
)
instead of Lemma
4.5
.

Proof of Theorem
4.2
.

Let
\eta^{\prime}=O(\eta^{2}\alpha^{3}/k)
. We will prove that whenever
\Delta\geqslant\poly(k/\eta^{\prime})^{k}=\poly\left(\frac{k}{\eta\alpha}\right)^{k}
, Algorithm
4.4
, when run with input
Y
, with probability at least
0.99
,
recovers a collection
\hat{C}_{1},\hat{C}_{2},\ldots,\hat{C}_{\ell}
of
\ell=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}\log k/\eta\right)

subsets of indices satisfying
|\cup_{i\leqslant\ell}\hat{C}_{i}|\geqslant(1-\eta^{\prime}/k^{40})n

such that there is a partition
S\cup L=[\ell]
,
0<|S|<\ell
satisfying:

\min\Biggl\{\frac{1}{\alpha n}|\hat{C}_{i}\cap\cup_{j\in S}X_{j}|,\frac{1}{\alpha n}|\hat{C}_{i}\cap\cup_{j\in L}X_{j}|\Biggr\}\leqslant 100\eta^{\prime}/\alpha^{3}+O(\varepsilon/\alpha^{4})\,.

(4.7)

We first argue that this suffices to complete the proof.
Split
[\ell]
into two groups
G_{S},G_{L}
as follows.
For each
i
, let
j=\argmax_{r\in[\ell]}\frac{1}{\alpha n}|\hat{C}_{i}\cap X_{r}|
.
If
j\in S
, add it to
G_{S}
, else add it to
G_{L}
.
Observe that this process is well-defined - i.e, there cannot be
j\in S
and
j^{\prime}\in L
that both maximize
\frac{1}{\alpha n}|\hat{C}_{i}\cap X_{r}|
as
r
varies over
[k]
. To see this, WLOG, assume
j\in S
. Note that
\frac{1}{\alpha n}|\hat{C}_{i}\cap X_{j}|\geqslant 1/k
. Then, we immediately obtain:
\frac{1}{\alpha n}|\cup_{j\in S}X_{j}\cap\hat{C}_{i}|\geqslant 1/k
. Now, if we ensure that
\eta^{\prime}\leqslant\alpha^{3}/k^{2}
and
\varepsilon\leqslant O(\alpha^{4}/k)
, then,
\frac{1}{\alpha n}|\hat{C}_{i}\cap\cup_{j^{\prime}\in L}X_{j^{\prime}}|
is at most the RHS of (
4.7
) which is
\ll 1/k
. This completes the proof of well-definedness. Next, adding up (
4.7
) for each
i\in S
yields that

\frac{1}{|\hat{C}_{i}|}|\left(\cup_{i\in G_{S}}X_{i}\right)\cap\cup_{j\in L}X_{j}|\leqslant O(\log(k/\eta^{\prime})/\alpha)\left(\eta^{\prime}+\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon/\alpha\right)\right),

where we used that
|G_{S}|\leqslant\ell
. Combined with
|\cup_{i\leqslant\ell}\hat{C}_{i}|\geqslant(1-\eta^{\prime}/k^{40})n
, we obtain that

|\cup_{i\in G_{S}}X_{i}|\geqslant 1-\eta^{\prime}/k^{40}-O(\log(k/\eta^{\prime})/\alpha)\left(\eta^{\prime}+O(\varepsilon/\alpha)\right)=\eta+\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\log(k/\eta\alpha)\varepsilon/\alpha^{2}\right)

for
\eta^{\prime}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\eta^{2}\alpha^{3}/k\right)
.

We now go ahead and establish (
4.7
).
Let
\tilde{\zeta}
be a pseudo-distribution satisfying
\mathcal{A}
of degree
(k/\eta)^{\poly(k)}
satisfying
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}z_{i}=\alpha
for every
i
. Such a pseudo-distribution exists. To see why, let
\tilde{\zeta}
be the actual distribution that always sets
X^{\prime}=X
, chooses an
i
with probability
w_{i}
and outputs a uniformly subset
\hat{C}
of size
\alpha n
of
X_{i}
conditioned on
\hat{C}
satisfying
\mathcal{A}
. Then, notice that since
X
satisfies Condition
2.47
, by Fact
2.45
, the uniform distribution on each
X_{i}
has
t
-certifiably
C
-hypercontractive degree
2
polynomials and is
t
-certifiably
C\delta
-anti-concentrated. By an concentration argument using high-order Chebyshev inequality, similar to the proof of Lemma
2.51
(applied to uniform distribution on
X_{i}
of size
n\geqslant(dk)^{O(t)}
,
\hat{C}
chosen above satisfies the constraints
\mathcal{A}
with probability at least
1-o_{d}(1)
. Observe that the probabililty that
z_{i}
is set to
1
under this distribution is then at most
\alpha+o_{d}(1)
. Thus, such a distribution satisfies all the constraints in
\mathcal{A}
.

Next, let
M=\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}[zz^{\top}]
. Then, we claim that:

1.

o_{d}(1)+\alpha\geqslant M(i,j)\geqslant 0
for all
i,j
,

2.

M(i,i)\in\alpha\pm o_{d}(1)
for all
i
,

3.

\E_{j\sim[n]}M(i,j)\geqslant\alpha^{2}-o_{d}(1)
for every
i
.

The proofs of these basic observations are similar to those presented in Chapter 4.3 of
[
FKP19
]
(see also the proof of Theorem 5.1 in
[
BK20b
]
):
Observe that
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{7.98607pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-7.98607pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[7.98607pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4}$}}}\hskip-7.98607pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[7.98607pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}$}}}\left\{z_{i}z_{j}=z_{i}^{2}z_{j}^{2}\geqslant 0\right\}
for every
i
. Thus, by Fact
2.18
,
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}[z_{i}z_{j}]\geqslant 0
for every
i,j
. Next, observe that
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{7.98607pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-7.98607pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[7.98607pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-7.98607pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[7.98607pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}$}}}\left\{(1-z_{i})=(1-z_{i})^{2}\geqslant 0\right\}
for every
i
and thus,
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{7.98607pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-7.98607pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[7.98607pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-7.98607pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[7.98607pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}$}}}\left\{z_{i}(1-z_{j})\geqslant 0\right\}
. Thus, by Fact
2.18
again, we must have
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}[z_{i}z_{j}]\leqslant\tilde{\mathbb{E}}[z_{i}]\leqslant\alpha+o_{d}(1)
. Finally,
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{7.98607pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-7.98607pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[7.98607pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-7.98607pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[7.98607pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}$}}}\left\{\sum_{j}z_{i}z_{j}=z_{i}\sum_{j}z_{j}=\alpha nz_{i}\right\}
. Thus, by Fact
2.18
again, we must have
\sum_{j}M(i,j)=\sum_{j}\tilde{\mathbb{E}}[z_{i}z_{j}]=\alpha n\sum_{j}\tilde{\mathbb{E}}[z_{i}]\in(\alpha^{2}\pm o_{d}(1))n
. Let
B_{i}
be the entries in the
i
-th row
M_{i}
that are larger than
\alpha^{2}/2
. Then, by (1) and (2), we immediately derive that
B_{i}
must have at least
\alpha n/2
elements.
Call an entry of
M
large if it exceeds
\alpha^{2}\eta^{\prime}
. For each
i
, let
B_{i}
be the set of large entries in row
i
of
M
. Then, using (3) and (1) above gives that
|B_{i}|\geqslant\alpha(1-\alpha\eta^{\prime})n
for each
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
. Next, call a row
i
“good” if
\frac{1}{\alpha n}\min\{\left|\cup_{r\in L}X_{r}\cap B_{i}\right|,\left|\cup_{r^{\prime}\in S}X_{r^{\prime}}\cap B_{i}\right|\}\leqslant 100\eta^{\prime}/\alpha^{3}+O(\varepsilon/\alpha^{4})
. Let us estimate the fraction of rows of
M
that are good.

Towards that goal, let us apply Lemma
4.5
with
\eta
set to
\eta^{\prime}
and use Fact
2.18
(SoS Completeness), to obtain
\sum_{r\in S,r^{\prime}\in L}\E_{i\in X_{r}}\E_{j\in X_{r^{\prime}}}M(i,j)\leqslant\eta^{\prime}+O(\varepsilon/\alpha)
. Using Markov’s inequality, with probability
1-\alpha^{3}/100
over the uniformly random choice of
i
,
\E_{j\in X_{r^{\prime}}}M(i,j)\leqslant 100\frac{1}{\alpha^{3}}\eta^{\prime}+O(\varepsilon/\alpha^{4})
. Thus,
1-\alpha^{3}/100
fraction of the rows of
M
are good.

Next, let
R
be the set of
\frac{100}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{k^{50}}{\eta^{\prime}}\right)
rows sampled in the run of the algorithm and set
\hat{C}_{i}=B_{i}
for every
i\in R
. The probability that all of them are good is then at least
(1-\alpha^{3}/100)^{\frac{100}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{k^{50}}{\alpha\eta^{\prime}}\right)}\geqslant 1-\alpha
. Let us estimate the probability that
|\cup_{i\in R}\hat{C}_{i}|\geqslant(1-\eta^{\prime}/k^{40})n
. For a uniformly random
i
, the chance that a given point
t\in B_{i}
is at least
\alpha(1-\alpha\eta^{\prime})
. Thus, the chance that
t\not\in\cup_{i\in R}B_{i}
is at most
(1-\alpha/2)^{100/\alpha\log\left(k^{50}/(\alpha\eta^{\prime})\right)}\leqslant\eta^{\prime}/k^{50}
. Thus, the expected number of
t
that are not covered by
\cup_{i\in R}\hat{C}_{i}
is at most
n\eta^{\prime}/k^{50}
. Thus, by Markov’s inequality, with probability at least
1-1/k^{10}
,
1-\eta^{\prime}/k^{40}
fraction of
t
are covered in
\cup_{i\in R}\hat{C}_{i}
. By the above computations and a union bound, with probability at least
1-\eta^{\prime}/k^{10}
both the conditions below hold simultaneously: 1) each of the
\frac{100}{\alpha}\log\left(k^{50}/\eta^{\prime}\right)
rows
R
sampled are good and 2)
|\cup_{i\in R}\hat{C}_{i}|\geqslant(1-\eta^{\prime}/k^{40})n
. This completes the proof.
∎

4.3
Proof of Lemma
4.5

Our proof is based on the following simultaneous intersection bounds from
[
BK20b
]
.
We will use the following lemma that forms the crux of the analysis of the clustering algorithm in
[
BK20b
]
:

Lemma 4.7

(Simultaneous Intersection Bounds, Lemma 5.4 in
[
BK20b
]
)
.

Fix
\delta>0,k\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
X=X_{1}\cup X_{2}\cup\ldots X_{k}
be a good sample of size
n
from a
k
-mixture

\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
of Gaussians. Let
Y
be any
\varepsilon
-corruption of
X
.
Suppose there are
r,r^{\prime}\leqslant k
such that one of the following three conditions hold for some
\Delta\geqslant(k/\delta)^{O(k)}
:

1.

there exists a
v
such that
v^{\top}\Sigma(r^{\prime})v>\Delta v^{\top}\Sigma(r^{\prime})v
and

B=\max_{i\leqslant k}\frac{v^{\top}\Sigma(i)v}{v^{\top}\Sigma(r^{\prime})v}
, or

2.

there exists a
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\left\langle\mu(r)-\mu(r^{\prime}),v\right\rangle_{2}^{2}\geqslant\Delta^{2}v^{\top}\left(\Sigma(r)+\Sigma(r^{\prime})\right)v
, or,

3.

\left\lVert\Sigma(r^{\prime})^{-1/2}\Sigma(r)\Sigma(r^{\prime})^{-1/2}-I\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\geqslant\Delta^{2}\left(\left\lVert\Sigma(r^{\prime})^{-1/2}\Sigma(r)^{1/2}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}^{4}\right)
.

Then, for the linear polynomial
z(X_{r})=\frac{1}{\alpha n}\sum_{i\in X_{r}}z_{i}
in indeterminates
z_{i}
s satisfies:

\left\{\cup_{i\leqslant 5}\mathcal{A}_{i}\right\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{64.34578pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-64.34578pt\raisebox{-8.72893pt}{\makebox[64.34578pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{(k/\delta)^{O(k)}\log(2B)}$}}}\hskip-64.34578pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[64.34578pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{z}$}}}$}}}\left\{z(X_{r})z(X_{r^{\prime}})\leqslant O(\sqrt{\delta})+O(\varepsilon/\alpha)\right\}\,.

Proof of Lemma
4.5
.

Without loss of generality, assume that the pair of separated components are
\mathcal{N}(\mu_{1},\Sigma_{1})
and
\mathcal{N}(\mu_{2},\Sigma_{2})
. Let us start with the case when the pair is spectrally separated.
Then, there is a
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\Delta v^{\top}\Sigma_{1}v\leqslant v^{\top}\Sigma_{2}v
.

Consider an ordering of the true clusters along the direction
v
, renaming cluster indices if needed, such that
v^{\top}\Sigma_{1}v\leqslant v^{\top}\Sigma_{2}v\leqslant\ldots v^{\top}\Sigma_{k}v
. Let
j\leqslant k^{\prime}
be the largest integer such that
\poly(k/\eta)v^{\top}\Sigma_{j}v\leqslant v^{\top}\Sigma_{j+1}v
. Further, observe that since
j
is defined to be the largest index which incurs separation
\poly(k/\eta)
, all indices in
[j,k]
have spectral bound at most
\poly(k/\eta)
and thus
\frac{v^{\top}\Sigma_{k}v}{v^{\top}\Sigma_{j}v}\leqslant\poly(k/\eta)^{k}
.

Applying Lemma
4.7
with the above direction
v
to every
r<j
and
r^{\prime}\geqslant j
and observing that the parameter
B
in each case is at most
\frac{v^{\top}\Sigma_{k}v}{v^{\top}\Sigma_{j}v}\leqslant\Delta^{k}
yields:

\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{73.9242pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-73.9242pt\raisebox{-8.90556pt}{\makebox[73.9242pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O({k}^{2}s^{2}\poly\log(\Delta))}$}}}\hskip-73.9242pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[73.9242pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{z}$}}}$}}}\left\{z(X_{r})z(X_{r^{\prime}})\leqslant O(\varepsilon/\alpha)+\sqrt{\delta}\right\}\,.

Adding up the above inequalities over all
r\leqslant j-1
and
r^{\prime}\geqslant j+1
and taking
S=[j-1]
,
T=[k]\setminus[j-1]
completes the proof in this case.

Next, let us take the case when
\mathcal{N}(\mu_{1},\Sigma_{1})
and
\mathcal{N}(\mu_{2},\Sigma_{2})
are mean-separated. WLOG, suppose
\langle\mu_{1},v\rangle\leqslant\langle\mu_{2},v\rangle\ldots\leqslant\langle\mu_{k},v\rangle
. Then, we know that
\langle\mu_{k}-\mu_{1},v\rangle\geqslant\Delta v^{\top}\Sigma_{i}v
. Thus, there must exist an
i
such that
\langle\mu_{i}-\mu_{i+1},v\rangle\geqslant\Delta v^{\top}\Sigma_{i}v/k
. Let
S=[i]
and
L=[k]\setminus S
. Applying Lemma
4.7
and arguing as in the previous case (and noting that
\kappa=\poly(k)
) completes the proof.

Finally, let us work with the case of relative Frobenius separation. Since
\|\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{k}^{1/2}\|\leqslant\poly(k)
, the hypothesis implies that
\left\lVert\Sigma_{1}-\Sigma_{2}\right\rVert_{F}\geqslant\Delta/\poly(k)
. Let
B=\Sigma_{1}-\Sigma_{2}
and let
A=B/\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{F}
.
WLOG, suppose
\langle\Sigma_{1},A\rangle\leqslant\ldots\langle\Sigma_{k},A\rangle
.
Then, since
\langle\Sigma_{k},A\rangle-\langle\Sigma_{1},A\rangle\geqslant\Delta/\poly(k)
, there must exist an
i
such that
\langle\Sigma_{i+1},A\rangle-\langle\Sigma_{i},A\rangle\geqslant\Delta/\poly(k)
. Let us now set
S=[i]
and
L=[k]\setminus S
.

Then, for every
i\in S
and
j\in L
, we must have:
\langle\Sigma_{j},A\rangle-\langle\Sigma_{i},A\rangle\geqslant\Delta/\poly(k)
.
Thus,
\left\lVert\Sigma_{j}-\Sigma_{i}\right\rVert_{F}\geqslant\Delta/\poly(k)
.
And thus,
\Delta/\poly(k)\leqslant\left\lVert\Sigma_{j}-\Sigma_{i}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{j}^{1/2}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{j}\Sigma_{i}^{-1/2}-I\right\rVert_{F}
. Rearranging and using the bound on
\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{j}^{1/2}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
yields that
\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{j}\Sigma_{i}^{-1/2}-I\right\rVert_{F}\geqslant\Delta/\poly(k)
.

A similar argument as in the two cases above now completes the proof.

∎

4.4
Proof of Lemma
4.6

We use
\E_{z}
as a shorthand for
\frac{1}{\alpha n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}
. We will write
\frac{1}{w_{r}n}\sum_{j\in X_{r}}z_{j}=z(X_{r})
.
Note that
z(X_{r})\in[0,1]
. And finally, we will write
z^{\prime}(X_{r})=\frac{1}{w_{r}n}\sum_{j\in X_{r}}z_{j}\bm{1}(x_{j}=y_{j})
– the version of
z(X_{r})
that only sums over non-outliers.

We will use the following technical facts in the proof:

Fact 4.8

(Lower Bounding Sums, Fact 4.19
[
BK20b
]
)
.

Let
A,B,C,D
be scalar-valued indeterminates. Then, for any
\tau>0
,

\left\{0\leqslant A,B\leqslant A+B\leqslant 1\right\}\cup\left\{0\leqslant C,D\right\}\cup\left\{C+D\geqslant\tau\right\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{35.73775pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-35.73775pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[35.73775pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-35.73775pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[35.73775pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{A,B,C,D}$}}}$}}}\left\{AC+BD\geqslant\tau AB\right\}\,.

Fact 4.9

(Cancellation within SoS, Lemma 9.2 in
[
BK20b
]
)
.

For indeterminate
a
and any
t\in\mathbb{N}
,

\left\{a^{2t}\leqslant 1\right\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{a}$}}}$}}}\left\{a\leqslant 1\right\}\,.

Lemma 4.10

(Lower-Bound on Variance of Degree 2 Polynomials)
.

Let
Q\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
. Then, for any
i,j\leqslant k
, and
z^{\prime}(X_{r})=\frac{1}{w_{r}n}\sum_{i\in X_{r}}z_{i}\bm{1}(x_{i}=y_{i})
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.10762pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.10762pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[8.10762pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4}$}}}\hskip-8.10762pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.10762pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{z}$}}}$}}}\Biggl\{z^{\prime}(X_{r})^{2}z^{\prime}(X_{r}^{\prime})^{2}\leqslant\frac{(32Ct)^{2t}}{(\E_{X_{r}}Q-\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}Q)^{2t}}

\displaystyle\Biggl(\frac{\alpha^{4}}{w_{r}^{2}w_{r^{\prime}}^{2}}\left(\E_{z}(Q-\E_{z}Q)^{2}\right)^{t}

\displaystyle+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{w_{r}^{2}}\left(\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}(Q-\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}Q)^{2}\right)^{t}+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{w_{r^{\prime}}^{2}}\left(\E_{X_{r}}(Q-\E_{X_{r}}Q)^{2}\right)^{t}\Biggr)\Biggr\}\,.

Proof.

Let
z_{i}^{\prime}=z_{i}\bm{1}(x_{i}=y_{i})
for every
i
.
Using the substitution rule and non-negativity constraints of the
z_{i}
’s, we have

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.10762pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.10762pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[8.10762pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4}$}}}\hskip-8.10762pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.10762pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{z}$}}}$}}}\Biggl\{\E_{z}(Q-\E_{z}Q)^{2t}&=\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}n^{2}}\sum_{i,j\leqslant n}z_{i}^{\prime}z_{j}^{\prime}\left(Q(x_{i}-x_{j})-\E_{z}Q\right)^{2t}\\
&\geqslant\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}n^{2}}\sum_{i,j\in X_{r}\text{ or }i,j\in X_{r^{\prime}}}z_{i}^{\prime}z_{j}^{\prime}\left(Q(x_{i}-x_{j})-\E_{z}Q\right)^{2t}\Biggl\}\end{split}

(4.8)

Using the SoS almost triangle inequality, we have

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.10762pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.10762pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[8.10762pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4}$}}}\hskip-8.10762pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.10762pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{z}$}}}$}}}\Biggl\{&\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}n^{2}}\sum_{i,j\in X_{r}\text{ or }i,j\in X_{r^{\prime}}}z_{i}^{\prime}z_{j}^{\prime}\left(Q(x_{i}-x_{j})-\E_{z}Q\right)^{2t}\\
&\geqslant\left(\frac{1}{2^{2t}}\right)\left(\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}n^{2}}\sum_{i,j\in X_{r}}z_{i}^{\prime}z_{j}^{\prime}\left(\E_{X_{r}}Q-\E_{z}Q\right)^{2t}-\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}n^{2}}\sum_{i,j\in X_{r}}z_{i}^{\prime}z_{j}^{\prime}\left(Q(x_{i}-x_{j})-\E_{X_{r}}Q\right)^{2t}\right)\\
&\hskip 14.45377pt+\left(\frac{1}{2^{2t}}\right)\left(\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}n^{2}}\sum_{i,j\in X_{r}}z_{i}^{\prime}z_{j}^{\prime}\left(\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}Q-\E_{z}Q\right)^{2t}-\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}n^{2}}\sum_{i,j\in X_{r^{\prime}}}z_{i}^{\prime}z_{j}^{\prime}\left(Q(x_{i}-x_{j})-\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}Q\right)^{2t}\right)\\
&=2^{-2t}\left((w_{r}/\alpha)^{2}z(X_{r})^{2}\left(\E_{X_{r}}Q-\E_{z}Q\right)^{2t}-\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}n^{2}}\sum_{i,j\in X_{r}}\left(Q(x_{i}-x_{j})-\E_{X_{r}}Q\right)^{2t}\right)\\
&\hskip 14.45377pt+2^{-2t}\left((w_{r^{\prime}}/\alpha)^{2}z(X_{r^{\prime}})^{2}\left(\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}Q-\E_{z}Q\right)^{2t}-\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}n^{2}}\sum_{i,j\in X_{r^{\prime}}}\left(Q(x_{i}-x_{j})-\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}Q\right)^{2t}\right)\Biggl\}\end{split}

(4.9)

Using Fact
4.8
, we can further simplify the above as follows:

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.10762pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.10762pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[8.10762pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4}$}}}\hskip-8.10762pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.10762pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{z}$}}}$}}}\Biggl\{\E_{z}(Q-\E_{z}Q)^{2t}&\geqslant 2^{-6t}\frac{w_{r}^{2}w_{r^{\prime}}^{2}}{\alpha^{4}}z^{\prime}(X_{r})^{2}z^{\prime}(X_{r^{\prime}})^{2}(\E_{X_{r}}Q-\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}Q)^{2t}\\
&-2^{-6t}(w_{r}/\alpha)^{2}\E_{X_{r}}(Q-\E_{X_{r}}Q)^{2t}-2^{-6t}(w_{r^{\prime}}/\alpha)^{2}\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}(Q-\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}Q)^{2t}\\
&\geqslant 2^{-6t}\frac{w_{r}^{2}w_{r^{\prime}}^{2}}{\alpha^{4}}z^{\prime}(X_{r})^{2}z^{\prime}(X_{r^{\prime}})^{2}(\E_{X_{r}}Q-\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}Q)^{2t}-(w_{r}/\alpha)^{2}(Ct)^{2t}\left(\E_{X_{r}}(Q-\E_{X_{r}}Q)^{2}\right)^{t}\\
&-(w_{r^{\prime}}/\alpha)^{2}(Ct)^{2t}\left(\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}(Q-\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}Q)^{2}\right)^{t}\Biggl\}\end{split}

(4.10)

where the last inequality follows from the Certifiable Hypercontractivity constraint (
\mathcal{A}_{4}
). Rearranging completes the proof.

∎

We can use the lemma above to obtain a simultaneous intersection bound guarantee when there are relative Frobenius separated components in the mixture.

Lemma 4.11
.

Suppose
\left\lVert\Sigma^{-1/2}(\Sigma_{r}-\Sigma_{r^{\prime}})\Sigma^{-1/2}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\geqslant 10^{8}\frac{C^{6}t^{4}}{{\beta}^{2/t}\alpha^{2}}
. Then, for
z^{\prime}(X_{r})=\frac{1}{\alpha n}\sum_{i\in X_{r}}z_{i}\cdot\bm{1}(y_{i}=x_{i})
for every
r
,

\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{w}$}}}$}}}\left\{z^{\prime}(X_{r})z^{\prime}(X_{r}^{\prime})\leqslant\beta\right\}\,.

Proof.

We work with the transformation
x_{i}\rightarrow\Sigma^{-1/2}x_{i}
. Let
\Sigma_{z}^{\prime}=\Sigma^{-1/2}\Sigma_{z}\Sigma^{-1/2}
,
\Sigma_{r}^{\prime}=\Sigma^{-1/2}\Sigma_{r}\Sigma^{-1/2}
and
\Sigma_{r^{\prime}}^{\prime}=\Sigma^{-1/2}\Sigma_{r^{\prime}}\Sigma^{-1/2}
be the transformed covariances. Note that transformation is only for the purpose of the argument - our constraint system does not depend on
\Sigma
.

Notice that
\left\lVert\Sigma_{r^{\prime}}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{w_{r}}
and
\left\lVert\Sigma_{r^{\prime}}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{w_{r^{\prime}}}
.

We now apply Lemma
4.10
with
Q=\Sigma_{r}^{\prime}-\Sigma_{r^{\prime}}^{\prime}
. Then, notice that
\E_{X_{r}}Q-\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}Q=\left\lVert\Sigma_{r}^{\prime}-\Sigma_{r^{\prime}}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}=\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}
. Then, we obtain:

\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{z}$}}}$}}}\Biggl\{z^{\prime}(X_{r})^{2}z^{\prime}(X_{r^{\prime}})^{2}\\
\leqslant\left(\frac{32Ct}{\E_{X_{r}}Q-\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}Q}\right)^{2t}\left(\frac{\alpha^{4}}{w_{r}^{2}w_{r^{\prime}}^{2}}\left(\E_{z}(Q-\E_{z}Q)^{2}\right)^{t}+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{w_{r}^{2}}\left(\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}(Q-\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}Q)^{2}\right)^{t}+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{w_{r^{\prime}}^{2}}\left(\E_{X_{r}}(Q-\E_{X_{r}}Q)^{2}\right)\right)^{t}\Biggr\}\,.

(4.11)

Since
X_{r}
and
X_{r^{\prime}}
have certifiably
C
-bounded variance polynomials for
C=4
(as a consequence of Condition
2.47
and Fact
2.45
followed by an application of Lemma
2.25
), we have:

\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\left\{\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}(Q-\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}Q)^{2}\leqslant{6\left\lVert{\Sigma_{r^{\prime}}^{\prime}}^{1/2}Q{\Sigma_{r^{\prime}}^{\prime}}^{1/2}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}}\leqslant\frac{6}{w_{r^{\prime}}^{2}}\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\right\}\;,

and

\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\left\{\E_{X_{r}}(Q-\E_{X_{r}}Q)^{2}\leqslant{6\left\lVert{\Sigma_{r}^{\prime}}^{1/2}Q{\Sigma_{r}^{\prime}}^{1/2}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}}\leqslant\frac{6}{w_{r}^{2}}\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\right\}\,.

Finally, using the bounded-variance constraints in
\mathcal{A}
, we have:

\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{16.6693pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-16.6693pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[16.6693pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4}$}}}\hskip-16.6693pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[16.6693pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q,z}$}}}$}}}\E(Q-\E_{z}Q)^{2}\leqslant\frac{6}{\alpha^{2}}\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\,.

Plugging these estimates back in (
4.11
) yields:

\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.10762pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.10762pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[8.10762pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4}$}}}\hskip-8.10762pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.10762pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{z}$}}}$}}}\left\{z^{\prime}(X_{r})^{2}z^{\prime}(X_{r^{\prime}})^{2}\leqslant\frac{(1000Ct)^{2t}}{\alpha^{2t}\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2t}}\right\}\,.

(4.12)

Plugging in the lower bound on
\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2t}
and applying Fact
4.9
completes the proof.
∎

We can use the above lemma to complete the proof of Lemma
4.6
:

Proof of Lemma
4.6
.

WLOG, assume that
\Sigma=I
.
Let
Q=\Sigma_{r}-\Sigma_{r^{\prime}}
and let
\bar{Q}=Q/\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}
.
Consider the numbers
v_{i}=\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma_{r}\cdot Q)
.
Then, we know that
\max_{i,j}|v_{i}-v_{j}|\geqslant\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}
.
Thus, there must exist a partition of
[k]=S\cup T
such that
|v_{i}-v_{j}|\geqslant\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}/k
whenever
i\in S
and
j\in T
.

Thus, for every
i\in S
and
j\in T
,
\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-\Sigma_{j}\right\rVert^{2}_{F}\geqslant\left\lVert Q\right\rVert^{2}_{F}/k^{2}=10^{8}\frac{C^{6}t^{4}}{{(\beta^{2/t}\alpha^{2})}}
.
We can now apply Lemma above to every
i\in S,j\in T
, observe that
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{7.98607pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-7.98607pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[7.98607pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4}$}}}\hskip-7.98607pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[7.98607pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}$}}}\left\{z(X_{r})z(X_{r^{\prime}})\leqslant z^{\prime}(X_{r})z^{\prime}(X_{r^{\prime}})+2\varepsilon/\alpha\right\}
, and add up the resulting inequalities to finish the proof.

∎

4.5
Special Case: Algorithm for Uniform and Bounded Mixing Weights

In this subsection, we obtain a polynomial time algorithm when the input mixture has weights that are bounded from below. This includes the case of uniform weights and when the minimum mixing weight is at least some function of
k
. At a high level, our algorithm partitions the sample into clusters as long as there is a pair of components separated in TV distance and given samples that are not clusterable, runs the tensor decomposition algorithm to list decode.
We then use standard robust tournament results to pick a hypothesis from the list.

Theorem 4.12

(Robustly Learning Mixtures of Gaussians with Bounded Weights)
.

Given
0<\varepsilon<\mathcal{O}_{k}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(1\right)
,
let
Y=\{y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{n}\}
be a multiset of
n\geqslant n_{0}=\poly_{k}\left(d,1/\varepsilon\right)

\varepsilon
-corrupted samples
from a
k
-mixture of Gaussians
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i\leqslant k}w_{i}\mathcal{N}\left(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i}\right)
, such that
w_{i}\geqslant\alpha
.
Then, there exists an algorithm with running time
\poly_{k}(n^{1/\alpha})\cdot\exp\left(\poly_{k}(1/\alpha,1/\varepsilon)\right)

such that with probability at least
9/10
it outputs a hypothesis
k
-mixture of Gaussians

\widehat{\mathcal{M}}=\sum_{i\leqslant k}\hat{w}_{i}\mathcal{N}\left(\hat{\mu_{i}},\hat{\Sigma}_{i}\right)

such that
d_{\textrm{TV}}\left(\mathcal{M},\widehat{\mathcal{M}}\right)=\mathcal{O}_{k}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon\right)
.

Briefly, our algorithm simply does the following:

1.

Clustering via SoS
: Guess a partition of the mixture such that each component in the partition is not clusterable. Let the resulting partition have
t\leqslant k
components. In parallel, try all possible ways to run Algorithm
4.4
repeatedly to obtain a partition of the samples,
\{\tilde{Y}_{j}\}_{j\in[t]}
into exactly
t
components. For each such partition repeat the following.

2.

Robust Isotropic Transformation
: Run the algorithm corresponding to Lemma
6.7
on each set
\tilde{Y}_{j}
to make the sample approximately isotropic.
Grid search for weights over
[\alpha,1/k]^{k}
with precision
\alpha
.

3.

List-Decoding via Tensor Decomposition
: Run Algorithm
3.2
on each
\tilde{Y}_{j}
. Concatenate the lists to obtain
\mathcal{L}
.

4.

Robust Tournament
: Run the tournament from Fact
2.52
over all the hypotheses in
\mathcal{L}
, and output the winning hypothesis.

Proof Sketch.

Setting
\Delta=(k^{k^{O(k)}})
, it follows from Theorem
4.2
that we obtain a partition of
Y
into
\{\tilde{Y}_{j}\}_{j\in[t]}
, for some
t\in[k]
such that
\tilde{Y}_{j}
has at most
\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(k\varepsilon/\alpha\right)
outliers,
\left(1-\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(k\varepsilon/\alpha\right)\right)
-fraction of samples from at least one component of the input mixture and the resulting samples are not
\Delta
-separated (see Definition
4.1
).
It then follows from Lemma
6.7
that the mean
\mu_{j}
and covariance
\Sigma_{j}
of
\tilde{Y}_{j}
satisfy : a)
\left\lVert\mu_{j}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\sqrt{\varepsilon}k^{1.5}/\alpha^{1.5}\right)
,
b)
\left(1-\sqrt{\varepsilon}k^{1.5}/\alpha^{1.5}\right)I\preceq\Sigma_{j}\preceq\left(1-\sqrt{\varepsilon}k^{1.5}/\alpha^{1.5}\right)I
,
and c)
\left\lVert\Sigma_{j}-I\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\sqrt{\varepsilon}k^{1.5}/\alpha^{1.5}\right)
.

Each component,
\tilde{Y}_{j}
, of the partition can have at most
k
components. Assuming these correspond to
\{w^{(j)}_{i},\mu^{(j)}_{i}\Sigma^{(j)}_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
, observe,
\sum_{i\in[k]}w^{(j)}_{i}\Sigma^{(j)}_{i}+w^{(j)}_{i}\mu^{(j)}_{i}\left(\mu^{(j)}_{i}\right)^{\top}\preceq\left(1+\sqrt{\varepsilon}k^{1.5}/\alpha^{1.5}\right)I
. Thus, we have that

\left\lVert\mu^{(j)}_{i}\right\rVert^{2}_{2}\leqslant\left(1+\sqrt{\varepsilon}k^{1.5}\right)/\alpha^{2.5}

and combined with not being
\Delta
-separated, it follows that for all
i^{\prime}\in[k]
,

\begin{split}\left\lVert\Sigma^{(j)}_{i^{\prime}}-I\right\rVert_{F}=\left\lVert\Sigma^{(j)}_{i^{\prime}}-\Sigma_{j}+\left(\Sigma_{j}-I\right)\right\rVert_{F}&\leqslant\left\lVert\Sigma^{(j)}_{i^{\prime}}-\sum_{i\in[k]}w^{(j)}_{i}\Sigma^{(j)}_{i}+\sum_{i\in[k]}w^{(j)}_{i}\mu^{(j)}_{i}\left(\mu^{(j)}_{i}\right)^{\top}\right\rVert_{F}+\left\lVert\Sigma_{j}-I\right\rVert_{F}\\
&\leqslant\left\lVert\sum_{i\in[k]}w^{(j)}_{i}\left(\Sigma^{(j)}_{i^{\prime}}-\Sigma^{(j)}_{i}\right)\right\rVert_{F}+\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(k^{1.5}/\alpha^{2.5}\right)\\
&\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\Delta/\alpha\right)\;.\end{split}

There are at most
\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(k^{k}\right)
ways in which we can partition the set of input points such that each resulting component is not partially clusterable. We run the algorithm in parallel for each one.
Then, for the correct iteration, we apply Theorem
3.1
to get a list
\mathcal{L}
of size
\exp(\poly_{k}(1/\alpha,1/\varepsilon))
such that it contains a hypothesis
\{\hat{w}^{(j)}_{i},\hat{\mu}^{(j)}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}^{(j)}_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
such that
\left|\hat{w}^{(j)}_{i}-w^{(j)}_{i}\right|\leqslant\alpha
,
\left\lVert\hat{\mu}^{(j)}_{i}-\mu^{(j)}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\mathcal{O}_{k}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon\right)
and
\left\lVert\hat{\Sigma}^{(j)}_{i}-\Sigma^{(j)}_{i}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\mathcal{O}_{k}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon\right)
. Since
\left(1-1/\Delta\right)I\preceq\Sigma^{(j)}_{i}
, it then follows from Lemma
6.5
that the hypothesis is
\mathcal{O}_{k}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon\right)
-close to the input in total variation distance.

Algorithm
4.4
is called at most
\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(k^{k}\right)
times, and along with the robust isotropic transformation, this requires
\poly_{k}\left(n^{1/\alpha},1/\varepsilon\right)
. The grid search contributes a multiplicative factor of
(1/\alpha)^{k}
. The tensor decomposition algorithm and robust hypothesis section
\poly_{k}(n^{1/\alpha})\cdot\exp\left(\poly_{k}(1/\alpha,1/\varepsilon)\right)
.
∎

5
Spectral Separation of Thin Components

In this section, we show how to efficiently separate a thin
component, if such a component exists, given sufficiently accurate
approximations to the component means and covariances.
This is an important step in our overall algorithm and is
required to obtain total variation distance guarantees.

Specifically, the main algorithmic result of this section is described
in the following lemma:

Lemma 5.1
.

There is a polynomial-time algorithm with the following properties:
Let
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}G_{i}
with
G_{i}=\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
be a
k
-mixture of Gaussians on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
and let
X
be a set of points in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfying Condition
2.47

with respect to
\mathcal{M}
for some parameters
(\gamma,t)
. The algorithm takes input parameters

\eta,\delta
, satisfying
0<\delta<\eta<1/(100k)
, and
Y
, an
\varepsilon
-corrupted version of
X
,
as well as candidate parameters
\{\hat{\mu}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}_{i}\}_{i\leqslant k}
. Then as long as

1.

\texttt{Cov}(\mathcal{M})\succeq I/2
,

2.

\|\mu_{i}-\hat{\mu}_{i}\|_{2}<\delta
and
\|\Sigma_{i}-\hat{\Sigma}_{i}\|_{F}<\delta
, for all
i\in[k]
, and

3.

there exists an
s\in[k]
such that
\Sigma_{s}
has an eigenvalue
<\eta
,

the algorithm outputs a partition of
Y
into
Y_{1}\cup Y_{2}

such that there is a non-trivial partition of
[k]
into
Q_{1}\cup Q_{2}
,
so that letting
\mathcal{M}_{j}
,
j\in\{1,2\}
, be proportional to

\sum_{i\in Q_{j}}w_{i}G_{i}
and
W_{j}=\sum_{i\in Q_{j}}w_{i}
,
then
Y_{j}
is an
((O(k^{2}\gamma)+\tilde{O}(\eta^{1/2k}))/{W}_{j})
-corruption of a set satisfying Condition
2.47
with respect to
\mathcal{M}_{j}

with parameters
(O(k\gamma/{W}_{j}),t)
.

The key component in the proof of Lemma
5.1
is the following lemma:

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}G_{i}
with
G_{i}=\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})

be a
k
-mixture of Gaussians in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\texttt{Cov}(\mathcal{M})\succeq I/2
. Suppose that, for some

0<\delta<1/(100k)
, we are given
\hat{\mu}_{i}
and
\hat{\Sigma}_{i}
satisfying

\|\mu_{i}-\hat{\mu}_{i}\|_{2}<\delta
and
\|\Sigma_{i}-\hat{\Sigma}_{i}\|_{F}<\delta
, for all
i\in[k]
.
Suppose furthermore that for some
\eta>\delta
, there is a
\Sigma_{s}
,
s\in[k]
,
with an eigenvalue less than
\eta
. There exists a computationally efficient algorithm that takes
inputs
\eta
,
\delta
,
\hat{\mu}_{i}
,
\hat{\Sigma}_{i}
, and computes a function
F:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\{0,1\}
such that:

1.

For each
i\in[k]
,
F(G_{i})
returns the same value in
\{0,1\}
with probability
at least
1-\tilde{O}_{k}(\eta^{1/(2k)})
. We define the most likely value of
F(G_{i})
to be this value.

2.

There exist
i,j\in[k]
such that the most likely values of
F(G_{i})
and

F(G_{j})
are different.

Furthermore,
F(x)
can be chosen to be of the form
f(v\cdot x)
, for some
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
and
f:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
is an
O(k)
-piecewise constant function.

Given Lemma
5.2
, it is easy to finish the proof of Lemma
5.1
.

Proof of Lemma
5.1
.

We simply take the candidate parameters, obtain
F
from Lemma
5.2
,
and partition
Y=Y_{1}\cup Y_{2}
, so that
F
is constant on both
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
. We let
Q_{j}
be the set of
i
so that
F(G_{i})
returns the value
j-1
with large probability. Letting the partition of
X
for Condition
2.47
be
X=X_{1}\cup\ldots\cup X_{k}
, we let
X^{j}=\bigcup_{i\in Q_{j}}X_{i}
. Lemma
2.50
shows that the
X^{j}
satisfy the appropriate conditions for
\mathcal{M}_{j}
.
It remains to prove that
Y_{j}
equals
X^{j}
with a sufficiently small rate of corruptions.
The fraction of points misclassified by
F
equals
\varepsilon
(the fraction of outliers in the sample
Y
)
plus the misclassification error of
F
. We note that given the form of
F
and the fact that
the uncorrupted samples in
Y
satisfy Condition
2.47
, the fraction of misclassified samples from each component
i
is at most the probability that a random sample from
G_{i}
gets misclassified (at most
\tilde{O}_{k}(\eta^{1/(2k)})
by Lemma
5.2
) plus
O(k\gamma)
. Summing this over components, gives Lemma
5.1
.
∎

Let us now describe the algorithm to prove Lemma
5.2
(and evaluate
F
),
which is given in pseudocode below (Algorithm
5.3
).

Algorithm 5.3

(Algorithm for Spectrally Separating Thin Components)
.

Input:

Estimated parameters
\left\{\hat{\mu}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}_{i}\right\}_{i\leqslant k}
, parameters
\eta,\delta
.

Output:

A function
F:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
.

Operation:

1.

Find a unit-norm direction
v
such that there exists
s\in[k]
,
v^{T}\hat{\Sigma}_{s}v<2\eta
.

2.

Compute
(v^{T}\hat{\Sigma}_{i}v)
for all
i\in[k]
.

(a)

If there exists
j\in[k]
such that
(v^{T}\hat{\Sigma}_{j}v)>\sqrt{\eta}
,
find a
t
such that
\sqrt{\eta}>t>2\eta
and there is no
j\in[k]
with

t<v^{T}\hat{\Sigma}_{j}v<t\,\Omega(\eta^{-1/(2k)})
. Set
F(x)=1
if there is an
i
such that
|v\cdot(x-\hat{\mu}_{i})|<\sqrt{t}\log(1/\eta)
and
0
otherwise.

(b)

Otherwise, compute
v\cdot\hat{\mu}_{i}
for all
i\in[k]
. Find a
t

between the minimum and the maximum of
v\cdot\hat{\mu}_{i}
such that
there is no
v\cdot\hat{\mu}_{i}
within
1/(20k)
of
t
.
Set
F(x)=1
if
v\cdot x>t
and
0
otherwise.

Proof of Lemma
5.2
.

Let
v
be a unit vector and
s\in[k]
such that
v^{T}\hat{\Sigma}_{s}v<2\eta
.
By assumption, we have that
\mbox{\bf Var}[v\cdot\mathcal{M}]\geqslant 1/2
.
Furthermore,

\mbox{\bf Var}[v\cdot\mathcal{M}]=\sum_{i}w_{i}(v^{T}\Sigma_{i}v)+\sum_{i}w_{i}(v\cdot(\mu_{i}-\mu))^{2}\leqslant\sum_{i}w_{i}(v^{T}\Sigma_{i}v)+\sum w_{i}(v\cdot(\mu_{i}-\mu_{s}))^{2}\;,

where
\mu
is the mean of
\mathcal{M}
. This means that either
there exists
j\in[k]
such that
(v^{T}\Sigma_{j}v)>1/4
, or there exists

j\in[k]
such that
|v\cdot(\mu_{j}-\mu_{s})|>1/4
.
Since we have approximations of these quantities to order
\delta
,
we have that there is
j\in[k]
such that
(v^{T}\hat{\Sigma}_{j}v)>1/10

or that there is
j\in[k]
with
|v\cdot(\hat{\mu}_{j}-\hat{\mu}_{s})|>1/10
.

We first consider the case that there is a
j\in[k]
such that
(v^{T}\hat{\Sigma}_{j}v)>\sqrt{\eta}
.
Since there is a
j\in[k]
with
(v^{T}\hat{\Sigma}_{j}v)>\sqrt{\eta}
and
another
s\in[k]
with
(v^{T}\hat{\Sigma}_{s}v)<2\eta
,
there must be some
\sqrt{\eta}>t>2\eta
such that there is no
j\in[k]

with
t<v^{T}\hat{\Sigma}_{j}v<t\,\Omega(\eta^{-1/(2k)})
.
Otherwise, there must be at least one
\hat{\Sigma}_{i}
in each
2\eta\leqslant\Omega(\eta^{-1/(2k)})^{i}\leqslant\sqrt{\eta}
,
where we need more than
k
components.

For a given
x
, we define
F(x)
to be
1
if there exists
i
such that

|v\cdot(x-\hat{\mu}_{i})|<\sqrt{t}\log(1/\eta)
, and
F(x)=0
otherwise.

To show that this works, we note that for all
i\in[k]
,
if
v^{T}\hat{\Sigma}_{i}v\leqslant t
, then
\mbox{\bf Var}[v\cdot G_{i}]\leqslant t+\delta
,
and since
|v\cdot(\mu_{i}-\hat{\mu}_{i})|<\delta
,
by the Gaussian tail bound, we have that

\ProbOp_{x\sim G_{i}}\left(|x-\mu_{i}|\geqslant(\sqrt{t}\log(1/\eta)-\delta)\right)\leqslant\exp\left(-\frac{(\sqrt{t}\log(1/\eta)-\delta)^{2}}{2(t+\delta)}\right)=O(\eta)\;.

Thus, all but an
O(\eta)
-fraction of the samples of
G_{i}
have
F(x)=1
.

On the other hand, for components
i
with

v^{T}\hat{\Sigma}_{i}v\gg t\eta^{-1/(2k)}
, we have that

\mbox{\bf Var}[v\cdot G_{i}]\gg t\eta^{-1/(2k)}
.
Then, the density of
G_{i}
is at most
1/\sqrt{2\pi t\eta^{-1/(2k)}}
.
So, the probability that a sample from
v\cdot G_{i}
lies in any interval of length
2\sqrt{t}\log(1/\eta)
is at most

\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi t\eta^{-1/(2k)}}}2\sqrt{t}\log(1/\eta)=\tilde{O}(\eta^{1/(4k)})\;.

Since there are
k
such intervals, the probability that
F(x)
is
1
when
x
is drawn from
G_{i}

is at most
\tilde{O}_{k}(\eta^{1/(4k)})
. This completes our proof of point (1), and point (2)
follows from the fact that we know of component
G_{j}
in one class and
G_{s}
in the other class.

We next consider the case where
(v^{T}\hat{\Sigma}_{j}v)\leqslant\sqrt{\eta}
for all

j\in[k]
, and where
|v\cdot(\hat{\mu}_{j}-\hat{\mu}_{s})|>1/10
for some
j\in[k]
.
Then we can find some
t
between
v\cdot\hat{\mu}_{j}
and
v\cdot\hat{\mu}_{s}
such
that no
v\cdot\hat{\mu}_{i}
is within
1/(20k)
of
t
.
In this case, we define
F(x)
be
1
if
v\cdot x>t
and
0
otherwise.
To show part (1), first consider
i\in[k]
such that
v\cdot\hat{\mu}_{i}<t-1/(20k)
.
Then we have that
v\cdot\mu_{i}<t-1/(30k)
. Furthermore,

\mbox{\bf Var}[v\cdot G_{j}]\leqslant\delta+\sqrt{\eta}
. Therefore, the probability that
v\cdot G_{i}>t

is at most
\exp(-\Omega_{k}((\delta+\sqrt{\eta})^{-2}))
, which is sufficient.

A similar argument holds in the other direction for
i\in[k]
such that
v\cdot\hat{\mu}_{i}>t+1/(20k)
,
and statement (2) holds because we know that there are both kinds of components.
This completes the proof.
∎

6
Robust Proper Learning: Proof of Theorem
1.4

In this section, we show how to combine the partial clustering, tensor decomposition, and recursive clustering algorithms to establish
our main result. The main theorem we prove is as follows:

Theorem 6.1

(Robustly Learning
k
-Mixtures of Arbitrary Gaussians)
.

Given
0<\varepsilon<1/k^{k^{O(k^{2})}}
and a multiset
Y=\{y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{n}\}
of
n
i.i.d.
samples from a distribution
F
such that
d_{\mathsf{TV}}(F,\mathcal{M})\leqslant\varepsilon
,
for an unknown
k
-mixture of Gaussians
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i\leqslant k}w_{i}\mathcal{N}\left(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i}\right)
,
where
n\geqslant n_{0}=d^{O(k)}/\poly(\varepsilon)
,
Algorithm
6.3
runs
in time
n^{O(1)}\exp\left(O(k)/\varepsilon^{2}\right)

and with probability at least
0.99
outputs a hypothesis
k
-mixture of Gaussians

\widehat{\mathcal{M}}=\sum_{i\leqslant k}\hat{w}_{i}\mathcal{N}\left(\hat{\mu_{i}},\hat{\Sigma}_{i}\right)

such that
d_{\textrm{TV}}\left(\mathcal{M},\widehat{\mathcal{M}}\right)=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{c_{k}}\right)
,
with
c_{k}=1/(100^{k}C^{(k+1)!}k!\mathrm{sf}(k+1))
, where
C>0
is a universal constant
and
\mathrm{sf}(k)=\Pi_{i\in[k]}(k-i)!
is the super-factorial function.

As an immediate corollary, we obtain the following:

Corollary 6.2

(Robustly Learning
k
-Mixtures of Gaussians in Polynomial Time)
.

Given
0<\varepsilon<1/\exp\left(k^{k^{O(k^{2})}}\right)
, and a
multiset
Y=\{y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{n}\}
of
n
i.i.d. samples from a distribution
F

such that
d_{\mathsf{TV}}(F,\mathcal{M})\leqslant\varepsilon
, for an unknown
k
-mixture of Gaussians

\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i\leqslant k}w_{i}\mathcal{N}\left(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i}\right)
,
where
n\geqslant n_{0}=d^{O(k)}\log^{O(1)}(1/\varepsilon)
,
there exists an algorithm that runs in time
\poly_{k}(n,1/\varepsilon)

and with probability at least
0.99
outputs a
k
-mixture of Gaussians

\widehat{\mathcal{M}}=\sum_{i\leqslant k}\hat{w}_{i}\mathcal{N}\left(\hat{\mu_{i}},\hat{\Sigma}_{i}\right)

such that
d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathcal{M},\widehat{\mathcal{M}}\right)=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\left(1/\log(1/\varepsilon)\right)^{1/\left(k^{O(k^{2})}\right)}\right)
.

The corollary follows by running Algorithm
6.3
with
\varepsilon\leftarrow\sqrt{1/\log(1/\varepsilon)}

and applying Theorem
6.1
.

The algorithm establishing Theorem
6.1
is given in pseudocode below. Algorithm
6.4
takes as input a corrupted sample from a
k
-mixture of Gaussians
and outputs a set of
k
mixing weights, means, and covariances,
such that the resulting mixture is close to the input mixture in total variation distance
with non-negligible probability.
Algorithm
6.3
simply runs Algorithm
6.4
many
times to create a small list of candidate hypotheses (consisting of mixing weights, means, and covariances), and finally runs a robust tournament to outputs a winner. This boosts the probability of success to at least
0.99
.

Algorithm 6.3

(Algorithm for Robustly Learning Arbitrary GMMs)
.

Input:

An outlier parameter
\varepsilon>0
and a component-number parameter
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
An
\varepsilon
-corrupted sample
Y=\{y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{n}\}
from a
k
-mixture of Gaussians

\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i\in[k]}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
.

Parameters:

Let
c_{k}=1/(100^{k}C^{(k+1)!}\mathrm{sf}(k+1)k!)
be a scalar function of
k
, where
\textrm{sf}(k)=\prod_{i\in[k]}(k-1)!
and
C
is a sufficiently large constant.

Output:

A set of parameters
\{(\hat{w}_{i},\hat{\mu}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}_{i})\}_{i\in[k]}
, such that with probability at least
0.99
the mixture

\widehat{\mathcal{M}}=\sum_{i\in[k]}\hat{w}_{i}\mathcal{N}(\hat{\mu}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}_{i})
is
\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{c_{k}}\right)
-close in total variation distance to
\mathcal{M}
.

Operation:

1.

Let
\mathcal{L}=\{\phi\}
be an empty list. Repeat the following
\exp\left(O(k)/\varepsilon^{2}\right)
times :

(a)

Run Algorithm
6.4
with input
Y
, fraction of outliers
\varepsilon
, and number of components
k
.
Let the resulting output be a set of
k
mixing weights, means and covariances, denoted by

\left\{(\hat{w}_{i},\hat{\mu}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}_{i})\right\}_{i\in[k]}
.
Add
\left\{(\hat{w}_{i},\hat{\mu}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}_{i})\right\}_{i\in[k]}
to
\mathcal{L}
.

2.

Run the robust tournament from Fact
2.52
over all the hypotheses in

\mathcal{L}
. Output the winning hypothesis, denoted by

\{(\hat{w}_{i},\hat{\mu}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}_{i})\}_{i\in[k]}
.

Algorithm 6.4

(Cluster or List-Decode)
.

Input:

An outlier parameter
0<\varepsilon<1
and a component-number parameter
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
An
\varepsilon
-corrupted version
Y=\{y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{n}\}
of
X
,
where
X
is a set of
n
samples from a
k
-mixture of Gaussians

\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i\in[k]}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
such that

X
satisfies Condition
2.47
with respect to
\mathcal{M}

with parameters
(\varepsilon d^{-8k}k^{-C^{\prime}k},8k+48)
, where
C^{\prime}>0
is a sufficiently large constant.

Parameters:

Let
c_{k}=1/(100^{k}C^{(k+1)!}\mathrm{sf}(k+1)k!)
be a scalar function of
k
,
where
\textrm{sf}(k)=\prod_{i\in[k]}(k-1)!
and
C
is a sufficiently large constant.

Output:

A set of parameters
\{(\hat{w}_{i},\hat{\mu}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}_{i})\}_{i\in[k]}
such that with probability at least

\exp\left(-O(k)/\varepsilon^{2}\right)
,

d_{\textrm{TV}}\left(\sum_{i\in[k]}\hat{w}_{i}\mathcal{N}(\hat{\mu}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}_{i}),\mathcal{M}\right)\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{c_{k}}\right)
.

Operation:

1.

Treat Light Component as Noise
: If
k=0
, ABORT. With probability
1/2
, run Algorithm
6.4
on samples
Y
, with fraction of outliers
\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{1/(10C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}
and number of components
k-1
. Return the resulting set of estimated parameters,
\{(\hat{w}_{i},\hat{\mu}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}_{i})\}_{i\in[k-1]}
, appended with
(0,0,I)
. Else, do the following:

// We guess whether the event that the minimum mixing weight
\alpha
is at least
\varepsilon^{1/(10C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}

// holds. If it does not, we proceed with the algorithm. Else, we treat the
smallest weight

// component as noise and recurse with
k-1
components.

2.

Robust Isotropic Transformation
: With probability
0.5
, run the algorithm corresponding to
Lemma
6.7
on the samples
Y
, and let
\hat{\mu},\hat{\Sigma}
be the robust estimates
of the mean and covariance. If
k=1
, return
\left(\hat{w}=1,\hat{\mu},\hat{\Sigma}\right)
. Else, compute
\hat{U}\hat{\Lambda}\hat{U}^{\top}
, the eigendecomposition of
\Sigma
, and for all
i\in[n]
, apply the affine transformation
y_{i}\to\hat{U}^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}^{\dagger/2}\left(y_{i}-\hat{\mu}\right)
.

// The resulting estimates
\hat{\mu},\hat{\Sigma}
satisfy Lemma
6.7
, and the uncorrupted samples are

// effectively drawn from a nearly isotropic
k
-mixture.

3.

With probability
1/2
, run either
(a)
or
(b)
in the following:

(a)

Partial Clustering via SoS
: Run Algorithm
4.4
with outlier parameter
\varepsilon
and accuracy parameter
\varepsilon^{1/(5C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}
. Let
Y_{1}
,
Y_{2}
be the partition returned. Guess the number of components in
Y_{1}
to be some
k_{1}\in[k-1]
uniformly at random. Run Algorithm
6.4
with input
Y_{1}
, fraction of outliers
\varepsilon^{1/(10c^{k+1}(k+1)!)}
, and number of components
k_{1}
, and let
\Big\{(\hat{w}^{(1)}_{i},\hat{\mu}^{(1)}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}^{(1)}_{i})\Big\}_{i\in[k_{1}]}
be the resulting output. Similarly, run Algorithm
6.4
with input
Y_{2}
, fraction of outliers
\varepsilon^{1/(10c^{k+1}(k+1)!)}
, and number of components
k-k_{1}
, and let
\Big\{(\hat{w}^{(2)}_{i},\hat{\mu}^{(2)}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}^{(2)}_{i})\Big\}_{i\in[k-k_{1}]}
be the resulting output.
Output the set
\Big\{(\hat{w}^{(1)}_{i}|Y_{1}|/|Y|,\hat{\mu}^{(1)}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}^{(1)}_{i})\Big\}_{i\in[k_{1}]}\cup\Big\{(\hat{w}^{(2)}_{i}|Y_{2}|/|Y|,\mu^{(2)}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}^{(2)}_{i})\Big\}_{i\in[k-k_{1}]}
.

// When the mixture is covariance separated, the preconditions of Theorem
4.3

are

// satisfied (see Lemma
6.8
). The partition is non-trivial, and the fraction of outliers

// increases from
\varepsilon\to\varepsilon^{1/(10c^{k+1}(k+1)!)}
.

(b)

List-Decoding via Tensor Decomposition
: Run Algorithm
3.2

and let
L
be the resulting list of hypotheses such that each hypothesis is a set of parameters
\{(\hat{\mu}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}_{i})\}_{i\in[k]}
.
Let
\tau=\Theta\left(\varepsilon^{1/(40C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)
be an eigenvalue threshold.
Select a hypothesis,
\{(\hat{\mu}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}_{i})\}_{i\in[k]}\in L
uniformly at random.

// Conditioned on not being covariance separated, we satisfy the preconditions of

// Theorem
3.1
(see Lemma
6.9
).
The output is a list that contains
\{\hat{\mu}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}

// such that for all
i\in[k]
,
\left\lVert\hat{\mu}_{i}-\mu_{i}\right\rVert_{2}=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{1/(20C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)
and

//
\left\lVert\hat{\Sigma}_{i}-\Sigma_{i}\right\rVert_{F}=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{1/(20C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)
.

i.

Large Eigenvalues
: If for all
i\in[k]
,
\hat{\Sigma}_{i}\succeq\tau I
,
sample
\hat{w}_{i}
from
[0,1]
uniformly at random such that
\sum_{i}\hat{w}_{i}=(1\pm k\varepsilon)
.
Return

\Big\{\left(\hat{w}_{i},\hat{U}\hat{\Lambda}^{1/2}\hat{U}^{\top}\hat{\mu}_{i}+\hat{\mu},\hat{U}\hat{\Lambda}^{1/2}\hat{U}^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}_{i}\hat{U}\hat{\Lambda}^{1/2}\hat{U}^{\top}\right)\Big\}_{i\in[k]}
.

// If all estimated covariances have all eigenvalues larger than
\tau
, the recursion

// bottoms out and the hypothesis is returned.

ii.

Spectral Separation of Thin Components
: Else,
\exists v,i
s.t.
v^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}_{i}v\leqslant\tau
.
Run the algorithm corresponding to Lemma
5.1
with input
Y
, parameter estimates
\{(\hat{\mu}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}_{i})\}_{i\in[k]}
and threshold
\tau
. Let
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
be the resulting partition.

// Use small eigenvalue directions to partition the points.

A.

If
\min(|Y_{1}|,|Y_{2}|)<\varepsilon^{1/(400kC^{k+1}(k+1)!)}n
, run Algorithm
6.4
with input
Y
, fraction of outliers
2\varepsilon^{1/(400kC^{k+1}(k+1)!)}
and number of components being
k-1
, and let
\Big\{(\hat{w}^{(1)}_{i},\hat{\mu}^{(1)}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}^{(1)}_{i})\Big\}_{i\in[k_{1}]}
be the resulting output. Output the resulting hypothesis
\Big\{(\hat{w}_{i},\hat{U}\hat{\Lambda}^{1/2}\hat{U}^{\top}\hat{\mu}_{i}+\hat{\mu},\hat{U}\hat{\Lambda}^{1/2}\hat{U}^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}_{i}\hat{U}\hat{\Lambda}^{1/2}\hat{U}^{\top})\Big\}_{i\in[k-1]}\cup(0,0,I)
.

B.

Else, select
k_{1}\in[k-1]
uniformly at random. Run Algorithm
6.4
with input
Y_{1}
, fraction of outliers
\varepsilon^{1/(100kC^{k+1}(k+1)!)}
and number of components being
k_{1}
. Similarly, run Algorithm
6.4
with input
Y_{2}
, fraction of outliers
\varepsilon^{1/(100kC^{k+1}(k+1)!)}
and number of components
k-k_{1}
, and let
\Big\{(\hat{w}^{(2)}_{i},\hat{\mu}^{(2)}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}^{(2)}_{i})\Big\}_{i\in[k-k_{1}]}
be the resulting output. Output the set
\Big\{(\hat{w}^{(1)}_{i}|Y_{1}|/|Y|,\hat{U}\hat{\Lambda}^{1/2}\hat{U}^{\top}\hat{\mu}^{(1)}_{i}+\hat{\mu},\hat{U}\hat{\Lambda}^{1/2}\hat{U}^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}^{(1)}_{i}\hat{U}\hat{\Lambda}^{1/2}\hat{U}^{\top})\Big\}_{i\in[k_{1}]}\cup\Big\{(\hat{w}^{(2)}_{i}|Y_{2}|/|Y|,\hat{U}\hat{\Lambda}^{1/2}\hat{U}^{\top}\mu^{(2)}_{i}+\hat{\mu},\hat{U}\hat{\Lambda}^{1/2}\hat{U}^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}^{(2)}_{i}\hat{U}\hat{\Lambda}^{1/2}\hat{U}^{\top})\Big\}_{i\in[k-k_{1}]}
.

6.1
Analysis of Algorithm
6.3

To prove Theorem
6.1
, we will require the following intermediate results.
We defer some proofs in this subsection to Appendix
A
.

We use the following lemma to relate the Frobenius distance of covariances to the total variation distance between two Gaussians,
when the eigenvalues of the covariances are bounded below.

Lemma 6.5

(Frobenius Distance to TV Distance)
.

Suppose
