\mathcal{N}(\mu_{1},\Sigma_{1}),\mathcal{N}(\mu_{2},\Sigma_{2})
are Gaussians with
\left\|\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}\right\|_{2}\leqslant\delta
and

\left\|\Sigma_{1}-\Sigma_{2}\right\|_{F}\leqslant\delta
. If the eigenvalues of
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
are at least
\lambda>0
, then

d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{N}(\mu_{1},\Sigma_{1}),\mathcal{N}(\mu_{2},\Sigma_{2}))=O(\delta/\lambda)
.

We start by showing that when Condition
2.47
holds, the uniform distribution on a
(1-\varepsilon)
-fraction of the points is certifiably hypercontractive.

Lemma 6.6

(Component Moments to Mixture Moments)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i\in[k]}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
be a
k
-mixture with mean
\mu
and covariance
\Sigma
such that
w_{i}\geqslant\alpha
,
for some
0<\alpha<1
, and for all
i,j\in[k]
,
\left\lVert\Sigma^{\dagger/2}\left(\Sigma_{i}-\Sigma_{j}\right)\Sigma^{\dagger/2}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant 1/\sqrt{\alpha}
.
Let
X
be a multiset of
n
samples satisfying Condition
2.47

with respect to
\mathcal{M}
with parameters
(\gamma,t)
, for
0<\gamma<(dk/\alpha)^{-ct}
,
for a sufficiently large constant
c
, and
t\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\mathcal{D}
be the uniform distribution over
X
.
Then,
\mathcal{D}
is
2t
-certifiably
(c/\alpha)
-hypercontractive and for
d\times d
-matrix-valued indeterminate
Q
,
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\left\{\E_{\mathcal{M}}\left(x^{\top}Qx-\E_{\mathcal{M}}x^{\top}Qx\right)^{2}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(1/\alpha\right)\left\lVert\Sigma^{1/2}Q\Sigma^{1/2}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\right\}
.

Next, we show how to robustly estimate the mean and covariance of an
\varepsilon
-corrupted set of samples satisfying Condition
2.47
when the mixture is not partially clusterable, and make the inliers nearly isotropic.

Lemma 6.7

(Robust Isotropic Transformation)
.

Given
0<\varepsilon<1
, and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\alpha=\varepsilon^{1/(10C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}
.
Let
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}G_{i}
with
G_{i}=\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
be a
k
-mixture of Gaussians with
w_{i}\geqslant\alpha
for all
i\in[k]
,
and let
\mu
and
\Sigma
be the mean and covariance of
\mathcal{M}
such that
r=\rank(\Sigma)
and for all
i,j\in[k]
,
\left\lVert\Sigma^{\dagger/2}\left(\Sigma_{i}-\Sigma_{j}\right)\Sigma^{\dagger/2}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant 1/\sqrt{\alpha}
.
Let
X
be a set of points satisfying Condition
2.47

with respect to
\mathcal{M}
for some parameters
(\gamma,t)
.
Given a set
Y
, an
\varepsilon
-corrupted version of
X
, of size
n\geqslant n_{0}=d^{O(1)}
,
there exists an algorithm that takes
Y
as input and in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
outputs
estimators
\hat{\mu}
and
\hat{\Sigma}
such that

\hat{\Sigma}=\hat{U}\hat{\Lambda}\hat{U}^{\top}
is the eigenvalue decomposition,
where
\hat{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r}
has orthonormal columns and
\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
is a diagonal matrix. Further, we can obtain
n
samples
Y^{\prime}
by applying the affine transformation

y_{i}\to\hat{U}^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}^{\dagger/2}\left(y_{i}-\hat{\mu}\right)
to each sample,
such that a
(1-\varepsilon)
-fraction have mean
\mu^{\prime}
and covariance
\Sigma^{\prime}
satisfying

1.

\left\lVert\mu^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\left(1+\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}k}{\alpha}\right)\sqrt{\varepsilon/\alpha}\right)
,

2.

\left(\frac{1}{1+(k\sqrt{\varepsilon}/\alpha)}\right)I_{r}\preceq\Sigma^{\prime}\preceq\left(\frac{1}{1-(k\sqrt{\varepsilon}/\alpha)}\right)I_{r}
,

3.

\left\lVert\Sigma^{\prime}-I_{r}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\sqrt{\varepsilon}k/\alpha\right)
,

where
I_{r}
is the
r
-dimensional Identity matrix,
and the remaining points are arbitrary.
Let
X^{\prime}
be the set obtained by
\hat{U}^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}^{\dagger/2}\left(x_{i}-\hat{\mu}\right)
.
Then,
X^{\prime}
satisfies Condition
2.47
with respect to

\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}\mathcal{N}\left(\hat{U}^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}^{\dagger/2}(\mu_{i}-\hat{\mu}),\hat{U}^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}^{\dagger/2}\Sigma_{i}\hat{\Sigma}^{\dagger/2}\hat{U}\right)

and parameters
(\gamma,t)
, and
Y^{\prime}
is an
\varepsilon
-corruption of
X^{\prime}
.

Proof.

For any
t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
, it follows from Corollary
2.35

that
\mathcal{M}
has
2t^{\prime}
-certifiably
(4/\alpha)
-hypercontractive degree-
2
polynomials,
since
w_{i}\geqslant\alpha
for all
i
.
Next, Lemma
6.6
implies that the uniform distribution over
X

also has
2t^{\prime}
-certifiably
(8/\alpha)
-hypercontractive degree-
2
polynomials
and for
d\times d
-matrix-valued indeterminate
Q
,

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\left\{\E_{\mathcal{M}}\left(x^{\top}Qx-\E_{\mathcal{M}}x^{\top}Qx\right)^{2}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(1/\alpha\right)\left\lVert\Sigma^{1/2}Q\Sigma^{1/2}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\right\}.

Then, it follows from Fact
2.38
that if
\frac{16}{\alpha}t^{\prime}\varepsilon^{1-4/t^{\prime}}\ll 1
, there exists an algorithm that runs in time
n^{O(t^{\prime})}
and outputs estimates
\hat{\mu}
and
\hat{\Sigma}
satisfying:

1.

\left\lVert\Sigma^{\dagger/2}(\mu-\hat{\mu})\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant O(t^{\prime}/\alpha)^{1/2}\varepsilon^{1-1/t^{\prime}}
,

2.

\left(1-(k/\alpha)\varepsilon^{1-2/t^{\prime}}\right)\Sigma\preceq\hat{\Sigma}\preceq\left(1+(k/\alpha)\varepsilon^{1-2/t^{\prime}}\right)\Sigma
and,

3.

\left\lVert\Sigma^{\dagger/2}\left(\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma\right)\Sigma^{\dagger/2}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant(t^{\prime}/\alpha)O(\varepsilon^{1-1/t^{\prime}})
.

Setting
t^{\prime}=2
, compute
\hat{\Sigma}=\hat{U}\hat{\Lambda}\hat{U}^{\top}
, the eigendecomposition of
\hat{\Sigma}
,
such that
\hat{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r}
has orthonormal columns, where
r\leqslant d
is the rank of
\hat{\Sigma}
and

\hat{\Lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
is a diagonal matrix. Similarly, let
\Sigma=U\Lambda U^{\top}
be the
eigendecomposition of
\Sigma
. We apply the affine transformation
y_{i}\to\hat{U}^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}^{\dagger/2}\left(y_{i}-\hat{\mu}\right)

to each sample and thus we can assume throughout the rest of our argument
that we have access to
\varepsilon
-corrupted samples from a
k
-mixture of Gaussians
with mean
\mu^{\prime}=\hat{U}^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}^{\dagger/2}(\mu-\hat{\mu})

and covariance
\Sigma^{\prime}=\hat{U}^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}^{\dagger/2}\Sigma\hat{\Sigma}^{\dagger/2}\hat{U}
.
Then, we have that

\begin{split}\left\lVert\mu^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2}=\left\lVert\hat{U}^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}^{\dagger/2}(\mu-\hat{\mu})\right\rVert_{2}&\leqslant\left\lVert\hat{U}^{\top}\right\rVert_{\textrm{op}}\left\lVert\hat{\Sigma}^{\dagger/2}(\mu-\hat{\mu})\right\rVert_{2}\\
&\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\left(1+\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}k}{\alpha}\right)\sqrt{\varepsilon/\alpha}\right)\;,\end{split}

where the last inequality follows from (
1
) and (
2
).
It also follows from (
2
) that

\left(\frac{1}{1+(k\sqrt{\varepsilon}/\alpha)}\right)\hat{\Sigma}\preceq\Sigma\preceq\left(\frac{1}{1-(k\sqrt{\varepsilon}/\alpha)}\right)\hat{\Sigma}\;.

(6.1)

Multiplying out (
6.1
) with
\hat{U}^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}^{\dagger/2}
on the left
and
\hat{\Sigma}^{\dagger/2}\hat{U}
on the right, we have

\begin{split}\left(\frac{1}{1+(k\sqrt{\varepsilon}/\alpha)}\right)\hat{U}^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}^{\dagger/2}\hat{\Sigma}\hat{\Sigma}^{\dagger/2}\hat{U}\preceq\Sigma^{\prime}\preceq\left(\frac{1}{1-(k\sqrt{\varepsilon}/\alpha)}\right)\hat{U}^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}^{\dagger/2}\hat{\Sigma}\hat{\Sigma}^{\dagger/2}\hat{U}\;.\end{split}

Observe that (
2
) implies that the rank of
\hat{\Sigma}
and
\Sigma
is the same,
and thus
\hat{U}^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}^{\dagger/2}\hat{\Sigma}\hat{\Sigma}^{\dagger/2}\hat{U}=I_{r}
,
where
I_{r}
is the
r
-dimensional Identity matrix. Finally, we have that

\begin{split}\left\lVert\Sigma^{\prime}-I_{r}\right\rVert_{F}=\left\lVert\hat{U}^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}^{\dagger/2}\Sigma\hat{\Sigma}^{\dagger/2}\hat{U}-\hat{U}^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}^{\dagger/2}\hat{\Sigma}\hat{\Sigma}^{\dagger/2}\hat{U}\right\rVert_{F}&\leqslant\left\lVert\hat{U}\hat{\Lambda}^{-1/2}\hat{U}^{\top}\left(\Sigma-\hat{\Sigma}\right)\hat{U}\hat{\Lambda}^{-1/2}\hat{U}^{\top}\right\rVert_{F}\\
&=\left\lVert\hat{U}\hat{\Lambda}^{-1/2}\Lambda^{1/2}\Lambda^{-1/2}\hat{U}^{\top}\left(\Sigma-\hat{\Sigma}\right)\hat{U}\Lambda^{-1/2}\Lambda^{1/2}\hat{\Lambda}^{-1/2}\hat{U}^{\top}\right\rVert_{F}\\
&\leqslant\left\lVert\hat{\Lambda}^{-1/2}\Lambda^{1/2}\right\rVert^{2}_{\textrm{op}}\left\lVert\Sigma^{\dagger/2}\left(\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma\right)\Sigma^{\dagger/2}\right\rVert_{F}\\
&\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\sqrt{\varepsilon}k/\alpha\right)\;,\end{split}

where we use that
\hat{\Lambda}^{-1/2}=\hat{\Lambda}^{-1/2}\Lambda^{1/2}\Lambda^{-1/2}
,
the sub-multiplicative property of the Frobenius norm, the column span
U
and
\hat{U}
is identical
(see (
2
)), and the Frobenius recovery guarantee in (
3
).

Finally, it follows from Lemma
2.48
that Condition
2.47

is affine invariant and is thus preserved under
x_{i}\to\hat{U}^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\left(x_{i}-\hat{\mu}\right)
,
for
i\in[n]
, with parameters
(\gamma,t)
.
∎

The above robust isotropic transformation lemma allows us to obtain a covariance that is close to the identity matrix
in a full-dimensional subspace (potentially smaller than the input dimension).
Therefore, we will subsequently drop the subscript for the dimension, wherever it is clear from the context.

Next, we show that whenever the minimum mixing weight is sufficiently larger than the fraction of outliers,
and a pair of components is covariance separated, we can partially cluster the samples.

Lemma 6.8

(Non-negligible Weight and Covariance Separation)
.

Given
0<\varepsilon<1/k^{k^{O(k^{2})}}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
let
\alpha=\varepsilon^{1/(10C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}G_{i}
with
G_{i}=\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
be a
k
-mixture of Gaussians
with mixture covariance
\Sigma
such that
w_{i}\geqslant\alpha
for all
i\in[k]

and there exist
i,j\in[k]
such that

\left\lVert\Sigma^{\dagger/2}\left(\Sigma_{i}-\Sigma_{j}\right)\Sigma^{\dagger/2}\right\rVert_{F}>1/\sqrt{\alpha}
. Further, let
X
be a set of points satisfying Condition
2.47
with respect to
\mathcal{M}

for some parameters
\gamma\leqslant\varepsilon d^{-8k}k^{-Ck}
, for a sufficiently large constant
C
, and
t\geqslant 8k
.
Let
Y
be an
\varepsilon
-corrupted version of
X
of size
n\geqslant n_{0}=\left(dk\right)^{\Omega(1)}/\varepsilon
,
Algorithm
4.4
partitions
Y
into
Y_{1}
,
Y_{2}
in time
n^{O(1)}
such that
with probability at least
\alpha^{k\log(k/\alpha)}

there is a non-trivial partition of
[k]
into
Q_{1}\cup Q_{2}
so that letting
\mathcal{M}_{j}

be a distribution proportional to
\sum_{i\in Q_{j}}w_{i}G_{i}
and
W_{j}=\sum_{i\in Q_{j}}w_{i}
, then

Y_{j}
is an
\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{1/(10C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)
-corrupted version of
\bigcup_{i\in Q_{j}}X_{i}

satisfying Condition
2.47
with respect to
\mathcal{M}

with parameters
(\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(k\gamma/W_{j}\right),t)
.

Proof.

We run Algorithm
4.4
with sample set
Y
, number of components
k
,
the fraction of outliers
\varepsilon
and the accuracy parameter
\beta
.
Since
X
satisfies Condition
2.47
,
we can set
t^{\prime}\geqslant 24
,
\beta=\alpha^{t^{\prime}/4-4}k^{t^{\prime}}(t^{\prime})^{2t^{\prime}}\leqslant\alpha
in Theorem
4.3
.
Then, by assumption, there exist
i,j
such that

\left\lVert\Sigma^{\dagger/2}\left(\Sigma_{i}-\Sigma_{j}\right)\Sigma^{\dagger/2}\right\rVert_{F}>\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha}}=\Omega\left(\frac{k^{2}(t^{\prime})^{4}}{(\beta\alpha^{4})^{2/t^{\prime}}}\right)\;.

We observe that we also satisfy the other preconditions for Theorem
4.3
,
since
n\geqslant\left(dk/\right)^{\Omega(1)}/\varepsilon
.

Then, Theorem
4.3
implies that with probability at least
\alpha^{k\log(k/\alpha)}
,
the set
Y
is partitioned in two sets
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
such that

there is a non-trivial partition of
[k]
into
Q_{1}\cup Q_{2}
so that letting
\mathcal{M}_{j}
be a distribution proportional
to
\sum_{i\in Q_{j}}w_{i}G_{i}
and
W_{j}=\sum_{i\in Q_{j}}w_{i}
, then

Y_{j}
is an
\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{1/(10C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)
-corrupted version of
\bigcup_{i\in Q_{j}}X_{i}
.
By Lemma
2.50
,
\bigcup_{i\in Q_{j}}X_{i}

satisfies Condition
2.47
with respect to
\mathcal{M}
with parameters
(\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(k\gamma/W_{j}\right),t)
.

∎

When the mixture is not covariance separated and nearly isotropic, we can obtain a small list of hypotheses such that one of them is
close to the true parameters, via tensor decomposition.

Lemma 6.9

(Mixture is List-decodable)
.

Given
0<\varepsilon<1/k^{k^{O(k^{2})}}
let
\alpha=\varepsilon^{1/(10C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}
.
Let
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}G_{i}
with
G_{i}=\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})

be a
k
-mixture of Gaussians with mixture mean
\mu
and mixture covariance
\Sigma
,
such that
\left\lVert\mu\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\sqrt{\varepsilon/\alpha}\right)
,

\left\|\Sigma-I\right\|_{F}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\sqrt{\varepsilon}/\alpha\right)
,

w_{i}\geqslant\alpha
for all
i\in[k]
,
and
\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-\Sigma_{j}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant 1/\sqrt{\alpha}
for any pair of components,
and let
X
be a set of points satisfying Condition
2.47

with respect to
\mathcal{M}
for some parameters
\gamma=\varepsilon d^{-8k}k^{-Ck}
, for a sufficiently large constant
C
,
and
t=8k
. Let
Y
be an
\varepsilon
-corrupted version of
X
of size
n
,
Algorithm
3.2

outputs a list
L
of hypotheses of size
\exp\left(1/\varepsilon^{2}\right)
in time
\poly(|L|,n)

such that if we choose a hypothesis

\{\hat{\mu}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
uniformly at random,

\left\lVert\mu_{i}-\hat{\mu}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{1/(20C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)
and

\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-\hat{\Sigma}_{i}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{1/(20C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)
for all
i
with probability at least
\exp\left(-1/\varepsilon^{2}\right)
.

Proof.

Recall we run Algorithm
3.2
on the samples
Y
, the number of clusters
k
, the fraction of outliers
\varepsilon
and the minimum weight
\alpha=\varepsilon^{1/(10C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}
. Next, we show that the preconditions of Theorem
3.1
are satisfied. First, the upper bounds on
\left\|\mu\right\|_{2}
and
\left\|\Sigma-I\right\|_{F}
imply
\sum_{i\in k}w_{i}\left(\Sigma_{i}+\mu_{i}\mu_{i}^{\top}\right)=\Sigma+\mu\mu^{\top}\preceq(1+\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\sqrt{\varepsilon}/\alpha\right))I
. Since the LHS is a conic combination of PSD matrices, it follows that for all
i\in[k]
,
\mu_{i}\mu_{i}^{\top}\preceq\frac{1}{\alpha}\left(1+\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\sqrt{\varepsilon}/\alpha\right)\right)I
,
and thus
\left\lVert\mu_{i}\mu_{i}^{\top}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\frac{2}{\alpha}
. Next, we can write:

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-I\right\rVert_{F}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\|\Sigma_{i}-(\Sigma+\mu\mu^{\top})\right\|_{F}+\left\|\Sigma-I\right\|_{F}+\left\|\mu\mu^{\top}\right\|_{F}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-\sum_{j\in[k]}w_{j}\left(\Sigma_{j}+\mu_{j}\mu_{j}^{\top}\right)\right\rVert_{F}+\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}k}{\alpha}+\frac{\varepsilon}{\alpha}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\lVert\sum_{j\in[k]}w_{j}\left(\Sigma_{i}-\Sigma_{j}\right)\right\rVert_{F}+\frac{2}{\alpha}+\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}k}{\alpha}+\frac{\varepsilon}{\alpha}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{4}{\alpha}\;,

where the first and the third inequalities follow from the triangle inequality and the upper bound on
\left\lVert\mu_{i}\mu_{i}^{\top}\right\rVert_{F}
,
and the last inequality follows from the assumption that
\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-\Sigma_{j}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant 1/\sqrt{\alpha}

for every pair of covariances
\Sigma_{i},\Sigma_{j}
. So, we can set
\Delta=4/\alpha

in Theorem
3.1
.
Then, given the definition of
\alpha
, we have that

\eta=2k^{4k}\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(1+\Delta/\alpha\right)^{4k}\sqrt{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{2/5}\right)

and
1/\varepsilon^{2}\geqslant\log(1/\eta)(k+1/\alpha+\Delta)^{4k+5}/\eta^{2}
.
Therefore, Algorithm
3.2
outputs a list
L
of hypotheses
such that
\left|L\right|=\exp\left(1/\varepsilon^{2}\right)
, and with probability at least
0.99
,

L
contains a hypothesis that satisfies the following: for all
i\in[k]
,

\begin{split}\left\lVert\hat{\mu}_{i}-\mu_{i}\right\rVert_{2}&=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\frac{\Delta^{1/2}}{\alpha}\right)\eta^{G(k)}=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{-1/(20C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\cdot\varepsilon^{1/(10C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{1/(20C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)\textrm{ and }\\
\left\lVert\hat{\Sigma}_{i}-\Sigma_{i}\right\rVert_{F}&=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(k^{4}\right)\frac{\Delta^{1/2}}{\alpha}\eta^{G(k)}=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{1/(20C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)\;.\end{split}

(6.2)

Then if we choose a hypothesis in
L
uniformly at random, the probability that we choose the hypothesis satisfying (
6.2
) is at least
1/|L|=\exp\left(-1/\varepsilon^{2}\right)
.
∎

Finally, if the mixture has a covariance matrix with small variance along any direction, we can further cluster the points by projecting the mixture along that direction.

Lemma 6.10

(Spectral Separation of Thin Components)
.

Given
0<\varepsilon<1/k^{k^{O(k^{2})}}
, let
\alpha=\varepsilon^{1/(10C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}
. Let
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}G_{i}
with
G_{i}=\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
be a
k
-mixture of Gaussians with mixture covariance
\Sigma

such that
\left\lVert\Sigma-I\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\sqrt{\varepsilon}k/\alpha\right)
,
and let
X
be a set of points satisfying Condition
2.47
with respect to
\mathcal{M}
for some parameters
(\gamma,t)
. Given a set
Y
being an
\varepsilon
-corrupted version of
X
of size
n
,

and estimates
\{\hat{\mu}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
,
such that
\left\lVert\mu_{i}-\hat{\mu}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{1/(20C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)
,

\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-\hat{\Sigma}_{i}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{1/(20C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)
,
suppose there exists a unit vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

v^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}_{s}v\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{1/(40C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)
,
for some
s\in[k]
.
Then, there is an algorithm that efficiently partitions
Y
into
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}

such that

there is a non-trivial partition of
[k]
into
Q_{1}\cup Q_{2}
so that letting
\mathcal{M}_{j}
be a distribution proportional to
\sum_{i\in Q_{j}}w_{i}G_{i}
and
W_{j}=\sum_{i\in Q_{j}}w_{i}
, then

Y_{j}
is an
\left(\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(k^{2}\gamma\right)+\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{1/(80kC^{k+1}(k+1)!)}/W_{j}\right)\right)
-corrupted version of
\bigcup_{i\in Q_{j}}X_{i}
satisfying Condition
2.47

with respect to
\mathcal{M}_{j}
with parameter
(\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(k\gamma/W_{j}\right),t)
.

Proof.

We run the algorithm from Lemma
5.1
with the input being the samples
Y
,
the current hypothesis
\{\hat{\mu}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
,
and the minimum eigenvalue
\eta=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{1/(40C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)
.
Observe that the mixture covariance satisfies
\Sigma\succeq\left(1-\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\sqrt{\varepsilon}k/\alpha\right)\right)I\succeq I/2

and the upper bound on means and covariance is
\delta=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{1/(20kC^{k+1}(k+1)!)}n\right)
by assumption.
Therefore, we satisfy the preconditions of Lemma
5.1
.
Thus, we obtain a partition
Y_{1},Y_{2}
such that

there is a non-trivial partition of
[k]
into
Q_{1}\cup Q_{2}
so that letting
\mathcal{M}_{j}
be a distribution proportional to
\sum_{i\in Q_{j}}w_{i}G_{i}
and
W_{j}=\sum_{i\in Q_{j}}w_{i}
, then it follows from Lemma
2.50
that

Y_{j}
is an
\left(\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(k^{2}\gamma\right)+\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{1/(80kC^{k+1}(k+1)!)}/W_{j}\right)\right)
-corrupted version of
\bigcup_{i\in Q_{j}}X_{i}
satisfying Condition
2.47
with respect to
\mathcal{M}_{j}
with parameter
(\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(k\gamma/W_{j}\right),t)
.

∎

6.2
Proof of the Main Theorem

We are now ready to complete the proof of Theorem
6.1
.

Figure 1:
If we assume a
1/\poly(k)
lower bound on minimum weight,
then we can skip all blue steps above; the partial clustering is carried out till it can no longer be done within a cluster and then followed by the tensor decomposition step.

Proof of Theorem
6.1
.

We divide the proof into two parts: first we show that Algorithm
6.4
outputs a hypothesis
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}=\sum_{i\in[k]}\hat{w}_{i}\mathcal{N}(\hat{\mu}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}_{i})
such that
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}
and
\mathcal{M}
are
\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{c_{k}}\right)
-close in total variation distance with probability at least
\exp\left(-O(k)/\varepsilon^{2}\right)
; then we show that Algorithm
6.3
outputs a
k
-mixture of Gaussians
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}
such that
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}
and
\mathcal{M}
are
\mathcal{O}_{k}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{c_{k}}\right)
-close in total variation distance with probability 0.99.

We proceed the first part by induction on
k
.
Let
c_{k}=\frac{1}{(100)^{k}C^{(k+1)!}\mathrm{sf}(k+1)k!}
be a scalar that only depends on
k
,
where
C>0
is a sufficiently large universal constant.

Induction Hypothesis
:

Let
X
be a set of points satisfying Condition
2.47
with respect to a
k
-mixture of Gaussians
\mathcal{M}
for some parameters
\gamma=\varepsilon d^{-8k}k^{-C^{\prime}k}
,
where
C^{\prime}
is a sufficiently large constant and
t=8k+48
. Given a set
Y
being an
\varepsilon
-corrupted version of
X
of size
n
, the outlier parameter
\varepsilon
and the component-number parameter
k
,
Algorithm
6.3
returns a
k
-mixture of Gaussians
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}

such that
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}
and
\mathcal{M}
are
\mathcal{O}_{k}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{c_{k}}\right)
-close in total variation distance with probability
\exp\left(-(3k-2)/\varepsilon^{2}\right)
.

Base Case
:
For
k=1
, the algorithm returns the single Gaussian with mean
\hat{\mu}
and
\hat{\Sigma}
at Step 2. Suppose the true Gaussian is
N(\mu,\Sigma)
.
It follows from the proof of Lemma
6.7
,

\left\lVert\Sigma^{\dagger/2}\left(\hat{\mu}-\mu\right)\right\rVert_{2}=\left\lVert\Sigma^{\dagger/2}\left(\hat{\mu}-\mu\right)\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\sqrt{\varepsilon}\right)

and

\left\lVert\Sigma^{\dagger/2}\left(\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma\right)\Sigma^{\dagger/2}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\sqrt{\varepsilon}\right)\;,

and thus it follows from Fact
2.1
that the total variation distance
between the hypothesis Gaussian and the true Gaussian is at most
\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\sqrt{\varepsilon}\right)
.
We can then conclude that the base case is true.

Inductive Step
: We assume that our induction hypothesis holds for any
m<k
and then prove that the induction hypothesis holds for
k
.

Small Clusters Can be Treated as Noise.

Conditioning on the base case being true, we begin by guessing
whether the minimum weight is less than
\varepsilon^{1/(10C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}
with equal probability.

Let
w_{\min}=\min_{i}w_{i}
. If
w_{\min}\leqslant\varepsilon^{1/(10C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}
, our algorithm takes step 1 with probability 0.5. In this case,
we treat the smallest component as noise and recurse on the set of samples
Y
. We set the number of components to be
k-1

and the fraction of outliers being
\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{1/(10C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\leqslant 2\varepsilon^{1/(10C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}
. By Lemma
2.50
,
Y
is an
2\varepsilon^{1/(10C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}
-corrupted version of a set satisfying Condition
2.47
with respect to a
(k-1)
-mixture for parameters
\gamma=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(k\varepsilon d^{-8k}k^{-C^{\prime}k}/(1-w_{\min})\right)\leqslant\varepsilon d^{-8(k-1)}(k-1)^{-C^{\prime}(k-1)}
and
t=8k+48
.
Thus applying the inductive hypothesis to
Y
, we learn the mixture up to total variation distance
\mathcal{O}_{k}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\left(2\varepsilon^{1/(10C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)^{c_{k-1}}\right)\leqslant\mathcal{O}_{k}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{c_{k}}\right)
with probability

0.5\exp\left(-(3(k-1)-2)/\varepsilon^{2}\right)\geqslant\exp\left(-(3k-2)/\varepsilon^{2}\right)
. Now we may assume for all
i\in[k]
,
w_{i}\geqslant\varepsilon^{1/(10C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}
.

Mixture is Covariance Separated.

Let
\alpha=\varepsilon^{1/(10C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}
and

\psi_{1}=\{\exists\hskip 3.61371pt\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i}),\mathcal{N}(\mu_{j},\Sigma_{j})\mid\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-\Sigma_{j}\right\rVert_{F}>\alpha^{-1/2}\}

be the event that the samples were drawn from a mixture that is
covariance separated
.
First, consider the case where
\psi_{1}
is true. We will run 3(a) with probability 0.5.
Then it follows from Lemma
6.8
that
Y

can be partitioned into
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
in time
d^{O(1)}
, such that they both have at least one component
and the fraction of outliers in each set
Y_{1},Y_{2}
is at most
\varepsilon^{1/(10C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}
with probability
\alpha^{O(k\log(k/\alpha))}
.

Then, we can guess the number of components in
Y_{1}
and we will be correct with probability
1/k
. Conditioned on our guess being correct, let
Y_{1}
consist of
k_{1}
components and
Y_{2}
consist of
k_{2}
components and
k_{1}+k_{2}=k
.

Let
Q_{1}\cup Q_{2}
be the non-trivial partition of
[k]
in Lemma
6.8
,
\mathcal{M}_{j}
be a distribution proportional to
\sum_{i\in Q_{j}}w_{i}G_{i}
and
W_{j}=\sum_{i\in Q_{j}}w_{i}
, then By Lemma
2.50
,

Y_{j}
is an
\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{1/(10C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)
-corrupted version of
\bigcup_{i\in Q_{j}}X_{i}
satisfying Condition
2.47
with respect to
\mathcal{M}
with parameters
\gamma=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(k\varepsilon d^{-8k}k^{-C^{\prime}k}/\alpha\right)\leqslant\varepsilon d^{-8k_{j}}(k_{j})^{-C^{\prime}k_{j}}
and
t=8k+48
.
Then, applying the inductive hypothesis on
Y_{j}
for
j=1,2
, with number of components
k_{j}
,
we can learn the mixtures
\mathcal{M}_{j}
up to total variation distance error

\mathcal{O}_{k}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{c_{k_{j}}/(10C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)

with probability
\exp\left(-(3k_{j}-2)/\varepsilon^{2}\right)
. Finally if this is the case, we combine the two hypotheses on
Y_{1},Y_{2}
by multiplying each weight in the hypothesis of
Y_{j}
by
|Y_{j}|/|Y|
and then taking union of two hypotheses.
Then our combining method gives a final output that learns our full hypothesis to total variation distance error

\mathcal{O}_{k}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{c_{k_{1}}/(10C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)+\mathcal{O}_{k}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{c_{k_{2}}/(10C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)\leqslant\mathcal{O}_{k}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{c_{k}}\right)
with probability at least

0.5\cdot 0.5\cdot\frac{1}{k}\cdot\alpha^{O(k\log(k/\alpha))}\exp\left(-(3k_{1}-2)/\varepsilon^{2}\right)\exp\left(-(3k_{2}-2)/\varepsilon^{2}\right)\geqslant\exp\left(-(3k-2)/\varepsilon^{2}\right)
.

Mixture is not Covariance Separated.

Next, consider the case where
\psi_{1}
is false.
With probability 0.5, the algorithm guesses correctly and executes Step 2. Since the mixture is not covariance separated, we satisfy the preconditions of Lemma
6.7
, and after applying the transformation in Step 2,
\Sigma
, the covariance of the mixture
\mathcal{M}
,
is
\sqrt{\varepsilon}k/\alpha
-close to the
r
-dimensional identity,
where
r
is the rank of
\Sigma
. However, since we obtain the subspace exactly,
we can simply project all samples on the subspace and we drop the
r
in the subsequent exposition.

Let
X^{\prime}
be the set of points obtained by applying the Affine transformation from Step 2 as defined in Lemma
6.7
. Then,
X^{\prime}
satisfies Condition
2.47
with respect to a nearly isotropic mixture and parameters
\gamma=\varepsilon d^{-8k}k^{-C^{\prime}k}
and
t=8k+48
so that we can continue the algorithm with
X^{\prime}
. Whenever we return a hypothesis in the following steps, we will first apply the inverse of the transformation on our estimates
\hat{\mu}_{i}
and
\hat{\Sigma}_{i}
. Since total variation distance is affine invariant, we have the same error guarantee in total variation distance after applying the transformation.
From now on, we reduce to the case where
\Sigma
is
\sqrt{\varepsilon}k/\alpha
-close to the Identity.

There is a 50% chance our algorithm runs Step 3(b) and we will analyze the remainder of this case under that assumption.

It follows from Lemma
6.9
that we obtain a hypothesis
\{\hat{\mu}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
such that

\left\lVert\mu_{i}-\hat{\mu}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{1/(20C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)
and

\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-\hat{\Sigma}_{i}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{1/(20C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)
with probability
\exp\left(-1/\varepsilon^{2}\right)
.

Conditioned on the hypothesis being correct, we now split into two cases: either all eigenvalues of all the estimated covariances are large
(in which case we obtain total variation distance guarantees),
or there is a direction along which we can project and cluster further.

Covariance Estimates have Large Eigenvalues.

For the hypothesis
\{\hat{\mu}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
from the last step, we compute all the eigenvalues of the estimated covariance matrices,

\hat{\Sigma}_{i}
, for all
i\in[k]
.
If, for all
i\in[k]
,
\lambda_{\min}\left(\hat{\Sigma}_{i}\right)\geqslant c\varepsilon^{1/(40C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}
,
we land in Step 3(b).i that we guess the mixing weights
\hat{w}_{i}
uniformly in the range
[0,1]
and then we output the corresponding hypothesis
\{\hat{w}_{i},\hat{\mu}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
.
With probability at least
\varepsilon^{k}
,
\hat{w}_{i}
are within
\varepsilon
of the true mixing weights.
Under this condition, by Lemma
6.5
, the mixture

\widehat{\mathcal{M}}=\sum_{i\in[k]}\hat{w}_{i}\mathcal{N}\left(\hat{\mu}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}_{i}\right)
is

\mathcal{O}_{k}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{1/(40C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)\leqslant\mathcal{O}_{k}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{c_{k}}\right)
-close to
\mathcal{M}
in
total variation distance

with probability
0.5\cdot 0.5\cdot\varepsilon^{k}\cdot\exp\left(-1/\varepsilon^{2}\right)\geqslant\exp\left(-(3k-2)/\varepsilon^{2}\right)
.

One Covariance Has a Small Eigenvalue.

Consider the case (Step 3(b).ii) where there exists a unit-norm direction
v

and an estimate
\hat{\Sigma}_{i}
such that
v^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}_{i}v\leqslant c\varepsilon^{1/(40C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}
.
It then follows from Lemma
6.10
that we can partition
Y
into
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}

such that each has at least one cluster and the total number of outliers in both
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}

is at most
\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{1/(80kC^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)n
.
If
Y_{1}
or
Y_{2}
has size less than
\varepsilon^{1/(400kC^{k+1}(k+1)!)}n
,
then we can treat it as noise and get an additive
O(\varepsilon^{1/(400kC^{k+1}(k+1)!)})
-error in total variation distance.
Otherwise, the fraction of outliers in both sets is at most

\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left((\varepsilon^{1/(80kC^{k+1}(k+1)!)}n)/(\varepsilon^{1/(400kC^{k+1}(k+1)!)}n)\right)=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{1/(100kC^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)
.

We then guess the number of components,
k_{1}
, in
Y_{1}
with success probability
1/k
. Let
k_{2}=k-k_{1}
be the number of components in
Y_{2}
. Then, conditioned on this event holding,

Y_{j}
is an
\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{1/(100kC^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)
-corrupted version of a set satisfying Condition
2.47
with respect to a mixture of
k_{j}
components with parameter
\gamma=k\varepsilon d^{-8k}k^{-C^{\prime}k}/\alpha\leqslant\varepsilon d^{-8(k_{j})}(k_{j})^{-C^{\prime}(k_{j})}
and
t=8k+48
.

We can apply the inductive hypothesis to
Y_{1}

with number of components
k_{1}
and fraction of outliers
\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{1/(100kC^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)
,
and conclude that we learn the components of
Y_{1}

to total variation distance
\mathcal{O}_{k}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{c_{k_{1}}/(100kC^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)

with probability
\exp\left(-(3k_{1}-2)/\varepsilon^{2}\right)
.
A similar argument holds for
Y_{2}
.
Finally if this is the case, we combine the two hypotheses on
Y_{1},Y_{2}
by multiplying each weight by
|Y_{j}|/|Y|
and then taking union of two hypotheses.
Then our combining method gives a final output that learns our full hypothesis to total variation distance error

\mathcal{O}_{k}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{c_{k_{1}}/(100C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)+\mathcal{O}_{k}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{c_{k_{2}}/(100C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)+\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{1/(400kC^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)\leqslant\mathcal{O}_{k}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{c_{k}}\right)
with probability at least

0.5\cdot 0.5\cdot\frac{1}{k}\cdot\exp\left(-1/\varepsilon^{2}-(3k_{1}-2)/\varepsilon^{2}-(3k_{2}-2)/\varepsilon^{2}\right)\geqslant\exp\left(-(3k-2)/\varepsilon^{2}\right)
.

Sample Size and Running Time of Algorithm
6.4

By Lemma
2.51
, we need
n\geqslant kt^{C^{\prime}t}d^{t}/\gamma^{3}
samples to generate
X
satisfying Condition
2.47
with parameters
(\gamma,t)
. We set
\gamma=\varepsilon d^{-8k}k^{-C^{\prime}k}
and
t=8k+48
. Then
n\geqslant n_{0}=(8k)^{O(k)}d^{O(k)}/\varepsilon^{3}
.
The running time in each sub-routine we invoke is dominated by the running time
of the tensor decomposition algorithm, and by Lemma
6.9
in the worst case
this is
\poly(|L|,n)=\poly\left(\exp\left(1/\varepsilon^{2}\right),d^{O(k)}/\varepsilon^{3}\right)=d^{O(k)}\exp\left(1/\varepsilon^{2}\right)
.

This completes the first part of the proof.

Aggregating Hypotheses.

We run Algorithm
6.4
repeatedly on set
Y
and add the return hypothesis into a list
\mathcal{L}
until with probability 0.99, there exists a hypothesis
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}\in\mathcal{L}
such that
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}
and
\mathcal{M}
are
\mathcal{O}_{k}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{c_{k}}\right)
-close in total variation distance. Since Algorithm
6.4
outputs a correct mixture with probability
\exp\left(-(3k-2)/\varepsilon^{2}\right)
, we will run Algorithm
6.4
for
\exp\left(O(k)/\varepsilon^{2}\right)
times. Then the total running time is
\exp\left(O(k)/\varepsilon^{2}\right)\cdot d^{O(k)}\exp\left(1/\varepsilon^{2}\right)=d^{O(k)}\exp\left(O(k)/\varepsilon^{2}\right)
.

Robust Tournament.

Then we need to run a robust tournament in order to find a hypothesis that is close to the true mixture in total variation distance.
Fact
2.52
shows that we can do this efficiently only with access to an
\varepsilon
-corrupted set of samples
of size
\mathcal{O}_{k}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\log(1/\varepsilon)/\varepsilon^{2c_{k}}\right)
.

This completes the proof.
∎

7
More Efficient Robust Partial Cluster Recovery

In this section, we prove the following upgraded partial
clustering theorem. In contrast to Theorem
4.3
, here we obtain a probability of success that is inverse exponential in
k
instead of
1/\alpha
.

Theorem 7.1

(Robust Partial Clustering in Relative Frobenius Distance)
.

Let
0\leqslant\varepsilon<\alpha/k\leqslant 1
and
t\in\mathbb{N}
. There is an algorithm with the following guarantees:
Let
Y
be an
\varepsilon
-corruption of a sample
X
of size
n\geqslant(dk)^{Ct}/\varepsilon
for a large enough constant
C>0
, from

\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
that satisfies Condition
2.47

with parameters
2t
and
\gamma\leqslant\varepsilon d^{-8t}k^{-Ck}
, for a large enough constant
C>0
.
Suppose further that
w_{i}\geqslant\alpha>2\varepsilon
for each
i\in[k]
,
and that for some
t\in\mathbb{N}
,
\beta>0
there exist
i,j\leqslant k
such that

\left\lVert\Sigma^{\dagger/2}(\Sigma_{i}-\Sigma_{j})\Sigma^{\dagger/2}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}=\Omega\left((k^{2}t^{4})/\beta^{2/t}\alpha^{4}\right)
,
where
\Sigma
is the covariance of the mixture
\mathcal{M}
.
Then, for any
\eta\gg\sqrt{\varepsilon/\alpha}
, the algorithm runs in time
n^{O(t)}
, and with probability at least
2^{-O\left(k\right)}(1-O(\eta/\alpha-\sqrt{\eta}))
over the random choices of the algorithm,
outputs a partition
Y=Y_{1}\cup Y_{2}
satisfying:

1.

Partition respects clustering:
for each
i
,
\max\left\{\frac{1}{w_{i}n}|Y_{1}\cap X_{i}|,\frac{1}{w_{i}n}|Y_{2}\cap X_{i}|\right\}\geqslant 1-O(\sqrt{\eta})-O(\frac{\beta}{\eta\alpha^{2}})
, where
X_{i}\subset X
corresponding to the points drawn from
\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
.

2.

Partition is non-trivial:

\max_{i}\frac{1}{w_{i}n}|X_{i}\cap Y_{1}|,\max_{i}\frac{1}{w_{i}n}|X_{i}\cap Y_{2}|\geqslant 1-O(\sqrt{\eta})-O(\frac{\beta}{\eta\alpha^{2}})
.

7.1
Algorithm

Our algorithm will solve SoS relaxations of a polynomial inequality system.
The indeterminates in this system are
X^{\prime}
(that is intended to be the guess for the original uncorrupted sample), a cluster of size
\alpha n
within
X^{\prime}
(indicated by
z_{i}
s) with mean
\hat{\mu}
and covariance matrix
\hat{\Sigma}
and
\Pi
(intended to be the square root of
\hat{\Sigma}
). The input corrupted sample
Y
is a constant in this inequality system. Let
U\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and
m,z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
also be indeterminates of the proof system.
The system can be thought of as encoding the task of finding clusters
\hat{C}
within
Y
that satisfies certifiable hypercontractivity of degree
2
polynomials.

We present the constraints grouped together into meaningful categories below:
The first set of constraints enforce that
\hat{\Sigma}
is the square of
\Pi
.

\text{Covariance Constraints: $\mathcal{A}_{1}$}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &&\Pi&=UU^{\top}\\
&&\Pi^{2}&=\hat{\Sigma}\\
\end{aligned}\right\}

(7.1)

The intersection constraints force that
X^{\prime}
intersects
Y
in all but an
\varepsilon n
points (and thus,
2\varepsilon
-close to unknown sample
X
).

\text{Intersection Constraints: $\mathcal{A}_{2}$}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\forall i\in[n],&m_{i}^{2}&=m_{i}\\
&&\textstyle\sum_{i\in[n]}m_{i}&=(1-\varepsilon)n\\
&\forall i\in[n],&z_{i}(\tilde{y}_{i}-x^{\prime}_{i})&=0\end{aligned}\right\}

(7.2)

The subset constraints enforce that
z
indicate a subset of size
\alpha n
of
X^{\prime}
.

\text{Subset Constraints: $\mathcal{A}_{3}$}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\forall i\in[n].&z_{i}^{2}&=z_{i}\\
&&\textstyle\sum_{i\in[n]}z_{i}&=\alpha n\\
\end{aligned}\right\}

(7.3)

Parameter constraints create indeterminates to stand for the covariance
\hat{\Sigma}
and mean
\hat{\mu}
of
\hat{C}
(indicated by
z
).

\text{Parameter Constraints: $\mathcal{A}_{4}$}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &&\frac{1}{\alpha n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}\left(x^{\prime}_{i}-\hat{\mu}\right)\left(x^{\prime}_{i}-\hat{\mu}\right)^{\top}&=\hat{\Sigma}\\
&&\frac{1}{\alpha n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}x^{\prime}_{i}&=\hat{\mu}\\
\end{aligned}\right\}

(7.4)

Certifiable Hypercontractivity :
\mathcal{A}_{4}
=

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\forall t\leqslant 2s&\E_{z}(Q-\E_{z}Q)^{2t}&\leqslant(Ct/\alpha)^{t}2^{2t}\left(\E_{z}(Q-\E_{z}Q)^{2}\right)^{t}\\
&&\E_{z}(Q-\E_{z}Q)^{2}&\leqslant 10\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}\right)^{2}\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\end{aligned}\right\}

(7.5)

where we write
\E_{z}Q
as a shorthand for the polynomial
\frac{1}{\alpha^{n}}\sum_{i}z_{i}Q(x_{i})
and
\E_{z}(Q-\E_{X_{r}}Q)^{2j}
for the polynomial
\frac{1}{\alpha n}\sum_{i}z_{i}\left(Q(x^{\prime}_{i})-\frac{1}{\alpha n}\sum_{i\leqslant n}z_{i}Q(x^{\prime}_{i})\right)^{2j}
for any
j
. Note that
Q
is a
d\times d
-matrix valued indeterminate. Observe that
Q
itself can be eliminated from the system as is standard in several applications
[
KS17b
,
KS17a
,
HL18
,
BK20b
]
of SoS proofs in obtaining a succinct set of polynomial constraints (see Section 4.3 on “Succinct Representation of Constraints” in
[
FKP19
]
for an exposition).

Algorithm 7.2

(Polynomial Time Partial Clustering)
.

Given:

A sample
Y
of size
n
. An outlier parameter
\varepsilon>0
and an accuracy parameter
\eta>0
.

Output:

A partition of
Y
into partial clustering
Y_{1}\cup Y_{2}
.

Operation:

1.

Mean and Covariance Estimation
: Apply Robust Mean and Covariance Estimation (Fact
2.37
) to estimate
\hat{\mu}
and
\tilde{\Sigma}
such that
\frac{1}{2}\Sigma\preceq\tilde{\Sigma}\preceq 1.5\Sigma
where
\Sigma
is the covariance of the uncorrupted input mixture.

2.

Approximate Isotropic Transformation
: For each
y_{i}\in Y
, let
\tilde{y}_{i}=\tilde{\Sigma}^{\dagger/2}(y_{i}-\hat{\mu})
. Let
\tilde{Y}=\cup_{i\leqslant n}\tilde{y}_{i}
.

3.

SDP Solving:
Find a pseudo-distribution
\tilde{\zeta}
satisfying
\cup_{i=1}^{4}\mathcal{A}_{i}
such that
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}z_{i}\in\alpha\pm o_{d}(1)
for every
i
. If no such pseudo-distribution exists, output fail.

4.

Rounding:
Let
M=\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{z\sim\tilde{\zeta}}[zz^{\top}]
.

(a)

Generate candidate clusters
: For
\ell=O(1/\alpha\log\eta/\alpha)
times, draw a uniformly random
i\in[n]
and let
\hat{C}_{i}=\{j\mid M(i,j)\geqslant\alpha^{2}/2\}
. Let
\mathcal{L}=\cup_{i\leqslant\ell}\hat{C}_{i}
.

(b)

Candidate 2nd Moment Estimation:
For each
\hat{C}_{i}\in\mathcal{L}
, let
S_{i}
be the output of running robust 2nd moment estimation with Frobenius error (Lemma
7.7
) on
\hat{C}_{i}
with outlier parameter
\eta_{i}^{\prime}=O(\frac{\varepsilon}{\alpha}+\frac{\beta}{\alpha^{2}\eta})
.

(c)

Merge candidate clusters
: For each
i\leqslant\ell
, find
\mathcal{L}_{i}
to be the collection of all
j
such that
\left\lVert S_{i}-S_{j}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant 2C\tau
for a large enough constant
C>0
. Set
\hat{C}_{i}\cup\mathcal{L}_{i}=\hat{B}_{i}
. Repeat on
\mathcal{L}\setminus\{\mathcal{L}_{i}\cup i\}
.

(d)

Output a union of a random subset of candidates
: For
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}=\cup_{i}\hat{B}_{i}
, choose a uniformly random subset
S
of
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
, set
Y_{1}=\cup_{j\in S}\hat{B}_{j}
and set
Y_{2}=Y\setminus Y_{1}
.

Analysis of Algorithm

Lemma 7.3

(Success of Step 1)
.

Let
\tilde{\Sigma}
be the output of the robust covariance estimation algorithm (Fact
2.37
) applied to the input sample
Y
with outlier parameter
\varepsilon
. If
Y
is an
\varepsilon
-corruption of a sample
X
from a GMM with minimum weight
\geqslant\alpha\geqslant\Omega(\sqrt{\varepsilon})
, mixture mean
\mu
and covariance
\Sigma
satisfying Condition
2.47
, then,

0.5\Sigma\preceq\tilde{\Sigma}\preceq 1.5\Sigma\,,

\left\lVert\tilde{\Sigma}^{-1/2}(\mu-\hat{\mu})\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}/\alpha)\,.

Proof.

The lemma immediately follows by noting that GMMs with minimum weight
\alpha
are
4
-certifiably
1/\alpha
-subgaussian (Fact
2.27
) and
\alpha\geqslant\Omega(\sqrt{\varepsilon})
.

∎

Lemma 7.4

(Simultaneous Intersection Bounds for Frobenius Separated Case)
.

Let
X=X_{1}\cup X_{2}\cup\ldots X_{k}
be a sample of size
n\geqslant(dk)^{Ct}/\varepsilon
for a large enough constant
C>0
, from

\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
that satisfies Condition
2.47

with parameters
2t
and
\gamma\leqslant\varepsilon d^{-8t}k^{-Ck}
.
Suppose further that
\left\lVert\mu_{i}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\frac{2}{\alpha}
for every
i
,
\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{\alpha}
for every
i
and the mixture mean
\mu
, covariance
\Sigma
satisfy
\left\lVert\mu\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant 1
and
0.5I\preceq\Sigma\preceq 1.5I
.
Let
\tau=10^{8}\frac{C^{6}t^{4}}{\beta^{2/t}\alpha^{2}}
, for any
\beta>0
.
Then, given any
\varepsilon
-corruption
Y
of
X
, for every
i,j
such that
\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-\Sigma_{j}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\geqslant\Omega(\tau)
,

\left\{\cup_{i=1}^{4}\mathcal{A}_{i}\right\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{z}$}}}$}}}\left\{z^{\prime}(X_{i})z^{\prime}(X_{j})\leqslant\beta\right\}\,,

where
z^{\prime}(X_{i})=\frac{1}{w_{i}n}\sum_{j\in X_{i}}z_{j}\bm{1}(x_{j}=y_{j})
for every
i
.

Proof of Theorem
7.1
.

First, since
Y
is an
\varepsilon
-corruption of a sample
X
from a GMM such that
X
satisfies Condition
2.47
, our robust mean and covariance estimation procedure (Step 1) applied to the mixture succeeds and recovers an estimate of the covariance that is multiplicative
1\pm 0.5
-factor approximation in Löwner order. Thus, for the rest of the analysis, we can assume that the smallest and largest eigenvalue of the mixture covariance are at least
0.5
and at most
1.5
. Since each component has weight at least
\alpha
, this means that each of the constitute component covariance can now be assumed to have a spectral norm at most
1.5/\alpha
.

Next, by an argument similar to the one presented in the proof of Theorem
4.2
, the convex program we wrote is approximately solvable in polynomial time and is feasible whenever the uncorrupted sample
X
satisfies Condition
2.47
. The only change here is in the certifiable hypercontractivity constraints where instead of the RHS of the bounded variance constraint is stated in terms of
\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}
instead of
\left\lVert\Pi Q\Pi\right\rVert_{F}^{2}
with an additional slack of
O(1/\alpha^{2})
. This modified constraint is satisfied by all true clusters by an application of Lemma
2.25
since each of their covariance has spectral norm at most
1.5/\alpha
.

Rounding

Let
M=\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}zz^{\top}
. Then, by an argument similar to the proof of Theorem
4.2
, we can conclude:

1.

o_{d}(1)+\alpha\geqslant M(i,j)\geqslant 0
.

2.

\sum_{j=1}^{n}M(i,j)\geqslant(\alpha^{2}-o_{d}(1))n
for every
i
.

3.

For every
i
, let
B_{i}
be the set of “large entries”: i.e.
j
such that
M(i,j)\geqslant\alpha^{2}/2
. Then,
|B_{i}|\geqslant\alpha n/2
.

In the following, let
M_{i}
denote the
i
-th row of
M
and
\left\lVert M_{i}\right\rVert_{1}
for the sum of the non-negative entries of the vector
M_{i}
.

Candidate Clusters

For every
i
, let
F_{i}\subseteq[k]
be the set of all
i^{\prime}\in[k]
such that
\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-\Sigma_{i^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\geqslant\tau
(i.e.,
F_{i}
is the set of indices of true clusters whose covariances are far from that of the
i
-th cluster in Frobenius norm).
For every row
j\in[n]
, let
C(j)\in[k]
be such that
j\in X_{C(j)}

Let’s call
j
-th row of
M
“good” if
x_{j}=y_{j}
(i.e
j
-th sample is not an outlier) and the following condition holds:

\sum_{r\in F_{C(j)}}\sum_{\ell\in X_{r}:x_{\ell}=y_{\ell}}M(j,\ell)\leqslant\left\lVert M_{j}\right\rVert_{1}\left(\frac{\beta}{\eta}\right)\,.

Thus, by Markov’s inequality, the fraction of non-outlier entries in
B_{j}
that come from
X_{r^{\prime}}
such that
r^{\prime}\in F_{r}
is at most
2\left(\frac{\beta}{\eta\alpha^{2}}\right)
.

Let us estimate the fraction of good rows now.
From Lemma
4.6
and Fact
2.18
, we have that for every
r
and
r^{\prime}\in F_{r}
:

\tilde{\mathbb{E}}[z^{\prime}(X_{r})z^{\prime}(X_{r^{\prime}})]\leqslant\beta\,.

Here, recall that
z^{\prime}(X_{r})=\frac{1}{w_{r}n}\sum_{i\leqslant n}z_{i}\bm{1}(y_{i}=x_{i})
for every
r
.
Summing up over
r^{\prime}\in F_{r}
yields:

\frac{1}{w_{r}n}\sum_{r^{\prime}\in F_{r}}\sum_{i\in X_{r}:x_{i}=y_{i}}\sum_{j\in X_{r^{\prime}}:x_{j}=y_{j}}\tilde{\mathbb{E}}[z_{i}z_{j}]\leqslant n\beta\,.

Thus, by Markov’s inequality, with probability at least
1-\eta
over the choice of
i\in X_{r}
such that
x_{i}=y_{i}
, it must hold that:

\sum_{r^{\prime}\in F_{r}}\sum_{j\in X_{r^{\prime}}:x_{j}=y_{j}}\tilde{\mathbb{E}}[z_{i}z_{j}]\leqslant n\left(\frac{\beta}{\eta}\right)\,.

Using that
(1-\varepsilon/\alpha)
-fraction of
i\in X_{r}
satisfy
x_{i}=y_{i}
, for every
r
, we conclude that
1-\eta-\varepsilon/\alpha
-fraction of the rows
X_{r}
are good.

Thus, with probability at least
(1-\eta-\varepsilon/\alpha)^{\ell}\geqslant(1-O(\ell(\eta+\varepsilon/\alpha)))
, every candidate cluster picked in Step 1 of our rounding algorithm corresponds to the large entries from a good row of
M
.

We next claim that we cover most of the points in the input in the union of the candidate clusters:

|\cup_{i\leqslant\ell}\hat{C}_{i}|\geqslant\left(1-2\sqrt{\eta}-\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{\eta}\alpha}\right)n

(7.6)

with probability at least
1-\sqrt{\eta}
. To see why, let’s estimate the chance that an element
j\in[n]
does not appear in any of the
\hat{C}_{i}
s. First, we can assume that
j
-th row of
M
is good (this loses us
\eta+\varepsilon/\alpha
-fraction
j
s). For each such
j
, there are at least
\alpha n/2
large entries. Since
M
is symmetric, the
j
-th column of
M
also has
\alpha n/2
large entries. Further,
j
appears in
\cup_{i\leqslant\ell}\hat{C}_{i}
if at least one of the
\alpha n/2
large entries are chosen in our rounding. The chance that this does not happen in any of the
\ell
picks is at most
(1-\alpha/2)^{\ell}
. Since
\ell=\Theta\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}\log(1/\eta)\right)
, this chance is at most
O(\eta)
. Thus, in expectation
|[n]\setminus\cup_{i\leqslant\ell}\hat{C}_{i}|\leqslant O\left(\eta+\varepsilon/\alpha\right)n
. By Markov’s inequality, with probability at least
1-\sqrt{\eta}
,
|[n]\setminus\cup_{i\leqslant\ell}\hat{C}_{i}|\leqslant O\left(\sqrt{\eta}+\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{\eta}\alpha}\right)n
.

By a union bound, with probability at least
1-O\left(\eta\ell-\varepsilon\ell/\alpha\right)-\sqrt{\eta}\geqslant 1-O\left(\eta\log(1/\eta)/\alpha-\sqrt{\eta}\right)
, we must thus have both the following events hold simultaneously:

|\cup_{i\leqslant\ell}\hat{C}_{i}|\geqslant\left(1-2\sqrt{\eta}-\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{\eta}\alpha}\right)n\geqslant(1-3\sqrt{\eta})n

(7.7)

and, for every
1\leqslant i\leqslant\ell
,

|\hat{C}_{i}\cap(\cup_{r^{\prime}\in F_{C(r)}}X_{r^{\prime}})|\leqslant 2\left(\frac{\beta}{\eta\alpha}+\varepsilon/\alpha\right)\cdot|\hat{C}_{i}|\,.

(7.8)

Merging Candidate Clusters

Observe, following the proof of Theorem
4.3
, we know that there exists a partition of
Y
into sets
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
such that for all
i
,

\max\left\{\frac{1}{w_{i}n}|Y_{1}\cap X_{i}|,\frac{1}{w_{i}n}|Y_{2}\cap X_{i}|\right\}\geqslant 1-O(\sqrt{\eta})-O(\frac{\beta}{\eta\alpha^{2}}),

and

\max_{i}\frac{1}{w_{i}n}|X_{i}\cap Y_{1}|,\max_{i}\frac{1}{w_{i}n}|X_{i}\cap Y_{2}|\geqslant 1-O(\sqrt{\eta})-O(\frac{\beta}{\eta\alpha^{2}}).

Next, we show that the merging step preserves this partition.
For each
\hat{C}_{i}
, let
\hat{C}_{i}^{\prime}=\hat{C}_{i}\cap\cup_{j\not\in F_{C(i)}}X_{j}
. That is,
\hat{C}_{i}^{\prime}
is the subset of
\hat{C}_{i}
obtained by removing points from “far-off” clusters and the outliers. Then, since we know that
|\hat{C}_{i}|\geqslant\alpha n/2
and
|X\cap Y|\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)n
, we must have
|\hat{C}_{i}|-|\hat{C}_{i}^{\prime}|=\eta^{\prime}_{i}|\hat{C}_{i}|\leqslant\left(\frac{3\varepsilon}{\alpha}+\frac{2\beta}{\eta\alpha^{2}}\right)|\hat{C}_{i}|
, where we note that
\eta^{\prime}_{i}\leqslant\left(\frac{3\varepsilon}{\alpha}+\frac{2\beta}{\eta\alpha^{2}}\right)
.

Thus,
\hat{C}_{i}^{\prime}
is a collection of
\geqslant(1-\eta^{\prime}_{i})\alpha n/2
points from the submixture
\cup_{j\not\in F_{C(i)}}X_{j}
. We know that each
\mu_{i}
is of
\ell_{2}
norm at most
1/\alpha
, each
\Sigma_{i}
has spectral norm at most
1/\alpha
and that for every
r,r^{\prime}\not\in F_{C(i)}
,
\left\lVert\Sigma_{r}-\Sigma_{r^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leqslant\tau
. Further,
\Sigma_{r}
is at most
\tau+1/\alpha=O(\tau)
-different in Frobenius norm from the covariance of the sub-mixture. By an argument similar to the proof of Lemma
2.44
, we can establish that the submixture with components
r
such that
r\not\in F_{C(i)}
is
O(\tau)
-certifiably bounded variance. Since
\hat{C}_{i}^{\prime}
is a subset of this sub-mixture of size
\alpha n/2
, we immediately obtain that
\hat{C}_{i}^{\prime}
is
O(\tau/\alpha)
-certifiably bounded variance.
Thus, applying Lemma
7.7
with outlier parameter
\eta_{i}^{\prime}
to input
\hat{C}_{i}
yields an estimate
S_{i}
of the 2nd moment of
\hat{C}_{i}^{\prime}
within a Frobenius error of at most
O(\tau/\alpha)

From Lemma
7.8
, this is an additional
O(1/\alpha)
different in Frobenius norm from the 2nd moment of the sub-mixture which, as argued above, is itself at most
O(\tau)
different in Frobenius norm from
\Sigma_{i}
. Chaining together yields that
\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-S_{i}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leqslant O(\tau/\alpha)
for some constant
C
.

Since for every
r\in S,r^{\prime}\in T
it holds that
\left\lVert\Sigma_{r}-\Sigma_{r^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\gg\Omega(\tau/\alpha)
, conditioned on the good event above, our algorithm never merges
\hat{C}_{i}
and
\hat{C}_{j}
whenever
i,j
are non-outliers and
i
is in some cluster in
S
and
j
is in some cluster in
T
. On the other hand, if
i,j
belong to the same cluster, then, the corresponding estimate
\left\lVert S_{i}-S_{j}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leqslant 2C\tau
. Thus, our merging process always merges together any such candidates.

As a result, the output of the merging process can have at most one
i
from any true cluster – thus, the number of distinct members of
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
is at most
k
. We note that the running time is dominated by computing a pseudo distribution satisfying the union of all the constraints (Step 3 in Algorithm
7.2
) and requires
n^{O(t)}
time. Step 4 computes a degree
O(1)
sos relaxation for at most
O(\ell)
components and the merging only requires a fixed polynomial in
d
and
k
time.

∎

7.2
Proof of Lemma
7.4

In the following lemma, we show that the constraint system
\mathcal{A}
, via a low-degree sum-of-squares proof, implies that a lower bound on the variance of any degree
2
polynomial on
X^{\prime}
whenever the cluster
\hat{C}
(indicated by
z
) appreciably intersects two well-separated true clusters.

Lemma 7.5

(Lower-Bound on Variance of Degree 2 Polynomials)
.

Let
Q\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be any fixed matrix.
Then, for any
i,j\leqslant k
, and
z^{\prime}(X_{r})=\frac{1}{w_{r}n}\sum_{i\in X_{r}}z_{i}\cdot\bm{1}(y_{i}=x_{i})
, we have for any
r\neq r^{\prime}\in[k]
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{z}$}}}$}}}\Biggl\{z^{\prime}(X_{r})z^{\prime}(X_{r}^{\prime})\leqslant\frac{(32Ct/\alpha)^{2t}}{(\E_{X_{r}}Q-\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}Q)^{2t}}

\displaystyle\Biggl(\frac{\alpha^{4}}{w_{r}^{2}w_{r^{\prime}}^{2}}\left(\E_{z}(Q-\E_{z}Q)^{2}\right)^{t}

\displaystyle+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{w_{r}^{2}}\left(\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}(Q-\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}Q)^{2}\right)^{t}+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{w_{r^{\prime}}^{2}}\left(\E_{X_{r}}(Q-\E_{X_{r}}Q)^{2}\right)^{t}\Biggr)\Biggr\}\,.

Proof.

Let
z_{i}^{\prime}=z_{i}\bm{1}(y_{i}=x_{i})
for every
i
.
For every
1\leqslant r\leqslant k
, let
\E_{X_{r}}Q
denote the expectation of the homogenous degree
2
polynomial defined by
Q
:
\E_{X_{r}}Q=\frac{1}{w_{r}n}\sum_{i,j\in X_{r}}Q(x_{i})
for every
r
where
Q(x_{i})=x_{i}^{\top}Qx_{i}
. Similarly, let
\E_{z}Q
be the quadratic polynomial in
z
defined by
\E_{z}Q=\frac{1}{\alpha n}\sum_{i\leqslant n}z_{i}Q(x_{i})
.
Using the substitution rule and non-negativity of the
z_{i}^{\prime}
s, we have for any
r,r^{\prime}\in[k]
:

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{z}$}}}$}}}\Biggl\{\E_{z}(Q-\E_{z}Q)^{2t}&=\frac{1}{\alpha n}\sum_{i\in[n]}z_{i}\left(Q(x_{i})-\E_{z}Q\right)^{2t}\\
&\geqslant\frac{1}{\alpha n}\sum_{i\in X_{r}\cup X_{r^{\prime}}:x_{i}=y_{j}}z^{\prime}_{i}\left(Q(x_{i})-\E_{z}Q\right)^{2t}\Biggl\}\end{split}

(7.9)

Then, using the SoS almost triangle inequality (Fact
2.21
), we have:

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{z}$}}}$}}}\Biggl\{&\frac{1}{\alpha n}\sum_{i\in X_{r}\cup X_{r^{\prime}}}z^{\prime}_{i}\left(Q(x_{i})-\E_{z}Q\right)^{2t}\\
&\geqslant 2^{-2t}\left(\frac{1}{\alpha n}\sum_{i\in X_{r}:i}z^{\prime}_{i}\left(\E_{X_{r}}Q-\E_{z}Q\right)^{2t}-\frac{1}{\alpha n}\sum_{i\in X_{r}}z^{\prime}_{i}\left(Q(x_{i})-\E_{X_{r}}Q\right)^{2t}\right)\\
&\hskip 14.45377pt+2^{-2t}\left(\frac{1}{\alpha n}\sum_{i\in X_{r}:i:x_{i}=y_{i}}z^{\prime}_{i}\left(\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}Q-\E_{z}Q\right)^{2t}-\frac{1}{\alpha n}\sum_{i\in X_{r^{\prime}},x_{i}=y_{i}}z^{\prime}_{i}\left(Q(x_{i})-\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}Q\right)^{2t}\right)\\
&=2^{-2t}\left(\frac{w_{r}}{\alpha}z^{\prime}(X_{r})\left(\E_{X_{r}}Q-\E_{z}Q\right)^{2t}-\frac{1}{\alpha n}\sum_{i\in X_{r}}\left(Q(x_{i})-\E_{X_{r}}Q\right)^{2t}\right)\\
&\hskip 14.45377pt+2^{-2t}\left(\frac{w_{r^{\prime}}}{\alpha}z^{\prime}(X_{r^{\prime}})\left(\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}Q-\E_{z}Q\right)^{2t}-\frac{1}{\alpha n}\sum_{i\in X_{r^{\prime}}}\left(Q(x_{i})-\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}Q\right)^{2t}\right)\Biggl\}\end{split}

(7.10)

Next, observe that by the SoS almost triangle inequality (Fact
2.21
), we must have:

\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}$}}}\left\{\left(\E_{X_{r}}Q-\E_{z}Q\right)^{2t}+\left(\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}Q-\E_{z}Q\right)^{2t}\geqslant 2^{-2t}\left(\E_{X_{r}}Q-\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}Q\right)^{2t}\right\}\,.

Further, note that
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{20.53386pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-20.53386pt\raisebox{-7.21112pt}{\makebox[20.53386pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(1)}$}}}\hskip-20.53386pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[20.53386pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}$}}}\left\{\frac{w_{r}}{\alpha}z^{\prime}(X_{r})+\frac{w_{r^{\prime}}}{\alpha}z^{\prime}(X_{r^{\prime}})\leqslant\frac{1}{\alpha n}\sum_{i}z_{i}\leqslant 1\right\}
. Thus, using Fact
4.8
with
A=\frac{w_{r}}{\alpha}z^{\prime}(X_{r})
,
B=\frac{w_{r^{\prime}}}{\alpha}z^{\prime}(X_{r^{\prime}})
,
C=\left(\E_{X_{r}}Q-\E_{z}Q\right)^{2t}
, and
D=\left(\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}Q-\E_{z}Q\right)^{2t}
and
\tau=2^{-2t}\left(\E_{X_{r}}Q-\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}Q\right)^{2t}
, we can derive:

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.10762pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.10762pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[8.10762pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4}$}}}\hskip-8.10762pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.10762pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{z}$}}}$}}}\Biggl\{(Ct/\alpha)^{2t}\left(\E_{z}(Q-\E_{z}Q)^{2}\right)^{t}&\geqslant\E_{z}(Q-\E_{z}Q)^{2t}\geqslant 2^{-6t}\frac{w_{r}w_{r^{\prime}}}{\alpha^{2}}z^{\prime}(X_{r})z^{\prime}(X_{r^{\prime}})\left(\E_{X_{r}}Q-\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}Q\right)^{2t}\\
&-2^{-6t}\frac{w_{r}}{\alpha}\E_{X_{r}}\left(Q-\E_{X_{r}}Q\right)^{2t}-2^{-6t}\frac{w_{r^{\prime}}}{\alpha}\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}\left(Q-\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}Q\right)^{2t}\\
&\geqslant 2^{-6t}\frac{w_{r}w_{r^{\prime}}}{\alpha^{2}}z^{\prime}(X_{r})z^{\prime}(X_{r^{\prime}})(\E_{X_{r}}Q-\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}Q)^{2t}-\frac{w_{r}}{\alpha}(Ct/\alpha)^{2t}\left(\E_{X_{r}}\left(Q-\E_{X_{r}}Q\right)^{2}\right)^{t}\\
&-\frac{w_{r^{\prime}}}{\alpha}(Ct/\alpha)^{2t}\left(\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}\left(Q-\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}Q\right)^{2}\right)^{t}\Biggl\}\end{split}

(7.11)

where the first inequality uses the Certifiable Hypercontractivity constraints (
\mathcal{A}_{4}
) and the last inequality follows from the Certifiable Hypercontractivity of
X_{r}
and
X_{r^{\prime}}
(Condition
2.47
).
Rearranging completes the proof.

∎

We can use the lemma above to obtain a simultaneous intersection bound guarantee when there are relative Frobenius separated components in the mixture.

Lemma 7.6

(Lemma
4.6
, restated)
.

Suppose
\left\lVert\Sigma^{-1/2}(\Sigma_{r}-\Sigma_{r^{\prime}})\Sigma^{-1/2}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\geqslant 10^{8}\frac{C^{6}t^{4}}{\beta^{2/t}\alpha^{4}}
. Then, for
z(X_{r})=\frac{1}{w_{r}n}\sum_{i\in X_{r}}z_{i}\cdot\bm{1}(y_{i}=x_{i})
,

\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}$}}}\left\{z(X_{r})z(X_{r^{\prime}})\leqslant\beta\right\}\,.

Proof.

WLOG, we will work with the transformed points
x_{i}\rightarrow\Sigma^{-1/2}x_{i}
where
\Sigma
is the covariance of the mixture. Note that our algorithm does not need to know
\Sigma
– this transformation is only for simplifying notation in the analysis that follows.

Let
\tilde{\Sigma}_{z}=\Sigma^{-1/2}\Sigma_{z}\Sigma^{-1/2}
,
\tilde{\Sigma}_{r}=\Sigma^{-1/2}\Sigma_{r}\Sigma^{-1/2}
and
\tilde{\Sigma}_{r^{\prime}}=\Sigma^{-1/2}\Sigma_{r^{\prime}}\Sigma^{-1/2}
be the transformed covariances. Then, notice that
\left\lVert\tilde{\Sigma}_{r}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{w_{r}}\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\frac{1.5}{w_{r}}
and
\left\lVert\tilde{\Sigma}_{r^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{w_{r^{\prime}}}\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\frac{1.5}{w_{r^{\prime}}}
.

We now apply Lemma
4.10
with
Q=\tilde{\Sigma}_{r}-\tilde{\Sigma}_{r^{\prime}}
. Then, notice that
\E_{X_{r}}Q-\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}Q=\left\lVert\tilde{\Sigma}_{r}-\tilde{\Sigma}_{r^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}+\mu_{r}^{\top}(\tilde{\Sigma}_{r}-\tilde{\Sigma_{r^{\prime}}})\mu_{r}-\mu_{r^{\prime}}^{\top}(\tilde{\Sigma}_{r}-\tilde{\Sigma_{r^{\prime}}})\mu_{r^{\prime}}\geqslant\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}-\frac{4}{\alpha}
. Then, we obtain:

\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{z}$}}}$}}}\Biggl\{z(X_{r})z(X_{r^{\prime}})\\
\leqslant\left(\frac{32Ct/\alpha}{\E_{X_{r}}Q-\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}Q}\right)^{2t}\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}}{w_{r}w_{r^{\prime}}}\left(\E_{z}(Q-\E_{z}Q)^{2}\right)^{t}+\frac{\alpha}{w_{r}}\left(\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}(Q-\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}Q)^{2}\right)^{t}+\frac{\alpha}{w_{r^{\prime}}}\left(\E_{X_{r}}(Q-\E_{X_{r}}Q)^{2}\right)\right)^{t}\Biggr\}\,.

(7.12)

Since
X_{r}
and
X_{r^{\prime}}
have certifiably
C
-bounded variance polynomials for
C=4
(as a consequence of Condition
2.47
and Fact
2.45
followed by an application of Lemma
2.25
), we have:

\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}(Q-\E_{X_{r^{\prime}}}Q)^{2}\leqslant{6\left\lVert{\tilde{\Sigma}_{r^{\prime}}}^{1/2}Q{\tilde{\Sigma}_{r^{\prime}}}^{1/2}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}}\leqslant\frac{10}{w_{r^{\prime}}^{2}}\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leqslant\frac{10}{\alpha^{2}}\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\;,

and

\E_{X_{r}}(Q-\E_{X_{r}}Q)^{2}\leqslant{6\left\lVert{\tilde{\Sigma}_{r}}^{1/2}Q{\tilde{\Sigma}_{r}}^{1/2}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}}\leqslant\frac{10}{w_{r}^{2}}\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leqslant\frac{10}{\alpha^{2}}\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\,.

Finally, using the bounded-variance constraints in
\mathcal{A}
, we have:

\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{16.6693pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-16.6693pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[16.6693pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4}$}}}\hskip-16.6693pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[16.6693pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q,z}$}}}$}}}\E(Q-\E_{z}Q)^{2}\leqslant\frac{10}{\alpha^{2}}\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\,.

Plugging these estimates back in (
7.12
) yields:

\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.10762pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.10762pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[8.10762pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4}$}}}\hskip-8.10762pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.10762pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{z}$}}}$}}}\Biggl\{z(X_{r})z(X_{r^{\prime}})\leqslant\frac{(1000Ct/\alpha)^{2t}}{\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2t}\alpha^{2t}}\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}}{w_{r}}+\frac{\alpha}{w_{r^{\prime}}}+\frac{\alpha}{w_{r}w_{r^{\prime}}}\right)\leqslant\frac{3}{w_{r}w_{r^{\prime}}}\frac{(1000Ct)^{2t}}{\alpha^{2t}\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2t}}\leqslant\frac{3(1000Ct)^{2t}}{\alpha^{2t}\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2t}}\Biggr\}\,.

(7.13)

Plugging in the lower bound on
\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2t}
and applying cancellation within SoS (Fact
4.9
) completes the proof.
∎

7.3
2nd Moment Estimation Subroutine

The following lemma gives a 2nd moment estimation algorithm with error in Frobenius norm for distributions that have a certifiably bounded covariance. The proof is very similar to the SoS based mean and covariance estimation algorithms but we provide it in full for completeness here.

Lemma 7.7

(2nd Moment Estimation in Frobenius Norm)
.

Let
1/100\geqslant\eta>0
. There is an
n^{O(1)}
time algorithm that takes input an
\eta
-corruption
Y
of an sample
X
of size
n
and outputs an estimate
M_{2}
of the 2nd moment of
X
with the following properties:
Let
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a collection of
n
points satisfying
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\left\{\frac{1}{|X|}\sum_{x\in X}\left(Q(x)-\frac{1}{|X|}Q(x)\right)^{2}\leqslant C\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\right\}
for a matrix-valued indeterminate
Q
. Let
M_{2}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{x\in X}xx^{\top}
. Then, the estimate
\hat{M_{2}}
output by the algorithm satisfies:

\left\lVert\hat{M_{2}}-M_{2}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leqslant 80C\eta\,.

Proof.

Consider the constraint system with scalar-valued indeterminates
z_{i}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
and
d
-dimensional vector-valued indeterminates
x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{n}
with the following set of constraints:

\mathcal{A}=

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\forall i\leqslant n&z_{i}^{2}&=z_{i}\\
&&\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}&=(1-\eta)n&\\
&&\tilde{M}_{2}&=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x^{\prime}_{i}{x_{i}^{\prime}}^{\top}\\
&\forall i\leqslant n&z_{i}x_{i}^{\prime}&=z_{i}y_{i}\\
&&\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left({x^{\prime}}_{i}^{\top}Qx^{\prime}_{i}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{x^{\prime}}_{i}^{\top}Qx^{\prime}_{i}\right)^{2}&\leqslant C\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\end{aligned}\right\}

(7.14)

Observe that
X^{\prime}=X
and
z_{i}
set to the
0
-
1
indicator of non-outliers satisfies the constraint system. Thus, the constraints are feasible.

Our algorithm finds a pseudo-distribution
\tilde{\zeta}
of degree
10
satisfying the above constraints and output
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{M}_{2}]
. Let us now analyze this algorithm.
The key is the following statement that gives a sum-of-squares proof of closeness of
\tilde{M}_{2}
and
M_{2}
in Frobenius norm.
We use the notation
\E_{X}Q
and
\E_{X^{\prime}}Q
to abbreviate
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{\top}Qx_{i}
and
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{x^{\prime}}_{i}^{\top}Qx^{\prime}_{i}
respectively.

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\Biggl\{

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{\top}Qx_{i}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{x^{\prime}}_{i}^{\top}Qx^{\prime}_{i}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-z_{i}\bm{1}(x_{i}=y_{i}))x_{i}^{\top}Qx_{i}-{x^{\prime}}_{i}^{\top}Qx^{\prime}_{i}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\frac{1}{n}(1-z_{i}\bm{1}(x_{i}=y_{i}))^{2}\right)\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(x_{i}^{\top}Qx_{i}-{x^{\prime}}_{i}^{\top}Qx^{\prime}_{i}\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant 20\eta\cdot\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(x_{i}^{\top}Qx_{i}-\E_{X}Q\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left({x^{\prime}}_{i}^{\top}Qx^{\prime}_{i}-\E_{X^{\prime}}Q\right)^{2}+\left(\E_{X}Q-\E_{X^{\prime}}Q\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant 20\eta(2C\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2})+20\eta\left(\E_{X}Q-\E_{X^{\prime}}Q\right)^{2}\Biggr\}

where the first inequality follows by the SoS version of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the 2nd by the SoS version of the Almost Triangle inequality.

Rearranging and using that
1-20\eta>1/2
now yields that:

\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\Biggl\{\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{\top}Qx_{i}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{x^{\prime}}_{i}^{\top}Qx^{\prime}_{i}\right)^{2}\leqslant 80C\eta\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\Biggr\}

Notice that the LHS above equals the linear polynomial
\langle\tilde{M}_{2}-M_{2},Q\rangle
.
We now plug in
Q=\tilde{M}_{2}-M_{2}
to obtain:

\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\Biggl\{\left\lVert\tilde{M}_{2}-M_{2}\right\rVert_{F}^{4}\leqslant 80C\eta\left\lVert\tilde{M}_{2}-M_{2}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\Biggr\}

Applying Fact
2.24
yields:

\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\Biggl\{\left\lVert\tilde{M}_{2}-M_{2}\right\rVert_{F}^{8}\leqslant 80^{4}C^{4}\eta^{4}\Biggr\}

Taking pseudo-expectations with respect to
\tilde{\zeta}
and using Hölder’s inequality for pseudo-distributions yields that

\left\lVert\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}\tilde{M}_{2}-M_{2}\right\rVert_{F}^{8}\leqslant\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}\left\lVert\tilde{M}_{2}-M_{2}\right\rVert_{F}^{8}\leqslant 80^{4}C^{4}\eta^{4}\,.

Taking the
4
-th root, we can conlcude our rounded value
\hat{M}_{2}=\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}\tilde{M}_{2}
satisfies:

\left\lVert\hat{M}_{2}-M_{2}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leqslant 80C\eta\,.

This completes the proof.

∎

We also note the following simple consequence of the certifiable bounded variance property that follows via an argument similar to the one employed in the proof of the previous lemma.

Lemma 7.8

(Subsamples of Bounded-Variance Distributions)
.

Let
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a collection of
n
points satisfying
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\left\{\frac{1}{|X|}\sum_{x\in X}(Q(x)-\frac{1}{|X|}Q(x))^{2}\leqslant C\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\right\}
for a matrix-valued indeterminate
Q
. Let
M_{2}=\frac{1}{|X|}\sum_{x\in X}xx^{\top}
be the 2nd moment of
X
. Let
S\subseteq X
be a subset of size at least
\beta|X|
. Then,

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{7.98607pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-7.98607pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[7.98607pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4}$}}}\hskip-7.98607pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[7.98607pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}$}}}\left\{\left\lVert\frac{1}{|S|}\sum_{x\in S}xx^{\top}-M_{2}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{\beta}\right\}\,.

Proof.

We have by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality:

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\Biggl\{\left(\frac{1}{|X|}\sum_{x\in X}\bm{1}(x\in S)(x^{\top}Qx-M_{2}),Q\rangle)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\frac{1}{|X|}\bm{1}(x\in S)^{2}\right)\left(\sum_{x\in X}(x^{\top}Qx-M_{2}),Q\rangle)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\frac{|S|}{|X|}\right)\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\,.

We now substitute in
Q=\frac{1}{|X|}\sum_{x\in X}\bm{1}(x\in S)(x^{\top}Qx-M_{2}
to obtain:

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\Biggl\{\left\lVert\frac{1}{|X|}\sum_{x\in X}\bm{1}(x\in S)(x^{\top}Qx-M_{2})\right\rVert_{F}^{4}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\frac{|S|}{|X|}\right)\left\lVert\frac{1}{|X|}\sum_{x\in X}\bm{1}(x\in S)(x^{\top}Qx-M_{2})\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\,.

We now apply Fact
2.24
to yield:

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\Biggl\{\left\lVert\frac{1}{|X|}\sum_{x\in X}\bm{1}(x\in S)(x^{\top}Qx-M_{2})\right\rVert_{F}^{8}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\frac{|S|}{|X|}\right)^{4}\,.

We finally apply Fact
4.9
to conclude that:

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\Biggl\{\left\lVert\frac{1}{|X|}\sum_{x\in X}\bm{1}(x\in S)(x^{\top}Qx-M_{2})\right\rVert_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\frac{|S|}{|X|}\right)\,.

Rescaling gives the claim.
∎

8
Getting
\poly(\varepsilon)
-close in TV Distance: Proof of Theorem
1.5

Theorem 8.1

(Robustly Learning
k
-Mixtures with small error)
.

Given
0<\varepsilon<1/k^{k^{O(k^{2})}}
and a multiset
Y=\{y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{n}\}
of
n
i.i.d.
samples from a distribution
F
such that
d_{\mathsf{TV}}(F,\mathcal{M})\leqslant\varepsilon
,
for an unknown
k
-mixture of Gaussians
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i\leqslant k}w_{i}\mathcal{N}\left(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i}\right)
,
where
n\geqslant n_{0}=d^{O(k)}\poly_{k}(1/\varepsilon)
, there exists an algorithm that runs
in time
n^{O(1)}\poly_{k}\left(1/\varepsilon\right)

and with probability at least
0.99
outputs a hypothesis
k
-mixture of Gaussians

\widehat{\mathcal{M}}=\sum_{i\leqslant k}\hat{w}_{i}\mathcal{N}\left(\hat{\mu_{i}},\hat{\Sigma}_{i}\right)

such that
d_{\textrm{TV}}\left(\mathcal{M},\widehat{\mathcal{M}}\right)=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{c_{k}}\right)
,
with
c_{k}=1/(100^{k}C^{(k+1)!}k!\mathrm{sf}(k+1))
, where
C>0
is a universal constant
and
\mathrm{sf}(k)=\Pi_{i\in[k]}(k-i)!
is the super-factorial function.

In order to obtain the above theorem, we require recovering a polynomial sized list of candidate parameters, in addition to the efficient partial clustering result we obtained in the previous section.
To this end, we show the following list-recovery theorem which is similar to Theorem
3.1
, but the algorithm outputs a polynomial-size list instead.

Theorem 8.2

(Recovering a small list of candidate parameters)
.

Fix any
\alpha>\varepsilon>0,\Delta>0
.
Let
X
, a sample from a
k
-mixture of Gaussians

\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
satisfying Condition
2.47

with parameters
\gamma=\varepsilon d^{-8k}k^{-Ck}
, for
C
a sufficiently large universal constant,
and
t=8k
, and let
Y
be an
\varepsilon
-corruption of
X
. Let
X^{\prime}
be a set of
n^{\prime}=O\left(\varepsilon\eta/\left(k^{5}\left(\Delta^{4}+1/\alpha^{4}\right)\right)\right)^{-4ck}
fresh samples from
\cal M
and
Z
be an
\varepsilon
-corruptionn of
X^{\prime}
.
If
w_{i}\geqslant\alpha
,
\left\lVert\mu_{i}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\frac{2}{\sqrt{\alpha}}
and
\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-I\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\Delta
for every
i\in[k]
,
then, given
k,Y,Z
and
\varepsilon
, the algorithm outputs a list
L
of at most

\ell^{\prime}=O\left(\left(k^{5}\left(\Delta^{4}+1/\alpha^{4}\right)\right)^{4k}/\eta^{4k}\right)

candidate hypotheses (component means and covariances),
such that with probability at least
99/100

there exist
\{\hat{\mu}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}\subseteq L
satisfying

\left\lVert\mu_{i}-\hat{\mu}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\frac{\Delta^{1/2}}{\alpha}\right)\eta^{G(k)}
and

\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-\hat{\Sigma}_{i}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(k^{4}\right)\frac{\Delta^{1/2}}{\alpha}\eta^{G(k)}
, for all
i\in[k]
.
Here,
\eta=(2k)^{4k}\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(1/\alpha+\Delta\right)^{4k}\varepsilon^{1/(k^{O(k^{2})})}
and
G(k)=\frac{1}{C^{k+1}(k+1)!}
.
The running time of the algorithm is
\poly(|L|,{|Y|,d^{k}})\cdot\poly_{k}(1/\varepsilon)
.

8.1
Proof of Theorem
8.2

We use the following notation and background from Moitra-Valiant
[
MV10
]
:

Definition 8.3

(Statistically Learnable)
.

Given
\varepsilon>0
, we call a mixture of Gaussians
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})

\varepsilon
-statistically learnable if
\min_{i}w_{i}\geqslant\varepsilon
and
\min_{i\neq j}d_{TV}(\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i}),\mathcal{N}(\mu_{j},\Sigma_{j}))\geqslant\varepsilon
.

Definition 8.4

(Correct Subdivision)
.

Given a Gaussian mixture of
k
Gaussians,
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i}w_{i}N(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
and a mixture of
k^{\prime}\leqslant k
Gaussians
\hat{\mathcal{M}}=\sum_{i}\hat{w}_{i}N(\hat{\mu}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}_{i})
, we call
\hat{\mathcal{M}}
an
\varepsilon
-correct subdivision of
\mathcal{M}
if there is a function
\pi:[k]\to[k^{\prime}]
that is onto and

1.

\forall j\in[k^{\prime}],\left|\sum_{i:\pi(i)=j}w_{i}-\hat{w}_{j}\right|\leqslant\varepsilon

2.

\forall i\in[k],\left\|\mu_{i}-\hat{\mu}_{\pi(i)}\right\|+\left\|\Sigma_{i}-\hat{\Sigma}_{\pi(i)}\right\|_{F}\leqslant\varepsilon
.

Theorem 8.5

(Theorem 8 in
[
MV10
]
)
.

Given an
\varepsilon
-statistically learnable Gaussian mixture
\mathcal{M}
in isotropic position, for some
\varepsilon>0
, there exists an algorithm that requires
n=\poly(d/\varepsilon)
samples and runs in time
O(\poly_{k}(n))
and with probability at least
99/100
recovers an
\varepsilon
-correct sub-division
\hat{\mathcal{M}}
. Let the corresponding algorithm be referred to as PARTITION PURSUIT.

The algorithm has two steps: first run the first three steps of Algorithm 3.2 to get the list
L^{\prime}
of
\hat{S}
and
V^{\prime}_{\hat{S}}
