; then apply the following proposition to learn the mixture in the subspace
V^{\prime}_{\hat{S}}
. This proposition is a generalization of Theorem
8.5
without the assumption that the total variation distance between each pair of components is at least
\varepsilon
. The sample and time complexities has a worse, but still polynomial dependence on
\varepsilon
. Note that although the algorithm in the proposition is non-robust, we can take a sample without noise with constant probability because the algorithm only requires a polynomial number of samples in
\varepsilon
.

Algorithm 8.6

(Efficient List-Recovery of Candidate Parameters)
.

Input:

An
\varepsilon
-corruption
Y
of a sample
X
from a
k
-mixture of
Gaussians
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
. Let Z be an additional
\varepsilon
-corrupted sample of size
n^{\prime}
from
\mathcal{M}
.

Requirements:

The guarantees of the algorithm hold if the mixture parameters
and the sample
X
satisfy:

1.

w_{i}\geqslant\alpha
for all
i\in[k]
,

2.

\left\lVert\mu_{i}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant 2/\sqrt{\alpha}
for all
i\in[k]
,

3.

\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-I\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\Delta
for all
i\in[k]
.

4.

X
satisfies Condition
2.47
with parameters
(\gamma,t)
,
where
\gamma=\varepsilon d^{-8k}k^{-Ck}
, for
C
a sufficiently large universal constant, and
t=8k
.

5.

The number of fresh samples
n^{\prime}=O\left(\varepsilon\eta/\left(k^{5}\left(\Delta^{4}+1/\alpha^{4}\right)\right)\right)^{-4ck}
, for a fixed constant
c
.

Parameters:

\eta=(2k)^{4k}\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(1/\alpha+\Delta\right)^{4k}\varepsilon^{1/(k^{O(k^{2})})}
,
D=C(k^{4}/(\alpha\sqrt{\eta}))
,

\delta=2\eta^{1/(C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}
,
\ell^{\prime}=100\log k\left(\eta/\left(k^{5}\left(\Delta^{4}+1/\alpha^{4}\right)\right)\right)^{-4k}
,
for some sufficiently large absolute constant
C>0
,
\lambda=4\eta
,
\phi=10(1+\Delta^{2})/(\sqrt{\eta}\alpha^{5})
,
\varepsilon_{1}=O\left(\sqrt{\Delta}\delta^{1/4}/\alpha\right)
.

Output:

A list
L
of hypotheses such that there exists at least one,
\{\hat{\mu}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}_{i}\}_{i\leqslant k}\in L
, satisfying:
\left\lVert\mu_{i}-\hat{\mu}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\frac{\Delta^{1/2}}{\alpha}\right)\eta^{G(k)}
and

\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-\hat{\Sigma}_{i}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(k^{4}\right)\frac{\Delta^{1/2}}{\alpha}\eta^{G(k)}
, where
G(k)=\frac{1}{C^{k+1}(k+1)!}
.

Operation:

1.

Robust Estimation of Hermite Tensors
: For
m\in[4k]
, compute
\hat{T}_{m}
such that
\max_{m\in[4k]}\left\lVert\hat{T}_{m}-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left[h_{m}(\mathcal{M})\right]\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\eta
using the robust mean estimation algorithm in Fact
2.36
.

2.

Random Collapsing of Two Modes of
\hat{T}_{4}
: Let
L^{\prime}
be an empty list. Repeat
\ell^{\prime}
times:
For
j\in[4k]
, choose independent standard Gaussians in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, denoted by
x^{(j)},y^{(j)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)
,
and uniform draws
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{t}
from
[-D,D]
. Let
\hat{S}
be a
d\times d
matrix such that
for all
r,s\in[d]
,
\hat{S}(r,s)=\sum_{j\in[4k]}a_{j}\hat{T}_{4}(r,s,x^{(j)},y^{(j)})=\sum_{j\in[4k]}a_{j}\sum_{g,h\in[d]}\hat{T}_{4}(r,s,g,h)x^{(j)}(g)y^{(j)}(h)
.
Add
\hat{S}
to the list
L^{\prime}
.

3.

Construct Low-Dimensional Subspace
: Let
V
be the span of all singular vectors of the natural
d\times d^{m-1}
flattening of
\hat{T}_{m}
with singular values
\geqslant\lambda
for
m\leqslant 4k
. For each
\hat{S}\in L^{\prime}
, let
V^{\prime}_{\hat{S}}
be the span of
V
plus all the singular vectors of
\hat{S}
with singular value larger than
\delta^{1/4}
.

4.

Moitra-Valiant for Low-Dimensional Subspace
: Initialize
L
to be the empty list. For each
\hat{S}\in L^{\prime}
, let
\hat{P}=UU^{\top}
be the orthogonal projection matrix onto the span of
V^{\prime}_{\hat{S}}
, where
U\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
has orthonormal columns. Let
m=\mathrm{dim}(V^{\prime}_{\hat{S}})
and let
\hat{Z}\subset Z
be a randomly chosen subset of size
\poly(m/\varepsilon_{1})
.
Let
U_{m}
denote the first
m
columns of
U
and for all
z\in\hat{Z}
, compute
U_{m}^{\top}z
. Run PARTITION PURSUIT on the resulting set of points and let
\{\hat{\mu}^{\hat{P}}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}^{\hat{P}}_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
be the parameters corresponding to the
\varepsilon
-correct subdivision output by PARTITION PURSUIT. Let
\hat{\mu}_{i}^{\top}=[(\hat{\mu}^{\hat{P}}_{i})^{\top},0]
be a
d
dimensional vector padded with
0
s and
\hat{\Sigma}_{i}
be a
d\times d
matrix with
\hat{\Sigma}^{\hat{P}}_{i}
in the top left
m\times m
sub-matrix and
0
’s elsewhere. Add
\{U\hat{\mu}_{i},U\hat{\Sigma}_{i}U^{\top}+(\hat{S}+I)-\hat{P}\left(I+S\right)\hat{P}\}_{i\in[k]}
to
L
.

Proposition 8.7
.

Given
\varepsilon>0
and a sample
X
of size
\poly(d,1/\varepsilon)
from a
k
-mixture of Gaussians
\mathcal{M}
with mixture covariance
\Sigma
such that
0.99I\preceq\Sigma\preceq 1.01I

and satisfies
w_{i}\geqslant\varepsilon
, the PARTITION PURSUIT algorithm runs in time
\poly(d,1/\varepsilon)
and with probability at least
9/10

returns an
O(\varepsilon)
-correct sub-division, denoted by
\hat{\mathcal{M}}
.

Recall, the PARTITION PURSUIT algorithm satisfies Theorem
8.5
and we will prove that with an appropriately chosen parameter
\varepsilon
, PARTITION PURSUIT also satisfies Proposition
8.7
. The main idea is that if any two components are actually close enough in total variation distance, then any algorithm with access to only a polynomial number of samples could never distinguish these two components from a single Gaussian. So if all pairwise distances are either sufficiently large or sufficiently small, the algorithm will behave as if it were given sample access to a mixture that meets the requirements of Theorem
8.5
.

Lemma 8.8
.

Given
0<\gamma,\delta<1
and two distributions,
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}
over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
d_{TV}(\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{2})<\gamma
, let
X_{1}
be set of
n
i.i.d. samples from
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
X_{2}
be
n
i.i.d. samples from
\mathcal{D}_{2}
. Let
\mathcal{A}
be any algorithm that takes as input
X_{1}
and outputs a list of
m
real numbers,
Y_{1}=\{y_{i}\}_{i\in[m]}
, such that
y_{i}\in[-1,1]
with probability at least
1-\delta
. Then, for any
\tau>0
,
\mathcal{A}
on input
X_{2}
outputs a list of
m
real numbers
Y_{2}=\{y^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in[m]}
such that with probability at least
1-\delta-(4mn\gamma/\tau)
, for all
i\in[m]
,
\left|y_{i}-y^{\prime}_{i}\right|\leqslant\tau
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{U}_{1}
be the uniform distribution over
X_{1}
and
\mathcal{U}_{2}
be the uniform distribution over
X_{2}
. Then,

\begin{split}d_{TV}\left(\mathcal{U}_{1},\mathcal{U}_{2}\right)\leqslant\sqrt{2}H^{2}\left(\mathcal{U}_{1},\mathcal{U}_{2}\right)&=\sqrt{2}nH^{2}\left(\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{2}\right)\\
&\leqslant\sqrt{2}nd_{TV}\left(\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{2}\right)\\
&\leqslant\sqrt{2}\gamma n\end{split}

(8.1)

Consider the family of functions
\mathcal{F}
that take as input
n
samples and output a single bit in
\{0,1\}
. We know that for any function
f\in\mathcal{F}
, the probability that
f(X_{1})\neq f(X_{2})
is at most
\sqrt{2}\gamma n
. Recall, the algorithm outputs
m
real numbers in the range
[-1,1]
, which we can discretize into a grid
\Delta
of length
\tau
. There are at most
2/\tau
distinct grid points and for any
y_{i}\in[-1,1]
, there exists a point
z_{i}\in\Delta
such that
\left|y_{i}-z_{i}\right|\leqslant\tau
. Further, observe we can represent each
y_{i}
using
2/\tau
functions
f\in\mathcal{F}
. Then, union bounding over the events that each of the
2/\tau
functions output different bits, for each of the
m
parameters, we have that with probabiltiy at least
1-\left(2\sqrt{2}\gamma nm/\tau\right)
, any algorithm outputs a list
\{y_{i}^{\prime}\}_{i^{\prime}\in[m]}
such that
\left|y_{i}-y^{\prime}_{i}\right|\leqslant\tau
. Finally, union bounding over the event that algorithm
\mathcal{A}
fails with probability
\delta
yields the claim.
∎

We then prove there is a gap
[f(d,\varepsilon_{1}),\varepsilon_{1})
between pairwise distances of components so that if we merge components within distance
f(d,\varepsilon_{1})
, the resulting mixture is
\varepsilon_{1}
-statistically learnable.

Lemma 8.9
.

Let
f(d)(\varepsilon)=f(d,\varepsilon)
. There exists
\ell\in[k^{2}]
such that for every pair of components, either
d_{TV}(\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i}),\mathcal{N}(\mu_{j},\Sigma_{j}))<(f(d))^{\ell}(\varepsilon)
or
d_{TV}(\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i}),\mathcal{N}(\mu_{j},\Sigma_{j}))\geqslant(f(d))^{\ell-1}(\varepsilon)
. Moreover, the set of Gaussians with total variation distance at most
(f(d))^{\ell}(\varepsilon)
is an equivalence class.

Proof.

We can see that intervals
\left\{\left[(f(d))^{\ell}(\varepsilon),(f(d))^{\ell-1}(\varepsilon)\right)\right\}_{\ell\in[k^{2}]}
are disjoint. There are at most
k^{2}-1
distinct values of
d_{TV}(\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i}),\mathcal{N}(\mu_{j},\Sigma_{j}))
. So there exists an interval
\left[(f(d))^{\ell}(\varepsilon),(f(d))^{\ell-1}(\varepsilon)\right)
such that for every pair of components
\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i}),\mathcal{N}(\mu_{j},\Sigma_{j})
, either
d_{TV}(\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i}),\mathcal{N}(\mu_{j},\Sigma_{j}))<(f(d))^{\ell}(\varepsilon)
or
d_{TV}(\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i}),\mathcal{N}(\mu_{j},\Sigma_{j}))\geqslant(f(d))^{\ell-1}(\varepsilon)
.

Next, we show for any
\ell
, Gaussians with pair wise TV distance
(f(d)^{\ell})(\varepsilon)
form an equivalence class. Consider component Gaussians
G_{1},G_{2}
and
G_{3}
such that
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
are at total variation distance at most
(f(d))^{\ell}(\varepsilon)
and
G_{2}
and
G_{3}
are also at total variation distance at most
(f(d))^{\ell}(\varepsilon)
.

\begin{split}d_{TV}(G_{1},G_{3})&\leqslant d_{TV}(G_{1},G_{2})+d_{TV}(G_{2},G_{3})\\
&\leqslant 2(f(d))^{\ell}(\varepsilon)\\
&\ll(f(d))^{\ell-1}(\varepsilon)\end{split}

and since there is no pair of Gaussians with total variation distance inside the interval
\left[(f(d))^{\ell}(\varepsilon),(f(d))^{\ell-1}(\varepsilon)\right)
, this implies
d_{TV}(G_{1},G_{3})\leqslant(f(d))^{\ell}(\varepsilon)
.
∎

We can now complete the proof of Proposition
8.7
:

Proof of Proposition
8.7
.

By Lemma
8.9
, there exists an interval
\left[(f(d))^{\ell}(\varepsilon),(f(d))^{\ell-1}(\varepsilon)\right)
such that there is no pair of Gaussians with total variation distance inside the interval and
(f(d))^{\ell}(\varepsilon),(f(d))^{\ell-1}(\varepsilon)
are polynomials in
d
and
\varepsilon
. Let
\varepsilon_{1}=(f(d))^{\ell-1}(\varepsilon)
and
f(d,\varepsilon_{1})=(f(d))^{\ell}(\varepsilon)
. Let
X
be a set of
n=(d/\varepsilon)^{c}
samples from
\mathcal{M}
, where
c
is fixed universal constant. Let
\bar{\mathcal{M}}
be the mixture obtained by merging all components in an equivalence class with total variation distance at most
f(d,\varepsilon_{1})
to a single Gaussian and observe
d_{TV}\left(\mathcal{M},\bar{\mathcal{M}}\right)\leqslant kf(d,\varepsilon_{1})
. Next, observe that PARTITION PURSUIT outputs at most
k
means and covariances, which can be represented as a list of at most
2kd^{2}
real numbers. Further, since
\Sigma\preceq 1.01I
and
w_{i}\geqslant\varepsilon
, the means of each component
\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant 2/\varepsilon
and
\|\Sigma_{i}\|_{F}^{2}\leqslant O(d^{2}/\varepsilon)
.

Then, rescaling the instance by
O(\varepsilon/d^{2})
and applying Lemma
8.8
with
\mathcal{D}_{1}=\mathcal{M}
,
\mathcal{D}_{2}=\bar{\mathcal{M}}
, input samples
X
and accuracy parameter
\tau=(\varepsilon/d)^{c_{2}}
, for a large enough constant
c_{2}
, it follows that with probability at least

1-0.99-O(f(d,\varepsilon_{1})\cdot(\varepsilon/d)^{c_{3}})
, for a fixed constant
c_{3}
, the resulting list of numbers is
\tau
-close to that obtained by running PARTITION PURSUIT on a set of
n
samples from
\bar{\mathcal{M}}
. Since
\bar{\mathcal{M}}
is
\varepsilon_{1}
-statistically learnable, it follows from Theorem
8.5
that with probability at least
9/10
, PARTITION PURSUIT will output an
O(\varepsilon_{1})
-correct sub-division
\hat{\mathcal{M}}
.

∎

Proof of Theorem
8.2
.

Recall, by part (1) of Proposition
3.5
, the dimension of the subspace
V^{\prime}_{\hat{S}}
is
m=\mathrm{dim}(V^{\prime}_{\hat{S}})=O\left(\frac{\left(k(1+\Delta+1/\alpha)\right)^{4k+5}}{\eta^{2}}\right)
and let
\varepsilon_{1}=\sqrt{\Delta}\delta^{1/4}/\alpha
. Let
c_{mv}
be a fixed constant such that
\left(m/\varepsilon_{1}\right)^{c_{mv}}
samples suffice for applying Theorem
8.5
. Further, obseve in the fresh sample
Y
, the probability that any given sample is corrupted is
\varepsilon
. Let
\zeta
be the event that a random subset of
\left(m/\varepsilon_{1}\right)^{c_{mv}}
samples from
Z
does not contain any corrupted points. Then, the event
\zeta
holds with probability at least
\left(1-\varepsilon\right)^{\left(m/\varepsilon_{1}\right)^{c_{mv}}}
. Conditioning on
\zeta
and running step 4 of Algorithm
8.6
, it follows from Proposition
8.7
that we recover
O(\varepsilon_{1})
-accurate estimates to the parameters of
\mathcal{M}
in the subspace, i.e.
\left\|U^{\top}\mu_{i}-\hat{\mu}_{i}\right\|_{2}\leqslant O(\varepsilon_{1})
and
\left\|U^{\top}\Sigma_{i}U-\hat{\Sigma}_{i}\right\|_{F}\leqslant O(\varepsilon_{1})
. Since we repeat the above for
\ell^{\prime}
candidate subspaces in
L^{\prime}
, the probability over all probability of success is
\left(1-\varepsilon\right)^{\left(m/\varepsilon_{1}\right)^{c_{mv}}\cdot\ell^{\prime}}
.

By part (2) in Proposition
3.5
, there is a vector
\mu_{i}^{\prime}\in V^{\prime}_{\hat{S}}
such that
\left\|\mu_{i}-\mu_{i}^{\prime}\right\|\leqslant\frac{20}{\alpha}\delta^{1/4}\Delta^{1/2}
where
\delta=2\eta^{1/(C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}
and
\eta=O(4k(1+1/\alpha+\Delta)^{4k}\sqrt{\varepsilon_{1}})
. Let
\hat{P}=UU^{\top}
be a projection matrix where the columns of
Q
span
V^{\prime}_{\hat{S}}
and let
Q^{\top}\mu_{i}
be the projection of the true means to the corresponding subspace. Then,

\begin{split}\left\|U\hat{\mu}_{i}-\mu_{i}\right\|_{2}&\leqslant\left\|U\hat{\mu}_{i}-\mu_{i}^{\prime}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\mu_{i}^{\prime}-\mu_{i}\right\|_{2}\\
&\leqslant\left\|U\hat{\mu}_{i}-P(\mu_{i}^{\prime}-\mu_{i}+\mu_{i})\right\|_{2}+O\left(\frac{\sqrt{\Delta}\delta^{1/4}}{\alpha}\right)\\
&\leqslant\left\|\hat{\mu}_{i}-U^{\top}\mu_{i}\right\|_{2}+O\left(\frac{\sqrt{\Delta}\delta^{1/4}}{\alpha}\right)\\
&\leqslant O\left(\frac{\sqrt{\Delta}\delta^{1/4}}{\alpha}\right).\end{split}

where the third inequality follows from observing that
U^{\top}\mu_{i}
is the true mean in the low dimensional subspace and applying Proposition
8.7
.

By Proposition
3.4
, there exists
\hat{S}\in L^{\prime}
such that
\hat{S}-(\Sigma_{i}-I)=P_{i}+Q_{i}
where

\left\|P_{i}\right\|_{F}=O(\sqrt{\eta/\alpha})
.
Again by part (3) in Proposition
3.5
, there exists a symmetric matrix
Q_{i}^{\prime}\in V_{\hat{S}}^{\prime}\times V_{\hat{S}}^{\prime}
such that
\left\|Q_{i}-Q_{i}^{\prime}\right\|_{F}\leqslant O(\frac{k^{2}}{\alpha}\delta^{1/4}\Delta^{1/2})
. We also know that in the subspace spanned by
V_{\hat{S}}^{\prime}
,
\left\|\hat{\Sigma}_{i}-U^{\top}\Sigma_{i}U\right\|_{F}^{2}\leqslant\poly(\varepsilon_{2})
. Recall, Algorithm
8.6
outputs the following estimate:
\hat{M}=U\hat{\Sigma}U^{\top}+\left(I+\hat{S}\right)-\hat{P}\left(I+\hat{S}\right)\hat{P}
. Observe, for any matrix
M
and projection matrix
P
,
M=PMP+(I-P)M(I-P)+PM(I-P)+(I-P)MP
. Then,

\begin{split}\left\|\Sigma_{i}-\hat{M}\right\|_{F}&\leqslant\underbrace{\left\|\hat{P}\left(\Sigma_{i}-\hat{M}\right)\hat{P}\right\|_{F}}_{(1)}+\underbrace{\left\|\left(I-\hat{P}\right)\left(\Sigma_{i}-\hat{M}\right)\left(I-\hat{P}\right)\right\|_{F}}_{(2)}\\
&\hskip 14.45377pt+\underbrace{\left\|\hat{P}\left(\Sigma_{i}-\hat{M}\right)\left(I-\hat{P}\right)\right\|_{F}}_{(3)}+\underbrace{\left\|\left(I-\hat{P}\right)\left(\Sigma_{i}-\hat{M}\right)\hat{P}\right\|_{F}}_{(4)}\\
\end{split}

(8.2)

We bound each of the terms above. Since
\hat{P}\left(I+S-P(I+S)P\right)\hat{P}=0
, we can bound term (1) as follows

\left\|\hat{P}\left(\Sigma_{i}-\hat{M}\right)\hat{P}\right\|_{F}=\left\|\hat{P}\Sigma_{i}\hat{P}-\hat{P}U\hat{\Sigma}_{i}U^{\top}\hat{P}\right\|_{F}=\left\|U^{\top}\Sigma_{i}U-\hat{\Sigma}_{i}\right\|\leqslant\ O\left(\frac{\sqrt{\Delta}\delta^{1/4}}{\alpha}\right)

(8.3)

Similarly, since
\left(I-\hat{P}\right)\left(U\hat{\Sigma}_{i}U^{\top}\right)\left(I-\hat{P}\right)=0
and
\Sigma_{i}=I+\hat{S}-P_{i}-Q_{i}
, we can bound term (2) as follows:

\begin{split}\left\|\left(I-\hat{P}\right)\left(\Sigma_{i}-\hat{M}\right)\left(I-\hat{P}\right)\right\|_{F}&=\left\|\left(I-\hat{P}\right)\left(\Sigma_{i}-\left(I+\hat{S}\right)\right)\left(I-\hat{P}\right)\right\|_{F}\\
&\leqslant\left\|\left(I-\hat{P}\right)\left(\Sigma_{i}-\left(I+\hat{S}-Q_{i}\right)\right)\left(I-\hat{P}\right)\right\|_{F}+\left\|\left(I-\hat{P}\right)Q_{i}\left(I-\hat{P}\right)\right\|_{F}\\
&\leqslant\left\|P_{i}\right\|_{F}^{2}+\left\|\left(I-\hat{P}\right)\left(Q_{i}-Q_{i}^{\prime}\right)\left(I-\hat{P}\right)\right\|_{F}+\left\|\left(I-\hat{P}\right)Q_{i}^{\prime}\left(I-\hat{P}\right)\right\|_{F}\\
&\leqslant O\left(\sqrt{\frac{\eta}{\alpha}}+\frac{k^{2}\delta^{1/4}\Delta^{1/2}}{\alpha}\right)\end{split}

(8.4)

Next, we bound term (3). Observe,
\hat{P}\left(U\hat{\Sigma}_{i}U^{\top}\right)\left(I-\hat{P}\right)=0
and
\hat{P}(I+S)\hat{P}(I-\hat{P})=0
. Thus,

\begin{split}\left\|\hat{P}\left(\Sigma_{i}-\hat{M}\right)\left(I-\hat{P}\right)\right\|_{F}&=\left\|\hat{P}\left(\Sigma_{i}-\left(I+\hat{S}\right)\right)\left(I-\hat{P}\right)\right\|_{F}\\
&=\left\|\hat{P}\left(P_{i}+Q_{i}\right)\left(I-\hat{P}\right)\right\|_{F}\\
&\leqslant\left\|\hat{P}P_{i}\left(I-\hat{P}\right)\right\|_{F}\\
&\leqslant O\left(\sqrt{\frac{\eta}{\alpha}}\right)\end{split}

(8.5)

Obseve, term (4) follows from a similar argument. Combining equations (
8.3
), (
8.4
),(
8.5
) and substituting back into (
8.2
) we can conclude

\left\|\Sigma_{i}-\hat{M}\right\|_{F}\leqslant O\left(\sqrt{\frac{\eta}{\alpha}}+\frac{k^{2}\delta^{1/4}\Delta^{1/2}}{\alpha}\right)

The size of
L^{\prime}
is
\ell^{\prime}=O\left(\log k\left(\eta/\left(k^{5}\left(\Delta^{4}+1/\alpha^{4}\right)\right)\right)^{-4k}\right)
and since we add a single tuple of k means and covariances for each subspace in
L^{\prime}
, the list
L
has the same size. The running time is
\poly\left(|Y|,|L|,d^{k},m,1/\varepsilon_{1}\right)
concluding the proof.
∎

8.2
Proof of Theorem
8.1

Since we have all the main ingredients: the tensor decomposition algorithm recovering a polynomial size of list (Theorem
8.2
), the upgraded partial clustering algorithm with high probability of success (Theorem
7.1
) and the spectral separation algorithm of thin components (Lemma
5.1
), we can now complete the proof of Theorem
8.1
.

The algorithm establishing Theorem
8.1
is almost the same as Algorithm
6.3
. The only difference is we will replace Algorithm
4.4
by Algorithm
7.2
and replace Algorithm
3.2
by Algorithm
8.6
. The following two lemmas show that by modifying the parameters slightly and applying the upgraded partial clustering and tensor decomposition algorithms, we can have the same conclusions as in Lemma
6.8
and Lemma
6.9
with a polynomial success probability. Then the proof of Theorem
8.1
is exactly the same as the proof of Theorem
6.1
in Section
6.2
except for the use of Lemma
8.10
and Lemma
8.11
instead of Lemma
6.8
and Lemma
6.9
.

Lemma 8.10

(Non-negligible Weight and Covariance Separation)
.

Given
0<\varepsilon<1/k^{k^{O(k^{2})}}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
let
\alpha=\varepsilon^{1/(45C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}G_{i}
with
G_{i}=\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
be a
k
-mixture of Gaussians
with mixture covariance
\Sigma
such that
w_{i}\geqslant\alpha
for all
i\in[k]

and there exist
i,j\in[k]
such that

\left\lVert\Sigma^{\dagger/2}\left(\Sigma_{i}-\Sigma_{j}\right)\Sigma^{\dagger/2}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}>1/\alpha^{5}
. Further, let
X
be a set of points satisfying Condition
2.47
with respect to
\mathcal{M}

for some parameters
\gamma\leqslant\varepsilon d^{-8k}k^{-Ck}
, for a sufficiently large constant
C
, and
t\geqslant 8k
.
Let
Y
be an
\varepsilon
-corrupted version of
X
of size
n\geqslant n_{0}=\left(dk\right)^{\Omega(1)}/\varepsilon
,
Algorithm
7.2
partitions
Y
into
Y_{1}
,
Y_{2}
in time
n^{O(1)}
such that
with probability at least
2^{-O(k)}(1-O(\alpha))

there is a non-trivial partition of
[k]
into
Q_{1}\cup Q_{2}
so that letting
\mathcal{M}_{j}

be a distribution proportional to
\sum_{i\in Q_{j}}w_{i}G_{i}
and
W_{j}=\sum_{i\in Q_{j}}w_{i}
, then

Y_{j}
is an
\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{1/(45C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)
-corrupted version of
\bigcup_{i\in Q_{j}}X_{i}

satisfying Condition
2.47
with respect to
\mathcal{M}

with parameters
(\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(k\gamma/W_{j}\right),t)
.

Proof.

We run Algorithm
7.2
with sample set
Y
, number of components
k
,
the fraction of outliers
\varepsilon
and the accuracy parameter
\eta
.
Since
X
satisfies Condition
2.47
,
we can set
t=10
,
\beta=(k^{2}t^{4}\alpha)^{t/2}=O_{k}(\alpha^{5})
and
\eta=\alpha^{2}\gg\sqrt{\varepsilon/\alpha}
in Theorem
7.1
.
Then, by assumption, there exist
i,j
such that

\left\lVert\Sigma^{\dagger/2}\left(\Sigma_{i}-\Sigma_{j}\right)\Sigma^{\dagger/2}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}>\frac{1}{\alpha^{5}}=\Omega\left(\frac{k^{2}t^{4}}{\beta^{2/t}\alpha^{4}}\right)\;.

We observe that we also satisfy the other preconditions for Theorem
7.1
,
since
n\geqslant\left(dk/\right)^{\Omega(1)}/\varepsilon
.

Then, Theorem
7.1
implies that with probability at least
2^{-O(k)}(1-O(\eta/\alpha-\sqrt{\eta}))=2^{-O(k)}(1-O(\alpha))
,
the set
Y
is partitioned in two sets
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
such that
there is a non-trivial partition of
[k]
into
Q_{1}\cup Q_{2}
so that letting
\mathcal{M}_{j}
be a distribution proportional
to
\sum_{i\in Q_{j}}w_{i}G_{i}
and
W_{j}=\sum_{i\in Q_{j}}w_{i}
, then

Y_{j}
is an
\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{1/(45C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)
-corrupted version of
\bigcup_{i\in Q_{j}}X_{i}
.
By Lemma
2.50
,
\bigcup_{i\in Q_{j}}X_{i}

satisfies Condition
2.47
with respect to
\mathcal{M}
with parameters
(\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(k\gamma/W_{j}\right),t)
.

∎

When the mixture is not covariance separated and nearly isotropic, we can obtain a small list of hypotheses such that one of them is
close to the true parameters, via tensor decomposition.

Lemma 8.11

(Mixture is List-decodable)
.

Given
0<\varepsilon<1/k^{k^{O(k^{2})}}
let
\alpha=\varepsilon^{1/(45C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}
.
Let
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}G_{i}
with
G_{i}=\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})

be a
k
-mixture of Gaussians with mixture mean
\mu
and mixture covariance
\Sigma
,
such that
\left\lVert\mu\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\sqrt{\varepsilon/\alpha}\right)
,

\left\|\Sigma-I\right\|_{F}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\sqrt{\varepsilon}/\alpha\right)
,

w_{i}\geqslant\alpha
for all
i\in[k]
,
and
\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-\Sigma_{j}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leqslant 1/\alpha^{5}
for any pair of components,
and let
X
be a set of points satisfying Condition
2.47

with respect to
\mathcal{M}
for some parameters
\gamma=\varepsilon d^{-8k}k^{-Ck}
, for a sufficiently large constant
C
,
and
t=8k
. Let
Y
be an
\varepsilon
-corrupted version of
X
of size
n
,
Algorithm
8.6

outputs a list
L
of hypotheses of size
O((1/\varepsilon)^{4k^{2}})
in time
\poly(|L|,n)

such that if we choose a hypothesis

\{\hat{\mu}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
uniformly at random,

\left\lVert\mu_{i}-\hat{\mu}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{1/(20C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)
and

\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-\hat{\Sigma}_{i}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{1/(20C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)
for all
i
with probability at least
O(\varepsilon^{4k^{2}})
.

Proof.

Recall we run Algorithm
8.6
on the samples
Y
, the number of clusters
k
, the fraction of outliers
\varepsilon
and the minimum weight
\alpha=\varepsilon^{1/(20C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}
. Next, we show that the preconditions of Theorem
8.2
are satisfied. First, the upper bounds on
\left\|\mu\right\|_{2}
and
\left\|\Sigma-I\right\|_{F}
imply
\sum_{i\in k}w_{i}\left(\Sigma_{i}+\mu_{i}\mu_{i}^{\top}\right)=\Sigma+\mu\mu^{\top}\preceq(1+\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\sqrt{\varepsilon}/\alpha\right))I
. Since the LHS is a conic combination of PSD matrices, it follows that for all
i\in[k]
,
\mu_{i}\mu_{i}^{\top}\preceq\frac{1}{\alpha}\left(1+\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\sqrt{\varepsilon}/\alpha\right)\right)I
,
and thus
\left\lVert\mu_{i}\mu_{i}^{\top}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\frac{2}{\alpha}
. Next, we can write:

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-I\right\rVert_{F}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\|\Sigma_{i}-(\Sigma+\mu\mu^{\top})\right\|_{F}+\left\|\Sigma-I\right\|_{F}+\left\|\mu\mu^{\top}\right\|_{F}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-\sum_{j\in[k]}w_{j}\left(\Sigma_{j}+\mu_{j}\mu_{j}^{\top}\right)\right\rVert_{F}+\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}k}{\alpha}+\frac{\varepsilon}{\alpha}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\lVert\sum_{j\in[k]}w_{j}\left(\Sigma_{i}-\Sigma_{j}\right)\right\rVert_{F}+\frac{2}{\alpha}+\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}k}{\alpha}+\frac{\varepsilon}{\alpha}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{2}{\alpha^{5/2}}\;,

where the first and the third inequalities follow from the triangle inequality and the upper bound on
\left\lVert\mu_{i}\mu_{i}^{\top}\right\rVert_{F}
,
and the last inequality follows from the assumption that
\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-\Sigma_{j}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leqslant 1/\alpha^{5}

for every pair of covariances
\Sigma_{i},\Sigma_{j}
. So, we can set
\Delta=2\alpha^{-5/2}

in Theorem
8.2
.
Then, given the definition of
\alpha
, we have that

\eta=2k^{4k}\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(1+\Delta/\alpha\right)^{4k}\sqrt{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{2/5}\right)

and
1/\varepsilon^{2}\geqslant\log(1/\eta)(k+1/\alpha+\Delta)^{4k+5}/\eta^{2}
.
Therefore, Algorithm
8.6
outputs a list
L
of hypotheses
such that
\left|L\right|=\exp\left(1/\varepsilon^{2}\right)
, and with probability at least
0.99
,

L
contains a hypothesis that satisfies the following: for all
i\in[k]
,

\begin{split}\left\lVert\hat{\mu}_{i}-\mu_{i}\right\rVert_{2}&=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\frac{\Delta^{1/2}}{\alpha}\right)\eta^{G(k)}=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{-1/(20C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\cdot\varepsilon^{1/(10C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{1/(20C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)\textrm{ and }\\
\left\lVert\hat{\Sigma}_{i}-\Sigma_{i}\right\rVert_{F}&=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(k^{4}\right)\frac{\Delta^{1/2}}{\alpha}\eta^{G(k)}=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\varepsilon^{1/(20C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}\right)\;.\end{split}

(8.6)

Then if we choose a hypothesis in
L
uniformly at random, the probability that we choose the hypothesis satisfying (
6.2
) is at least
1/|L|=\exp\left(-1/\varepsilon^{2}\right)
.
∎

9
Robust Parameter Recovery: Proof of Theorem
1.6

In order to show that our algorithm recovers the individual components and the parameters, we will prove the following identifiability theorem. Without any assumption on the mixtures, it is impossible to distinguish components within
\varepsilon
total variation distance with
\varepsilon
-fraction of noise. So given two mixtures of Gaussians with
\varepsilon
total variation distance, the theorem shows that there exist two partitions of components of the two mixtures respectively such that any two components in the matched pair is are
\poly(\varepsilon)
-close in total variation distance.

Theorem 9.1

(Identifiability)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i=1}^{k_{1}}w_{i}G_{i},\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{k_{2}}w_{i}^{\prime}G_{i}^{\prime}
be two mixtures of Gaussians such that
d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}^{\prime})\leqslant\varepsilon
. Then there exists a partition of
[k_{1}]
into sets
R_{0},R_{1},\dots,R_{\ell}
and a partition of
[k_{2}]
into sets
S_{0},S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell}
such that

1.

Let
W_{i}=\sum_{j\in R_{i}}w_{j}
for
i=0,1,\dots,k_{1}
,

W_{i}^{\prime}=\sum_{j\in S_{i}}w_{j}^{\prime}
for
i=0,1,\dots,k_{2}
.
Then for all
i\in[\ell]
,

\displaystyle|W_{i}-W_{i}^{\prime}|

\displaystyle\leqslant\poly_{k}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{TV}}(G_{j},G_{j^{\prime}}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leqslant\poly_{k}(\varepsilon)\quad\forall j\in R_{i},j^{\prime}\in S_{i}

2.

W_{0},W_{0}^{\prime}\leqslant\poly_{k}(\varepsilon)
.

Corollary 9.2
.

There is an algorithm with the following behavior:
Given
\varepsilon>0
and a multiset of
n=d^{O(k)}\poly(\varepsilon)
samples from a distribution
F
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

d_{\mathsf{TV}}(F,\mathcal{M})\leqslant\varepsilon
, for an unknown target
k
-GMM
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
,
the algorithm runs in time
d^{O(k)}\poly_{k}(1/\varepsilon)
and outputs a
k^{\prime}
-GMM hypothesis

\widehat{\mathcal{M}}=\sum_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}\widehat{w}_{i}\mathcal{N}(\widehat{\mu}_{i},\widehat{\Sigma}_{i})
with
k^{\prime}\leqslant k

such that with high probability
there exists a partition of
[k]
into
k^{\prime}+1
sets
R_{0},R_{1},\dots,R_{k^{\prime}}
such that

1.

Let
W_{i}=\sum_{j\in R_{i}}w_{j}
.
Then for all
i\in[k^{\prime}]
,

\displaystyle|W_{i}-\hat{w}_{i}|

\displaystyle\leqslant\poly_{k}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{N}(\mu_{j},\Sigma_{j}),\mathcal{N}(\widehat{\mu}_{i},\widehat{\Sigma}_{i}))

\displaystyle\leqslant\poly_{k}(\varepsilon)\quad\forall j\in R_{i}

2.

The sum of weights of exceptional components in
R_{0}
is at most
\poly_{k}(\varepsilon)
.

Parameter estimation is implied by TV distance for individual Gaussians (in relative Frobenius norm).
The corollary follows immediately from the identifiability theorem.

Outline of Proof.

The first step is to deal with the components in
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
with small weights. We will construct
\tilde{\mathcal{M}},\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
by removing components with small weights. If we prove the statement on
\tilde{\mathcal{M}},\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
, we can then deduce the theorem in the general case with worse, but still polynomial dependencies on
\varepsilon
. The second step is a partial clustering, after which the components within each cluster have TV distance bounded by
1-\mbox{poly}(\varepsilon)
. We prove this lemma in a separate section.
After that we modify the parameters slightly so that the resulting parameters for different components are either identical or have a minimum separation. After this, we can use a lemma from
[
LM20
]
that provides a 1-1 mapping between the components of two such mixtures with small TV distance such that the mapped pairs have small TV distance.

Distance between Gaussians.

We use the following facts for Gaussian distributions.

Lemma 9.3

(Frobenius Distance to TV Distance)
.

Suppose
N(\mu_{1},\Sigma_{1}),N(\mu_{2},\Sigma_{2})
are Gaussians with
\left\|\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}\right\|_{2}\leqslant\delta
and
\left\|\Sigma_{1}-\Sigma_{2}\right\|_{F}\leqslant\delta
. If the eigenvalues of
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
are at least
\lambda>0
, then
d_{\mathsf{TV}}(N(\mu_{1},\Sigma_{1}),N(\mu_{2},\Sigma_{2}))=O(\delta/\lambda)
.

Lemma 9.4

(Lemma 5.4 in
[
LM20
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a mixture of
k
Gaussians that is connected if we draw edges between all components
i,j
in
\mathcal{M}
such that
d_{\mathsf{TV}}(G_{i},G_{j})\leqslant 1-\delta
. Let
\Sigma
be the covariance matrix of
\mathcal{M}
. Then for any components
\Sigma_{i}
of the mixture

1.

\Sigma_{i}\succeq\poly_{k}(\delta)\Sigma

2.

\left\|\Sigma^{-1/2}(\Sigma-\Sigma_{i})\Sigma^{-1/2}\right\|_{F}\leqslant\poly_{k}(\delta)^{-1}
.

The proof is identical to Lemma 5.4 in
[
LM20
]
. The only difference is that in
[
LM20
]
the authors assume that the minimal weight of
\mathcal{M}
is at least
\delta
and TV distance between any pair of components is at least
\delta
but here we do not need these two assumptions, which does not affect the proof.

Fact 9.5

(Claim 3.9 in
[
LM20
]
)
.

Let
\partial
denote the differential operator with respect to
y
. If

f(y)=P(y,X)\exp\left(a(X)y+\frac{1}{2}b(X)y^{2}\right)

where
P
is a polynomial in
y
of degree
k
(whose coefficients are polynomials in
X
) and
a(X),b(X)
are polynomials in
X
then

(\partial-(a(X)+yb(X)))f(y)=Q(y,X)\exp\left(a(X)y+\frac{1}{2}b(X)y^{2}\right)

where
Q
is a polynomial in
y
with degree exactly
k-1
whose leading coefficient is
k
times the leading coefficient of
P
.

Fact 9.6

(Corollary 3.10 in
[
LM20
]
)
.

Let
\partial
denote the differential operator with respect to
y
. If

f(y)=P(y,X)\exp\left(a(X)y+\frac{1}{2}b(X)y^{2}\right)

where
P
is a polynomial in
y
of degree
k
then

(\partial-(a(X)+yb(X)))^{k+1}f(y)=0.

Fact 9.7

(Claim 3.11 in
[
LM20
]
)
.

Let
\partial
denote the differential operator with respect to
y
. If

f(y)=P(y,X)\exp\left(a(X)y+\frac{1}{2}b(X)y^{2}\right)

where
P
is a polynomial in
y
of degree
k
. Let the leading coefficient of
P
(viewed as a polynomial in
y
) be
L(X)
. Let
c(X)
be a linear polynomial in
X
and
d(X)
be a quadratic polynomial in
X
such that
\{a(X),b(X)\}\neq\{c(X),d(X)\}
. If
b(X)\neq d(X)
then

(\partial-(c(X)+yd(X)))^{k^{\prime}}f(y)=Q(y,X)\exp\left(a(X)y+\frac{1}{2}b(X)y^{2}\right)

where
Q
is a polynomial of degree
k+k^{\prime}
in
y
with leading coefficient

L(X)(b(X)-d(X))^{k^{\prime}}

and if
b(X)=d(X)
then

(\partial-(c(X)+yd(X)))^{k^{\prime}}f(y)=Q(y,X)\exp\left(a(X)y+\frac{1}{2}b(X)y^{2}\right)

where
Q
is a polynomial of degree
k
in
y
with leading coefficient

L(X)(a(X)-c(X))^{k^{\prime}}.

Lemma 9.8
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i=1}^{k_{1}}w_{i}G_{i},\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{k_{2}}w_{i}^{\prime}G_{i}^{\prime}
be two mixtures of Gaussians such that
d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}^{\prime})\leqslant\varepsilon
.
For any constant
0<c_{1}<1
, there exists
i\in[k_{1}+k_{2}+1]
such that
w_{j},w_{j^{\prime}}^{\prime}\notin[\varepsilon^{c_{1}^{i-1}},\varepsilon^{c_{1}^{i}})
for any
j\in[k_{1}],j^{\prime}\in[k_{2}]
.
Moreover, if

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{M}}=\frac{\sum_{\{j:w_{j}\geqslant\varepsilon^{c_{1}^{i}}\}}w_{j}G_{j}}{\sum_{\{j:w_{j}\geqslant\varepsilon^{c_{1}^{i}}\}}w_{j}}

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}=\frac{\sum_{\{j:w_{j}^{\prime}\geqslant\varepsilon^{c_{1}^{i}}\}}w_{j}^{\prime}G_{j}^{\prime}}{\sum_{\{j:w_{j}^{\prime}\geqslant\varepsilon^{c_{1}^{i}}\}}w_{j}^{\prime}}

then
d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\tilde{\mathcal{M}},\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})\leqslant O_{k}(\varepsilon^{c_{1}^{i-1}})
.

Proof.

We can see that
[\varepsilon^{c_{1}^{i-1}},\varepsilon^{c_{1}^{i}})
with
i\in[k_{1}+k_{2}+1]
are
k_{1}+k_{2}+1
disjoint intervals and

w_{j},w_{j^{\prime}}^{\prime}
with
j\in[k_{1}],j^{\prime}\in[k_{2}]
have at most
k_{1}+k_{2}
distinct values. So there is one interval containing no weights.

We then construct
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
by removing the small components in
\mathcal{M}
. The sum of weights removed is at most
k\varepsilon^{c_{1}^{i-1}}
. So

d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{M},\tilde{\mathcal{M}})\leqslant k\varepsilon^{c_{1}^{i-1}}
. Similarly, we have
d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})\leqslant k\varepsilon^{c_{1}^{i-1}}
. By the triangle inequality,

d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\tilde{\mathcal{M}},\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})\leqslant d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}^{\prime})+d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{M},\tilde{\mathcal{M}})+d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})\leqslant O_{k}(\varepsilon^{c_{1}^{i-1}}).

∎

Lemma
9.8
shows that we can remove components with tiny weights in the mixtures.
So in the following lemma, we will assume
M
and
M^{\prime}
are Gaussian mixtures with minimal weights at least
\poly(\varepsilon)
.
We will show that we can partition the union of components of two mixtures so that if we prove Theorem
9.1
for each part of the partition, we can combine them to prove Theorem
9.1
on the full mixtures.

Lemma 9.9
.

For any constant
0<c_{3}<1
, there exist
c_{1},c_{2}>0
that depend on
k
and
c_{3}
, such that
if
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i=1}^{k_{1}}w_{i}G_{i},\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{k_{2}}w_{i}^{\prime}G_{i}^{\prime}
with
k_{1},k_{2}\leqslant k
,

d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}^{\prime})\leqslant\varepsilon
and
w_{i},w_{i}^{\prime}\geqslant\varepsilon^{c_{1}}
for all
i
, then there exists a partition of
[k_{1}]
into sets
R_{1},\dots,R_{\ell}
and a partition of
[k_{2}]
into sets
S_{1},\dots,S_{\ell}
such that

1.

For all
i\in[\ell]
, let
W_{i}=\sum_{j\in R_{i}}w_{j},W_{i}^{\prime}=\sum_{j\in S_{i}}w_{j}^{\prime}
be the sum of weights in each piece.
Let
\mathcal{M}_{i}=\frac{1}{W_{i}}\sum_{j\in R_{i}}w_{j}G_{j},\mathcal{M}_{i}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{W_{i}^{\prime}}\sum_{j\in S_{i}}w_{j}^{\prime}G_{j}^{\prime}
be the submixtures of Gaussians after partition.
Then for all
i\in[\ell]
,

\displaystyle|W_{i}-W_{i}^{\prime}|

\displaystyle\leqslant\poly_{k}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{M}_{i},\mathcal{M}_{i}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leqslant O_{k}(\varepsilon^{c_{2}})

2.

Consider the graph with vertices corresponding to components in
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
and two components are adjacent if the total variation distance between them is at most
1-\varepsilon^{c_{2}c_{3}}
. Then the induced subgraph of vertices with indices
R_{i}\cup S_{i}
is connected for all
i\in[\ell]
.

The proof of Lemma
9.9
is deferred to Section
9.1
.
In the following two lemmas, we then prove Theorem
9.1
for each pair
\mathcal{M}_{i},\mathcal{M}_{i}^{\prime}
defined in Lemma
9.9
. In Lemma
9.10
, we construct two mixtures of which pairs of parameters are identical or separated. We also shows it suffices to work under this simplification.

Lemma 9.10
.

For any constant
0<c_{4}<1
, there exist
c_{3},c_{5}
that depend on
k
and
c_{4}
, such that if
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i=1}^{k_{1}}w_{i}G_{i},\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{k_{2}}w_{i}^{\prime}G_{i}^{\prime}
with
k_{1},k_{2}\leqslant k
and

1.

\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{M}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is isotropic,

2.

d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}^{\prime})\leqslant\varepsilon
,

3.

w_{i},w_{i}^{\prime}\geqslant\varepsilon^{c_{3}}
for all
i
,

4.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a graph with components
G_{i},G_{i}^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
as vertex set and two components are adjacent if the total variation distance between them is at most
1-\varepsilon^{c_{3}}
. Then
\mathcal{G}
is connected

then there exist two mixtures of Gaussians
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}=\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{k}_{1}}\tilde{w}_{i}\tilde{G}_{i},\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{k}_{2}}\tilde{w}_{i}^{\prime}\tilde{G}_{i}^{\prime}
such that

1.

Any pair in
\{\tilde{\mu}_{i}\}\cup\{\tilde{\mu}_{i}^{\prime}\}
is either identical or separated by at least
\varepsilon^{c_{4}c_{5}}

2.

Any pair in
\{\tilde{\Sigma}_{i}\}\cup\{\tilde{\Sigma}_{i}^{\prime}\}
is either identical or separated by at least
\varepsilon^{c_{4}c_{5}}
in Frobenius norm.

3.

\left\|\E(h_{m}(\tilde{\mathcal{M}}))-\E(h_{m}(\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}))\right\|_{F}\leqslant O_{k}(\varepsilon^{c_{5}})
for any
m\leqslant O(k)

4.

There exist
\pi_{1}:[k_{1}]\to[\tilde{k}_{1}]
and
\pi_{2}:[k_{2}]\to[\tilde{k}_{2}]
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{i:\pi_{1}(i)=j}w_{i}=\tilde{w}_{j},\sum_{i:\pi_{2}(i)=j}w_{i}^{\prime}=\tilde{w}_{j}^{\prime},

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{TV}}(G_{i},\tilde{G}_{\pi_{1}(i)})\leqslant\poly_{k}(\varepsilon),\quad\text{for all }i\in[k_{1}]

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{TV}}(G_{i}^{\prime},\tilde{G}_{\pi_{2}(i)}^{\prime})\leqslant\poly_{k}(\varepsilon),\quad\text{for all }i\in[k_{2}].

Proof.

For any
0<c_{4}<1
, there is
\ell\in[k^{2}]
such that the distance between any pair of parameters in
\{\mu_{i}\}\cup\{\mu_{i}^{\prime}\}
or the Frobenius distance between any pair in
\{\Sigma_{i}\}\cup\{\Sigma_{i}^{\prime}\}
is not in the interval
[\varepsilon^{(c_{4}/2)^{\ell-1}},\varepsilon^{(c_{4}/2)^{\ell}})
.

Now consider a graph
\mathcal{G}
on
k_{1}+k_{2}
nodes where each node represents a vector in
\{\mu_{i}\}\cup\{\mu_{i}^{\prime}\}
and two vectors
a,b
are adjacent if

\left\|a-b\right\|\leqslant\varepsilon^{(c_{4}/2)^{\ell-1}}.

We now construct new mixtures
\tilde{\mathcal{M}},\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
. For each connected component in
\mathcal{G}
say
\{\mu_{i_{1}},\dots,\mu_{j_{1}}^{\prime},\dots\}
, pick a representative say
\mu_{i_{1}}
and set
\tilde{\mu}_{i_{1}}=\cdots=\tilde{\mu}_{j_{1}}^{\prime}=\cdots=\mu_{i_{1}}
. Do this for all connected components and similar in the graph on covariance matrices with edges
(i,j)
if

\left\|\Sigma_{i}-\Sigma_{j}\right\|_{F}\leqslant\varepsilon^{(c_{4}/2)^{\ell-1}}.

After replacing close parameters with a representative, we may get some exactly same components in each new mixture. We then merge components with same means and covariances by adding their weights.
Since all representatives of means and covariances are in different connected components of the graphs, they are separated by at least
\varepsilon^{(c_{4}/2)^{\ell}}
. Setting
c_{5}=1/2(c_{4}/2)^{\ell-1}
gives a separation of
\varepsilon^{c_{4}c_{5}}
.

Next we prove
3
. There is a natural mapping
\pi_{1}:[k_{1}]\to[\tilde{k}_{1}]
that maps any component in
\mathcal{M}
to the merged component in
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
and a similar mapping
\pi_{2}:[k_{2}]\to[\tilde{k}_{2}]
for
\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
. For all
i
, we have

\left\|\tilde{\mu}_{\pi_{1}(i)}-\mu_{i}\right\|,\left\|\tilde{\mu}_{\pi_{2}(i)}^{\prime}-\mu_{i}^{\prime}\right\|,\left\|\tilde{\Sigma}_{\pi_{1}(i)}-\Sigma_{i}\right\|_{F},\left\|\tilde{\Sigma}_{\pi_{2}(i)}^{\prime}-\Sigma_{i}^{\prime}\right\|_{F}\leqslant O_{k}(1)\varepsilon^{(c_{4}/2)^{\ell-1}}

(9.1)

because for any pair of parameters above say
\tilde{\mu}_{\pi_{1}(i)}
and
\mu_{i}
, there is a path of length at most
2k
connecting
\mu_{i}
to the representative of the connected component, and each edge connects a pair with TV distance at most
\varepsilon
. Suppose
\left\|\mu_{i}\right\|,\left\|\Sigma_{i}-I\right\|_{F}\leqslant\Delta
. Then by Definition
2.4
, we have for any integer
m
,

\left\|\E(h_{m}(\mathcal{M}))-\E(h_{m}(\tilde{\mathcal{M}}))\right\|_{F}\leqslant O_{k}(m)\Delta^{m}\varepsilon^{(c_{4}/2)^{\ell-1}}.

Since
\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{M}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is isotropic and the minimum weight in
\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{M}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is at least
\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{c_{3}}
, we have
\left\|\mu_{i}\right\|\leqslant\sqrt{2/\varepsilon^{c_{3}}}
for all
i
. Applying Lemma
9.4
to
\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{M}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
, we have
\left\|I-\Sigma_{i}\right\|_{F}\leqslant\poly_{k}(\varepsilon^{c_{3}})^{-1}
.
So there is a constant
a
such that
\Delta\leqslant\varepsilon^{-ac_{3}}
.
If we take
c_{3}>0
so that
ac_{3}O(k)\leqslant 1/2(c_{4}/2)^{\ell-1}
and take
c_{5}=1/2(c_{4}/2)^{\ell-1}
,
then

\left\|\E(h_{m}(\mathcal{M}))-\E(h_{m}(\tilde{\mathcal{M}}))\right\|_{F}\leqslant O_{k}(m)\varepsilon^{(c_{4}/2)^{\ell-1}-O(m)ac_{3}}=O_{k}(\varepsilon^{c_{5}})

for
m\leqslant O(k)
. By the same argument, we have the similar inequality for
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}

\left\|\E(h_{m}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}))-\E(h_{m}(\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}))\right\|_{F}=O_{k}(\varepsilon^{c_{5}}).

Since we can use Proposition 3.3 to robustly estimate the Hermite tensors of a Gaussian mixture with
\varepsilon
-fraction of noise and
\poly(\varepsilon)
error guarantee, we must have

\left\|\E(h_{m}(\mathcal{M}))-\E(h_{m}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}))\right\|_{F}\leqslant\poly_{k}(\varepsilon).

Then by the triangle inequality,

\left\|\E(h_{m}(\tilde{\mathcal{M}}))-\E(h_{m}(\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}))\right\|_{F}\leqslant\left\|\E(h_{m}(\mathcal{M}))-\E(h_{m}(\tilde{\mathcal{M}}))\right\|_{F}+\\
\left\|\E(h_{m}(\mathcal{M}))-\E(h_{m}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}))\right\|_{F}+\left\|\E(h_{m}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}))-\E(h_{m}(\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}))\right\|_{F}=O(\varepsilon^{c_{5}}).

For the last conclusion, from the definition of
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
, we know that

\sum_{i:\pi_{1}(i)=j}w_{i}=\tilde{w}_{j},\sum_{i:\pi_{2}(i)=j}w_{i}^{\prime}=\tilde{w}_{j}^{\prime}.

Applying Lemma
9.4
to
\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{M}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
, we have that eigenvalues of
\Sigma_{i}
and
\Sigma_{i}^{\prime}
are at least
\poly(\varepsilon^{c_{3}})
for all
i
.
Then if
c_{3}
is sufficiently small, by Lemma
9.3
, (
9.1
) implies
d_{\mathsf{TV}}(G_{i},\tilde{G}_{\pi(i)})\leqslant\poly_{k}(\varepsilon)
and
d_{\mathsf{TV}}(G_{i}^{\prime},\tilde{G}_{\pi(i)}^{\prime})\leqslant\poly_{k}(\varepsilon)
for all
i
. ∎

The following lemma shows the identifiability under the simplification of Lemma
9.10
. It is proved in the proof of Lemma 8.2 in
[
LM20
]
.

Lemma 9.11
.

Suppose
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i=1}^{k_{1}}w_{i}G_{i},\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{k_{2}}w_{i}^{\prime}G_{i}^{\prime}
satisfies
1
,
2
,
3
in the conclusion of Lemma
9.10
with constants
c_{4},c_{5}
and the minimal weights are at least
\varepsilon^{c_{3}}
. There exists a sufficiently small function
f(k)>0
depending only on
k
such that if
c_{4}\leqslant f(k)
, then
k_{1}=k_{2}
and there exists a permutation
\pi
such that

|w_{i}-w_{\pi(i)}^{\prime}|\leqslant\poly_{k}(\varepsilon)
and
G_{i}=G_{\pi(i)}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Consider the component
G_{k_{2}}^{\prime}=N(\mu_{k_{2}}^{\prime},\Sigma_{k_{2}}^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
. We claim that there must be some
i\in[k_{1}]
such that

(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})=(\mu_{k_{2}}^{\prime},\Sigma_{k_{2}}^{\prime}).

Assume for the sake of contradiction that this is not the case. Let
S_{1}=\{i\in[k_{1}]:\Sigma_{i}=\Sigma_{k_{2}}^{\prime}\}
and
S_{2}=\{i\in[k_{2}-1]:\Sigma_{i}^{\prime}=\Sigma_{k_{2}}^{\prime}\}
.
Suppose
F,F^{\prime}
are the generating functions of
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}

\displaystyle F=\sum_{i=1}^{k_{1}}w_{i}\exp\left(\mu_{i}^{T}X+\frac{1}{2}X^{T}\Sigma_{i}Xy^{2}\right)=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{m!}h_{m}(\mathcal{M})y^{n}

\displaystyle F^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{k_{2}}w_{i}^{\prime}\exp\left({\mu_{i}^{\prime}}^{T}X+\frac{1}{2}X^{T}\Sigma_{i}^{\prime}Xy^{2}\right)=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{m!}h_{m}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime})y^{n}.

Then define the differential operators

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{i}=\partial-\mu_{i}^{T}X-X^{T}\Sigma_{i}Xy

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\prime}=\partial-{\mu_{i}^{\prime}}^{T}X-X^{T}\Sigma_{i}^{\prime}Xy

where partial derivatives are taken with respect to
y
. Now consider the differential operator

\mathcal{D}=(\mathcal{D}_{k_{2}-1}^{\prime})^{2^{k_{1}+k_{2}-2}}\cdots(\mathcal{D}_{1}^{\prime})^{2^{k_{1}}}\mathcal{D}_{k_{1}}^{2^{k_{1}-1}}\cdots\mathcal{D}_{1}

By Fact
9.6
,
\mathcal{D}(F)=0
. By Fact
9.6
and Fact
9.7
, we have

\mathcal{D}(F^{\prime})=P(y,X)\exp\left({\mu_{k_{2}}^{\prime}}^{T}X+\frac{1}{2}X^{T}\Sigma_{k_{2}}^{\prime}Xy^{2}\right)

where
P
is a polynomial of degree

\deg(P)=2^{k_{1}+k_{2}-1}-1-\sum_{i\in S_{1}}2^{i-1}-\sum_{i\in S_{2}}2^{k_{1}+i-2}

with leading coefficient

C_{0}=w_{k_{2}}^{\prime}\prod_{i\in[k_{1}]\setminus S_{1}}(X^{T}(\Sigma_{k_{2}}^{\prime}-\Sigma_{i})X)^{2^{i-1}}\prod_{i\in S_{1}}((\mu_{k_{2}}^{\prime}-\mu_{i})^{T}X)^{2^{i-1}}\\
\prod_{i\in[k_{2}-1]\setminus S_{2}}(X^{T}(\Sigma_{k_{2}}^{\prime}-\Sigma_{i}^{\prime})X)^{2^{k_{1}+i-2}}\prod_{i\in S_{2}}((\mu_{k_{2}}^{\prime}-\mu_{i}^{\prime})^{T}X)^{2^{k_{1}+i-2}}.

We now compare the following differentials evaluated at
y=0

\displaystyle(\mathcal{D}_{k_{2}}^{\prime})^{\deg(P)}\mathcal{D}(F)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{D}_{k_{2}}^{\prime})^{\deg(P)}\mathcal{D}(F^{\prime})

The first quantity is 0 because
\mathcal{D}(F)
is identically 0 as a formal power series. The second one is
\Omega_{k}(1)C_{0}
.
Since for any
i

(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})\neq(\mu_{k_{2}}^{\prime},\Sigma_{k_{2}}^{\prime})
, our assumptions imply that the separation between
\mu_{i},\mu_{k_{2}}^{\prime}
or
\Sigma_{i},\Sigma_{k_{2}}^{\prime}
is at least
\varepsilon^{c_{4}c_{5}}
.
Then we have
C_{0}\geqslant\varepsilon^{c_{4}c_{5}O_{k}(1)}
for some
X
. On the other hand, the coefficients of the formal power series
F,F^{\prime}
are the Hermite polynomials
h_{m}(\mathcal{M})
and
h_{m}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime})
. This is a contradiction with our assumption that

\left\|\E(h_{m}(\mathcal{M})-\E(h_{m}(\mathcal{M})\right\|_{F}\leqslant O_{k}(\varepsilon^{c_{5}})

as long as
c_{4}
is smaller than some sufficiently small function
f(k)
depending only on
k
. Thus there must be some component of
\mathcal{M}
that matches
G_{k_{2}}^{\prime}=N(\mu_{k_{2}}^{\prime},\Sigma_{k_{2}}^{\prime})
. We can repeat the argument for each component in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
and in
\mathcal{M}
to conclude that
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
have the same components.

Next we will show that the weights of the same components in
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
are close. We can assume that
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}G_{i},\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}^{\prime}G_{i}
are two mixtures on the same set of components. Without loss of generality,

w_{1}-w_{1}^{\prime}\leqslant\cdots w_{\ell}-w_{\ell}^{\prime}\leqslant 0\leqslant w_{\ell+1}-w_{\ell+1}^{\prime}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant w_{k}-w_{k}^{\prime}.

Then we can consider the following two mixtures

\displaystyle(w_{1}-w_{1}^{\prime})G_{1}+\cdots+(w_{\ell}-w_{\ell}^{\prime})G_{\ell}

\displaystyle(w_{\ell+1}-w_{\ell+1}^{\prime})G_{\ell+1}+\cdots+(w_{k}-w_{k}^{\prime})G_{k}.

If

\sum_{i=1}^{k}|w_{i}-w_{i}^{\prime}|>\varepsilon^{\zeta}

for some sufficiently small
\zeta
depending only on
k
, we can then normalize each of the above into a distribution and repeat the same argument, using the fact that pairs of components cannot be too close, to obtain a contradiction. Thus, the mixing weights of
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
are
\poly_{k}(\varepsilon)
-close and this completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
9.1
.

We first set
c_{4}=f(k)
as in Lemma
9.11
, and then
c_{3},c_{5}
according to
c_{4}
as in Lemma
9.10
, and
c_{1}^{\prime},c_{2}
according to
c_{3}
as in Lemma
9.9
. Let
c_{1}=\min\{c_{1}^{\prime},c_{2}c_{3}\}
.

By Lemma
9.8
, we can find
i
such that there is no
w_{j},w_{j}^{\prime}
in
[\varepsilon^{c_{1}^{i-1}},\varepsilon^{c_{1}^{i}})
.
Let
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}=\sum_{\{j:w_{j}\geqslant\varepsilon^{c_{1}^{i}}\}}w_{j}G_{j}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}=\sum_{\{j:w_{j}^{\prime}\geqslant\varepsilon^{c_{1}^{i}}\}}w_{j}^{\prime}G_{j}^{\prime}
. Then
d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\tilde{\mathcal{M}},\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})\leqslant O(\varepsilon^{c_{1}^{i-1}})
. Let
\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon^{c_{1}^{i-1}}
. We have
d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\tilde{\mathcal{M}},\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})\leqslant O(\varepsilon_{1})
and the minimum weights of
\tilde{\mathcal{M}},\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
are at least
\varepsilon_{1}^{c_{1}}
.

Now we can apply Lemma
9.9
on
\tilde{\mathcal{M}},\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
and get partitions of components of
\tilde{\mathcal{M}},\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
. For
i\in[\ell]
, let
\mathcal{M}_{i}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{i}
be the mixtures defined in Lemma
9.9
. We can apply a linear transformation to make
\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{M}_{i}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{i}
in isotropic position. Since the total variation distance is invariant under linear transformations, so we still have both conclusions in Lemma
9.9
. Let
\varepsilon_{2}=\varepsilon_{1}^{c_{2}}
. Then
d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{M}_{i},\mathcal{M}_{\pi(i)})\leqslant O(\varepsilon_{2})
and
\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{M}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
satisfies Lemma
9.4
with
\delta=\varepsilon_{2}^{c_{3}}
.
Weights of both mixtures increase when we do the partition. So minimum weights are at least
\varepsilon_{1}^{c_{1}}\geqslant\varepsilon_{1}^{c_{2}c_{3}}=\varepsilon_{2}^{c_{3}}
.

We now prove the statement on these smaller mixtures. First we can use Lemma
9.10
to merge close parameters of
\mathcal{M}_{i},\mathcal{M}_{i}^{\prime}
so that all pairs of parameters are either equal or separated by
\varepsilon_{2}^{c_{4}c_{5}}
. Under this simplification, Lemma
9.11
shows that there is a perfect matching between the same components in two mixtures and their weights are almost the same. By the last statement in Lemma
9.10
, it is also a matching between components of
\mathcal{M}_{i}
and
\mathcal{M}_{i}^{\prime}
by combining
\pi
and
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}
. Moreover, if
\tilde{G}_{j}=\tilde{G}_{\pi(j)}^{\prime}
, then
d_{\mathsf{TV}}(G_{\ell},G^{\prime}_{\ell})\leqslant\poly(\varepsilon_{2})
for all
\ell,\ell^{\prime}
such that
\pi_{1}(\ell)=j,\pi_{2}(\ell^{\prime})=\pi(j)
. Repeating the argument for all pieces in
\tilde{\mathcal{M}},\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
completes the proof.
∎

9.1
Proof of Lemma
9.9

In this section, we will prove Lemma
9.9
.
The following fact in [Liu-Moitra] shows that a good set of clusters of one mixture exists.

Fact 9.12

(Claim 7.6 in [LM’20])
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}G_{i}
be a mixture of Gaussians. For any constants
0<\delta<1
and
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
t\in[k^{2}]
such that there exists a partition (possibly trivial) of
[k]
into sets
R_{1},\dots,R_{\ell}
such that

1.

If we draw edges between all pairs
i,j
such that
d_{\mathsf{TV}}(G_{i},G_{j})\leqslant 1-\varepsilon^{\delta^{t}}
, then each piece of the partition is connected

2.

For any
i,j
in different pieces of the partition,
d_{\mathsf{TV}}(G_{i},G_{j})\geqslant 1-\varepsilon^{\delta^{t-1}}
.

Remark
.

Fact
9.12
can be applied to a set of Gaussians instead of a mixture of Gaussians by randomly assigning positive weights for all Gaussians.

Lemma 9.13
.

For any constant
0<c<1
, suppose
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i=1}^{k_{1}}w_{i}A_{i},\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{k_{2}}w_{i}^{\prime}B_{i}
are two mixtures of arbitrary distributions with
d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}^{\prime})\leqslant\varepsilon
and
w_{i},w_{i}^{\prime}\geqslant\varepsilon^{c}
. If for any
i\neq j
,
d_{\mathsf{TV}}(A_{i},B_{j})\geqslant 1-\varepsilon
, then
k_{1}=k_{2}
and
d_{\mathsf{TV}}(A_{i},B_{i})\leqslant\poly_{k_{1}}(\varepsilon)
for all
i\in[k_{1}]
.

Proof.

Suppose
\pi
is any coupling of
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
and
X,Y
are random variables with distributions
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
. Then
d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}^{\prime})=\min_{\pi}\{\ProbOp_{\pi}(X\neq Y)\}
. We define
\pi
to be the optimal coupling such that
d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}^{\prime})=\ProbOp_{\pi}(X\neq Y)
.
Then we can define
\hat{\pi}
on variables
i,j,X,Y
such that
\sum_{i\in[k_{1}],j\in[k_{2}]}\hat{\pi}(i,j,X,Y)=\pi(X,Y)
and the marginal distribution
\hat{\pi}_{X}
with fixed
i
of X is
w_{i}A_{i}
for all
i\in[k_{1}]
and the marginal distribution
\hat{\pi}_{Y}
with fixed
j
is
w_{j}^{\prime}B_{j}
for all
j\in[k_{2}]
. Let
P_{ij}=\int_{X,Y}\hat{\pi}(i,j,X,Y)dXdY
and
A_{ij}=\frac{1}{P_{ij}}\int_{Y}\hat{\pi}(i,j,X,Y)dY
be distributions on
X
,
B_{ij}=\frac{1}{P_{ij}}\int_{X}\hat{\pi}(i,j,X,Y)dX
be distributions on
Y
.
Then we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}^{\prime})&=\ProbOp_{\pi}(X\neq Y)=\ProbOp_{\hat{\pi}}(X\neq Y)\\
&=\sum_{i,j}P_{ij}\ProbOp_{\hat{\pi}}(X\neq Y\mid i,j)\\
&\geqslant\sum_{i,j}P_{ij}\cdot d_{\mathsf{TV}}(A_{ij},B_{ij}).\end{split}

(9.2)

By the definition of
P_{ij},A_{ij},B_{ij}
,

\displaystyle w_{i}A_{i}=P_{ij}A_{ij}+\sum_{j^{\prime}\neq j}P_{ij^{\prime}}A_{ij^{\prime}}

\displaystyle w_{i}^{\prime}B_{i}=P_{ij}B_{ij}+\sum_{i^{\prime}\neq i}P_{i^{\prime}j}B_{i^{\prime}j}

Dividing both sides by
\max\{w_{i},w_{j}^{\prime}\}
, we get

\displaystyle A_{i}=\frac{P_{ij}}{\max\{w_{i},w_{j}^{\prime}\}}A_{ij}+\left(1-\frac{w_{i}}{\max\{w_{i},w_{j}^{\prime}\}}\right)A_{i}+\sum_{j^{\prime}\neq j}\frac{P_{ij^{\prime}}}{\max\{w_{i},w_{j}^{\prime}\}}A_{ij^{\prime}}

\displaystyle B_{i}=\frac{P_{ij}}{\max\{w_{i},w_{j}^{\prime}\}}B_{ij}+\left(1-\frac{w_{j}^{\prime}}{\max\{w_{i},w_{j}^{\prime}\}}\right)B_{i}+\sum_{i^{\prime}\neq i}\frac{P_{i^{\prime}j}}{\max\{w_{i},w_{j}^{\prime}\}}B_{i^{\prime}j}

From the above two equations, we can write
A_{i},B_{i}
as linear combinations of two distributions.

\displaystyle A_{i}=\frac{P_{ij}}{\max\{w_{i},w_{j}^{\prime}\}}A_{ij}+\left(1-\frac{P_{ij}}{\max\{w_{i},w_{j}^{\prime}\}})\right)A_{i}^{\prime}

\displaystyle B_{i}=\frac{P_{ij}}{\max\{w_{i},w_{j}^{\prime}\}}B_{ij}+\left(1-\frac{P_{ij}}{\max\{w_{i},w_{j}^{\prime}\}}\right)B_{i}^{\prime}

Then by the triangle inequality,

d_{\mathsf{TV}}(A_{i},B_{j})\leqslant\frac{P_{ij}}{\max\{w_{i},w_{j}^{\prime}\}}d_{\mathsf{TV}}(A_{ij},B_{ij})+\left(1-\frac{P_{ij}}{\max\{w_{i},w_{j}^{\prime}\}}\right)

P_{ij}\cdot d_{\mathsf{TV}}(A_{ij},B_{ij})\geqslant P_{ij}-(1-d_{\mathsf{TV}}(A_{i},B_{j}))\max\{w_{i},w_{j}^{\prime}\}.

(9.3)

Combining (
9.2
) and (
9.3
), we have the following inequality on the TV distance between mixtures and the TV distance between components

d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}^{\prime})\geqslant\sum_{i,j}\left(P_{ij}-(1-d_{\mathsf{TV}}(A_{i},B_{j}))\max\{w_{i},w_{j}^{\prime}\}\right).

(9.4)

By the lower bounds on
d_{\mathsf{TV}}(A_{i},B_{j})
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\varepsilon\geqslant d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}^{\prime})&\geqslant\sum_{i,j}P_{ij}-\sum_{i\neq j}(1-d_{\mathsf{TV}}(A_{i},B_{j}))\max\{w_{i},w_{j}^{\prime}\}-\sum_{i}(1-d_{\mathsf{TV}}(A_{i},B_{i}))\max\{w_{i},w_{i}^{\prime}\}\\
&\geqslant 1-\sum_{i\neq j}\varepsilon-\sum_{i}\max\{w_{i},w_{i}^{\prime}\}+\sum_{i}\max\{w_{i},w_{i}^{\prime}\}d_{\mathsf{TV}}(A_{i},B_{i})\\
&\geqslant 1-\sum_{i\neq j}\varepsilon-\sum_{i}\max\{w_{i},w_{i}^{\prime}\}+w_{\min}d_{\mathsf{TV}}(A_{1},B_{1})\end{split}

(9.5)

where
A_{1},B_{1}
can be replaced by any
A_{i},B_{i}
pair. Let
k=\max\{k_{1},k_{2}\}
.
When
i\neq j
and
d_{\mathsf{TV}}(A_{i},B_{j})\geqslant 1-\varepsilon
, we plug it into Equation (
9.4
) and get

\displaystyle\varepsilon\geqslant d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}^{\prime})\geqslant\sum_{i\neq j}P_{ij}-(1-d_{\mathsf{TV}}(A_{i},B_{j}))\max\{w_{i},w_{j}^{\prime}\}\geqslant\sum_{i\neq j}(P_{ij}-\varepsilon).

This implies

\sum_{i\neq j}P_{ij}\leqslant k^{2}\varepsilon.

Then we can bound
\sum_{i}\max\{w_{i},w_{i}^{\prime}\}-1
in Equation (
9.5
)

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\max\{w_{i},w_{i}^{\prime}\}-1=\sum_{i}\max\{w_{i},w_{i}^{\prime}\}-w_{i}\leqslant\sum_{i\neq j}P_{ij}\leqslant k^{2}\varepsilon.

Plugging this bound into Equation (
9.5
), for any
i
, we have

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{TV}}(A_{i},B_{i})\leqslant\frac{1}{w_{\min}}\left(k^{2}\varepsilon+\sum_{i}\max\{w_{i},w_{i}^{\prime}\}-1\right)\leqslant\frac{2k^{2}\varepsilon}{\varepsilon^{c}}.

∎

Proof of Lemma
9.9
.

We apply Fact
9.12
on the union set of components of
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
with parameter
\delta
to find a partition
R_{1},\dots,R_{\ell}
. Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{G_{j}\in R_{i}}w_{j}G_{j}}{\sum_{G_{j}\in R_{i}}w_{j}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{i}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{G_{j}^{\prime}\in R_{i}}w_{j}^{\prime}G_{j}^{\prime}}{\sum_{G_{j}^{\prime}\in R_{i}}w_{j}^{\prime}}.

Then for any
i\neq j
, we know
d_{\mathsf{TV}}(G_{a},G_{b}^{\prime})\geqslant 1-\varepsilon^{\delta^{t-1}}
for
G_{a}\in R_{i},G_{b}^{\prime}\in R_{j}
.
By (
9.4
) in the proof of Lemma
9.13
, we have

d_{\mathsf{TV}}(M_{i},M_{j}^{\prime})\geqslant 1-2k\varepsilon^{\delta^{t-1}}.

Then by Lemma
9.13
, for any
i
, there exists
a
such that
d_{\mathsf{TV}}(M_{i},M_{i}^{\prime})\leqslant\varepsilon^{a\delta^{t-1}}
. Let
c_{2}=a\delta^{t-1}
. If we set
\delta=c_{2}c_{3}/\delta^{t-1}=ac_{3}
, the partition satisfies the second conclusion.
∎

Appendix A
Omitted Proofs

In this subsection, we provide the proofs that were omitted from Section
2
and Section
6
.

A.1
Omitted Proofs from Section
2.1

Lemma A.1

(Concentration of low-degree polynomials, Lemma
2.9
restated)
.

Let
T
be a
d
-dimensional, degree-
4
tensor such that
\left\lVert T\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\Delta

for some
\Delta>0
and let
x,y\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)
. Then, with probability at least
1-1/\poly(d)
, the following holds:

\left\lVert T\left(\cdot,\cdot,x,y\right)\right\rVert^{2}_{F}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\log(d)\Delta^{2}\right)\;.

Proof.

We note that

\displaystyle\E\left[\left\|T(\cdot,\cdot,x,y)\right\|_{F}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\E\left[\sum_{i_{1},i_{2}}\left(\sum_{i_{3},i_{4}}T\left(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}\right)x\left(i_{3}\right)y\left(i_{4}\right)\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\E\left[\sum_{i_{1},i_{2}}\left(\sum_{i_{3},i_{4}}T\left(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}\right)^{2}x\left(i_{3}\right)^{2}y\left(i_{4}\right)^{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}}T\left(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}\right)^{2}\leqslant\Delta^{2}\;.

The second equality follows from the fact that
x\left(i_{3}\right),y\left(i_{4}\right)
are independent and have zero means.
So the only non-zero terms are the squares. The third equality follows from the fact that

x\left(i_{3}\right),y\left(i_{4}\right)
are independent with unit variances. Observe that

\left\lVert T\left(\cdot,\cdot,x,y\right)\right\rVert^{2}_{F}
is a degree-
2
polynomial in Gaussian random variables.
Using standard concentration bounds for low-degree Gaussian polynomials
(see, e.g., Theorem 2.3 in
[
DRST14
]
), we obtain

\ProbOp\left[\left\lVert T(\cdot,\cdot,x,y)\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\geqslant t^{2}\E\left[\left\|T(\cdot,\cdot,x,y)\right\|_{F}^{2}\right]\right]\leqslant\exp\left(-ct\right)\;.

Setting
t=\Omega(\log(d))
completes the proof.
∎

A.2
Omitted Proofs from Section
2.2

Lemma A.2

(Spectral SoS Proofs, Lemma
2.23
restated)
.

Let
A
be a
d\times d
matrix. Then for
d
-dimensional vector-valued indeterminate
v
, we have:

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.25113pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.25113pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[8.25113pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-8.25113pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.25113pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\left\{v^{\top}Av\leqslant\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right\}\,.

Proof.

Note that
v
is the only variable in the proof here (
A
is a matrix of constants).
We note that
A\leqslant\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{2}I
or
\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{2}I-A
is PSD and thus
\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{2}I-A=QQ^{\top}
for some
d\times d
matrix
Q
. Thus,
\left\lVert Qv\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=v^{\top}(\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{2}I-A)v=\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{2}^{2}-v^{\top}Av
. Thus,
\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{2}^{2}-v^{\top}Av
is a sum of squares polynomial (namely
\left\lVert Qv\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
) in variable
v
. This completes the proof.

∎

Lemma A.3

(Frobenius Norms of Products of Matrices, Lemma
2.25
restated)
.

Let
B
be a
d\times d
matrix valued indeterminate for some
d\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, for any
0\preceq A\preceq I
,

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.38293pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.38293pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.38293pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.38293pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[10.38293pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{B}$}}}$}}}\left\{\left\lVert AB\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leqslant\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\right\}\,,

and,

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.38293pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.38293pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.38293pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.38293pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[10.38293pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{B}$}}}$}}}\left\{\left\lVert BA\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leqslant\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\right\}\,,

Proof.

The proof of the second claim is similar so we prove only the first. We have:

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.38293pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.38293pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.38293pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.38293pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[10.38293pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{B}$}}}$}}}\left\{\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{F}^{2}=\left\lVert(A+I-A)B\right\rVert_{F}^{2}=\left\lVert AB\right\rVert_{F}^{2}+\left\lVert(I-A)B\right\rVert_{F}^{2}+2\mathrm{tr}((I-A)BB^{\top}A)\right\}

Now,
A-A^{2}\succeq 0
, thus,
A-A^{2}=RR^{\top}
for some
d\times d
matrix
R
. Thus,
\mathrm{tr}((A-A^{2})BB^{\top})=\mathrm{tr}(RR^{\top}BB^{\top})=\left\lVert BR\right\rVert_{F}^{2}
- a sum of squares polynomial of degree
2
in indeterminate
B
. Thus,
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.38293pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.38293pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.38293pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.38293pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[10.38293pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{B}$}}}$}}}\left\{\mathrm{tr}((A-A^{2})BB^{\top})\geqslant 0\right\}
.
∎

A.3
Omitted Proofs from Section
2.3

Lemma A.4

(Shifts Cannot Decrease Variance, Lemma
2.32
restated)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
Q
be a
d\times d
matrix-valued indeterminate, and
C
be a scalar-valued indeterminate.
Then, we have that

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{18.7876pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-18.7876pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[18.7876pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-18.7876pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[18.7876pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q,C}$}}}$}}}\left\{\E_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\left(Q(x)-\E_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}[Q(x)]\right)^{2}\right]\leqslant\E_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\left(Q(x)-C\right)^{2}\right]\right\}\,.

Proof.

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{18.7876pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-18.7876pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[18.7876pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-18.7876pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[18.7876pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q,C}$}}}$}}}\Biggl\{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\left(Q(x)-C\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\left(Q(x)-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[Q(x)\right]+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[Q(x)\right]-C\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\left(Q(x)-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[Q(x)\right]\right)^{2}\right]+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[(Q(x)-C)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377pt+2\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\left(Q(x)-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[Q(x)\right]\right)\left(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[Q(x)\right]-C\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\left(Q(x)-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[Q(x)\right]\right)^{2}\right]+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[(Q(x)-C)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\left(Q(x)-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[Q(x)\right]\right)^{2}\right]\hskip 14.45377pt\Biggr\}\,.

∎

Lemma A.5

(Shifts of Certifiably Hypercontractive Distributions, Lemma
2.33
restated)
.

Let
x
be a mean-
0
random variable with distribution
\mathcal{D}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
t
-certifiably
C
-hypercontractive degree-
2
polynomials. Then, for any fixed constant vector
c\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the random variable
x+c
also has
t
-certifiable
4C
-hypercontractive degree-
2
polynomials.

Proof.

Observe that using that
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[x\right]=0
, we have that

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\left\{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[(x+c)^{\top}Q(x+c)\right]=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[x^{\top}Qx+c^{\top}Qc\right]\right\}\;.

Next, by two applications of the SoS Triangle Inequality (Fact
2.21
),
an application of Lemma
2.32
followed by certifiable hypercontractivity of
\mathcal{D}
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-7.75671pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t^{\prime}}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\Biggl\{

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\left((x+c)^{\top}Q(x+c)-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[(x+c)^{\top}Q(x+c)\right]\right)^{t^{\prime}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\left(\left(x^{\top}Qx-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[x^{\top}Qx\right]\right)+x^{\top}Qc+c^{\top}Qx\right)^{t^{\prime}}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant 4^{t^{\prime}}\left(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\left(x^{\top}Qx-\E_{\mathcal{D}}x^{\top}Qx\right)^{t^{\prime}}\right]+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[(x^{\top}Qc)^{t^{\prime}}\right]+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[(c^{\top}Qx)^{t^{\prime}}\right]\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant 4^{t^{\prime}}(Ct^{\prime})^{t^{\prime}}\left(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\left(x^{\top}Qx-\E_{\mathcal{D}}x^{\top}Qx\right)^{2}\right]^{t^{\prime}/2}+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[(x^{\top}Qc)^{2}\right]^{t^{\prime}/2}+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[(c^{\top}Qx)^{2}\right]^{t^{\prime}/2}\right)\Biggr\}\;.

On the other hand, notice that

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\Biggl\{

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\left((x+c)^{\top}Q(x+c)-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[(x+c)^{\top}Q(x+c)\right]\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\left(x^{\top}Qx-\E_{\mathcal{D}}x^{\top}Qx\right)^{2}\right]+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[(x^{\top}Qc)^{2}\right]+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[(c^{\top}Qx)^{2}\right]\right)\hskip 14.45377pt\Biggr\}\;.

Thus,

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-7.75671pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t^{\prime}}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\Biggl\{

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\left(x^{\top}Qx-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[x^{\top}Qx\right]\right)^{2}\right]^{t^{\prime}/2}+\left(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[(x^{\top}Qc)^{2}\right]\right)^{t^{\prime}/2}+\left(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[(c^{\top}Qx)^{2}\right]\right)^{t^{\prime}/2}

\displaystyle\leqslant 4^{t^{\prime}}(Ct^{\prime})^{t^{\prime}}\left(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\left((x+c)^{\top}Q(x+c)-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[(x+c)^{\top}Q(x+c)\right]\right)^{2}\right]\right)^{t^{\prime}/2}\Biggr\}\;.

As a result, we obtain:

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-7.75671pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t^{\prime}}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\Biggl\{

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\left((x+c)^{\top}Q(x+c)-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[(x+c)^{\top}Q(x+c)\right]\right)^{t^{\prime}}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant(4Ct^{\prime})^{t^{\prime}}\left(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\left((x+c)^{\top}Q(x+c)-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[(x+c)^{\top}Q(x+c)\right]\right)^{2}\right]\right)^{t^{\prime}/2}\Biggr\}\;,

which completes the proof.
∎

Lemma A.6

(Mixtures of Certifiably Hypercontractive Distributions,
Lemma
2.34
restated)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{k}
have
t
-certifiable
C
-hypercontractive degree-
2
polynomials on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
for some fixed constant
C
. Then, any mixture
\mathcal{D}=\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathcal{D}_{i}
also has
t
-certifiably
(C/\alpha)
-hypercontractive
degree-
2
polynomials for
\alpha=\min_{i\leqslant k,w_{i}>0}w_{i}
.

Proof.

Applying Lemma
2.21
followed by SoS Hölder’s inequality on the second term
and followed by a final application of SoS Hölder’s inequality (Fact
2.20
), we obtain:

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-7.75671pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t^{\prime}}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\Biggl\{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\left(x^{\top}Qx-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[x^{\top}Qx\right]\right)^{t^{\prime}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\left(x^{\top}Qx-\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}_{i}}\left[x^{\top}Qx\right]\right)^{t^{\prime}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}_{i}}\left[\left(x^{\top}Qx-\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}_{i}}\left[x^{\top}Qx\right]\right)^{t^{\prime}}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant 2^{t^{\prime}}\Biggl(\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}_{i}}\left[\left(x^{\top}Qx-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}_{i}}\left[x^{\top}Qx\right]\right)^{t^{\prime}}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377pt+\left(\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}_{i}}\left[x^{\top}Qx-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}_{i}}\left[x^{\top}Qx\right]\right]^{t^{\prime}}\right)\Biggr)

\displaystyle\leqslant 2^{t^{\prime}}\Biggl((Ct^{\prime})^{t^{\prime}}\left(\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}_{i}}\left[\left(x^{\top}Qx-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}_{i}}\left[x^{\top}Qx\right]\right)^{2}\right]\right)^{t^{\prime}/2}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377pt+\sum_{i}w_{i}\left(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}_{i}}\left[\left(x^{\top}Qx-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}_{i}}\left[x^{\top}Qx\right]\right)^{2}\right]\right)^{t^{\prime}}\Biggr)

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\frac{4Ct^{\prime}}{\alpha}\right)^{t^{\prime}}\left(\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}_{i}}\left[\left(x^{\top}Qx-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}_{i}}\left[x^{\top}Qx\right]\right)^{2}\right]\right)^{t^{\prime}/2}\Biggr\}\,.

On the other hand, note that by Lemma
2.32
, we know that

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\Biggl\{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\left(x^{\top}Qx-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[x\right]^{\top}Qx\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}_{i}}\left[\left(x^{\top}Qx-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[x^{\top}Qx\right]\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}_{i}}\left[\left(x^{\top}Qx-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}_{i}}\left[x^{\top}Qx\right]\right)^{2}\right]\Biggr\}\,.

Combining the two equations above completes the proof.
∎

Corollary A.7

(Certifiable Hypercontractivity of
k
-Mixtures of Gaussians, Corollary
2.35
restated)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a
k
-mixture of Gaussians
\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
with weights
w_{i}\geqslant\alpha
for every
i\in[k]
.
Then,
\mathcal{D}
has
t
-certifiably
4/\alpha
-hypercontractive degree-
2
polynomials.

Proof.

From
[
KOTZ14
]
, we know that the standard Gaussian random variable has
t
-certifiably
1
-hypercontractive degree-
2
polynomials. From Fact
2.31
, we immediately obtain that for any
PSD matrix
\Sigma
, the Gaussian
\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
also has
t
-certifiable
1
-hypercontractive degree-
2
polynomials.
From Lemma
2.33
, we obtain that for any
\mu
, the Gaussian

\mathcal{N}(\mu,\Sigma)
has
t
-certifiable
4
-hypercontractive degree-
2
polynomials. Finally, applying
Lemma
2.34
to
\mathcal{D}_{i}=\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
and mixture weights

w_{1},w_{2},\ldots w_{k}
, yields that
\mathcal{D}=\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
has
t
-certifiably
4/\alpha
-hypercontractive
degree-
2
polynomials. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma A.8

(Linear Transformations of Certifiably Bounded-Variance Distributions, Lemma
2.39
restated)
.

For
d\in\mathbb{N}
, let
x
be a random variable with distribution
\mathcal{D}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that for
d\times d
matrix-valued indeterminate
Q
,
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\left\{\E_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}(x^{\top}Qx-\E_{\mathcal{D}}x^{\top}Qx)^{2}\leqslant\left\lVert\Sigma^{1/2}Q\Sigma^{1/2}\right\rVert^{2}_{F}\right\}
. Let
A
be an arbitrary
d\times d
matrix and let
x^{\prime}=Ax
be the random variable with covariance
\Sigma^{\prime}=AA^{\top}
. Then, we have that

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\left\{\E_{x^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}({x^{\prime}}^{\top}Qx^{\prime}-\E_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}{x^{\prime}}^{\top}Qx^{\prime})^{2}\leqslant\left\lVert{\Sigma^{\prime}}^{1/2}Q{\Sigma^{\prime}}^{1/2}\right\rVert^{2}_{F}\right\}\,.

Proof.

The covariance of
x^{\prime}
is
AA^{\top}=\Sigma^{\prime}
, say. Let
{\Sigma^{\prime}}^{1/2}
be the PSD square root of
\Sigma^{\prime}
. The proof follows by noting that
{x^{\prime}}^{\top}Qx^{\prime}=(Ax)^{\top}Q(Ax)=x^{\top}(A^{\top}QA)x^{\top}
and that
\left\lVert A^{\top}QA\right\rVert_{F}^{2}=\mathrm{tr}(A^{\top}QAA^{\top}QA)=\mathrm{tr}(AA^{\top}QAA^{\top}Q)=\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma^{\prime}Q\Sigma^{\prime}Q)=\mathrm{tr}({\Sigma^{\prime}}^{1/2}Q{\Sigma^{\prime}}^{1/2}{\Sigma^{\prime}}^{1/2}Q{\Sigma^{\prime}}^{1/2})=\left\lVert{\Sigma^{\prime}}^{1/2}Q{\Sigma^{\prime}}^{1/2}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}
.
∎

Lemma A.9

(Variance of Degree-
2
Polynomials of Standard Gaussians, Lemma
2.40
restated)
.

We have that

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\left\{\E_{\mathcal{N}(0,I)}\left(x^{\top}Qx-\E_{\mathcal{N}(0,I)}x^{\top}Qx\right)^{2}\leqslant 3\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\right\}\,.

Proof.

We will view
xx^{\top}
and
I\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
as
d^{2}
-dimensional vectors. Consider the matrix

\E_{x\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)}(xx^{\top}-I)(xx^{\top}-I)^{\top}
. The diagonal of this matrix is
2I_{d^{2}}
.
The off-diagonal part has exactly one non-zero entry in any row (which corresponds to entry indexed by
(i,j)
and
(j,i)
for
i\neq j
),
and thus has spectral norm at most
1
by the Gershgorin circle theorem.
Thus,
\E_{x\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)}(xx^{\top}-I)(xx^{\top}-I)^{\top}\preceq 3I_{d^{2}}
.

We thus have:
