\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\Biggl\{\E_{\mathcal{N}(0,I)}\left(x^{\top}Qx-\E_{\mathcal{N}(0,I)}x^{\top}Qx\right)^{2}=\E_{\mathcal{N}(0,I)}\left\langle xx^{\top}-I,Q\right\rangle^{2}=\E_{\mathcal{N}(0,I)}\left\langle xx^{\top}-I,Q\right\rangle^{2}\\
\leqslant\left\lVert\E_{x\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)}(xx^{\top}-I)(xx^{\top}-I^{\prime})^{\top}\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leqslant 3\left\lVert I_{d^{2}}\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}=3\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\Biggr\}\,.

(A.1)

∎

Lemma A.10

(Variance of Degree-
2
Polynomials of Mixtures, Lemma
2.43
restated)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathcal{D}_{i}
be a
k
-mixture of distributions
\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{k}
with means
\mu_{i}
and covariances
\Sigma_{i}
. Let
\mu=\sum_{i}w_{i}\mu_{i}
be the mean of
\mathcal{M}
. Suppose that each of
\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{k}
have certifiably
C
-bounded-variance i.e. for
Q
: a symmetric
d\times d
matrix-valued indeterminate.

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\left\{\E_{x^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{D}_{i}}({x^{\prime}}^{\top}Qx^{\prime}-\E_{\mathcal{D}_{i}}{x^{\prime}}^{\top}Qx^{\prime})^{2}\leqslant C\left\lVert{\Sigma^{\prime}}^{1/2}Q{\Sigma^{\prime}}^{1/2}\right\rVert^{2}_{F}\right\}\,.

Further, suppose that for some
H>1
,
\left\|\mu_{i}-\mu\right\|_{2}^{2},\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-I\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant H
for every
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
.
Then, we have that

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\left\{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{M}}\left[\left(x^{\top}Qx-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{M}}\left[x^{\top}Qx\right]\right)^{2}\right]\leqslant 100CH^{2}\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\right\}\,.

Proof.

We have the following sequence of (in-)equalities:

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}

\displaystyle\Biggl\{\E_{x\sim\mathcal{M}}\left(x^{\top}Qx-\E_{x\sim\mathcal{M}}x^{\top}Qx\right)^{2}=\sum_{i\leqslant k}w_{i}\E_{x\sim\mathcal{D}_{i}}\left(x^{\top}Qx-\E_{x\sim\mathcal{M}}x^{\top}Qx\right)^{2}

(A.2)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\leqslant k}w_{i}\E_{x\sim\mathcal{D}_{i}}\left(x^{\top}Qx-\E_{x\sim\mathcal{D}_{i}}x^{\top}Qx+\E_{x\sim\mathcal{D}_{i}}x^{\top}Qx-\E_{x\sim\mathcal{M}}x^{\top}Qx\right)^{2}

(A.3)

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\sum_{i\leqslant k}w_{i}\E_{x\sim\mathcal{D}_{i}}\left(x^{\top}Qx-\E_{x\sim\mathcal{D}_{i}}x^{\top}Qx\right)^{2}+2\sum_{i}w_{i}\left(\E_{x\sim\mathcal{D}_{i}}x^{\top}Qx-\E_{x\sim\mathcal{M}}x^{\top}Qx\right)^{2}\Biggr\}\;,

(A.4)

where the third line follows from Fact
2.21
(SoS Almost Triangle Inequality).

Let us first bound the 2nd term in the RHS above.
Towards that, let
\Sigma=\sum_{i}w_{i}((\mu_{i}-\mu)(\mu_{i}-\mu)^{\top}+\Sigma_{i})

be the covariance of the mixture
\mathcal{M}
. Then, notice that

\Sigma=\sum_{i}w_{i}((\mu_{i}-\mu)(\mu_{i}-\mu)^{\top}+\Sigma_{i})=\sum_{i}w_{i}\mu_{i}\mu_{i}^{\top}+\sum_{i}w_{i}\Sigma_{i}-\mu\mu^{\top}
.
Thus, we can write

\displaystyle\mu_{i}\mu_{i}^{\top}+\Sigma_{i}-\Sigma-\mu\mu^{\top}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\neq i}w_{j}(\mu_{i}\mu_{i}^{\top}-\mu_{j}\mu_{j}^{\top})+\sum_{j\neq i}w_{j}(\Sigma_{i}-\Sigma_{j})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\neq i}w_{j}(\mu_{j}-\mu)(\mu_{j}-\mu)^{\top}-\sum_{j\neq i}(\mu-\mu_{j})(\mu-\mu_{j})^{\top}+\sum_{j\neq i}w_{j}(\Sigma_{i}-\Sigma_{j})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\neq i}w_{j}(\mu_{j}-\mu)(\mu_{j}-\mu)^{\top}-\sum_{j\neq i}(\mu-\mu_{j})(\mu-\mu_{j})^{\top}+\sum_{j\neq i}w_{j}(\Sigma_{i}-I)-\sum_{j\neq i}w_{j}(\Sigma_{j}-I)\,.

Here, in the second to last step, we added and subtracted
\sum_{j\neq i}w_{j}\mu\mu^{\top}

and used that
\sum_{i}w_{i}\mu_{i}=\mu
, and in the last step we added and subtracted
\sum_{j\neq i}w_{j}I
.

By application of the triangle inequality for Frobenius norm to the RHS of the above,
we have that:

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mu_{i}\mu_{i}^{\top}+\Sigma_{i}-\mu\mu^{\top}-\Sigma\right\rVert_{F}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{j\neq i}w_{j}\left\lVert(\mu_{i}-\mu)(\mu_{i}-\mu)^{\top}\right\rVert_{F}+\sum_{j\neq i}w_{j}\left\lVert(\mu_{j}-\mu)(\mu_{j}-\mu)^{\top}\right\rVert_{F}

\displaystyle+\sum_{j\neq i}w_{j}\left\lVert(\Sigma_{i}-I)\right\rVert_{F}+\sum_{j\neq i}w_{j}\left\lVert(I-\Sigma_{j})\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant H+H+H+H=4H\;.

Using the SoS version of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality (Fact
2.20
)
on indeterminate
Q
and constant
\mu\mu^{\top}-\mu_{i}\mu_{i}^{\top}+\Sigma_{i}-\Sigma
and the above bound,
we have:

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}

\displaystyle\Biggl\{\sum_{i}w_{i}\left(\E_{x\sim\mathcal{D}_{i}}x^{\top}Qx-\E_{x\sim\mathcal{M}}x^{\top}Qx\right)^{2}=\sum_{i}w_{i}\left(\left\langle\mu_{i}\mu_{i}^{\top}+\Sigma_{i},Q\right\rangle-\left\langle\mu\mu^{\top}+\Sigma,Q\right\rangle\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i}w_{i}\left\lVert\mu\mu^{\top}-\mu_{i}\mu_{i}^{\top}+\Sigma_{i}-\Sigma\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leqslant 16H^{2}\sum_{i}w_{i}\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}=16H^{2}\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\Biggr\}\,.

Let us now bound the first term in the RHS of (
A.4
) above.
First, observe that
x^{\top}Qx-\E_{\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})}x^{\top}Qx=(x-\mu_{i})^{\top}Q(x-\mu_{i})-\E_{\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})}(x-\mu_{i})^{\top}Q(x-\mu_{i})+2(x-\mu_{i})^{\top}Q\mu_{i}
. Thus, using Fact
2.21
and Lemma
2.42
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}

\displaystyle\Biggl\{\sum_{i\leqslant k}w_{i}\E_{\mathcal{D}_{i}}\left(x^{\top}Qx-\E_{x\sim\mathcal{D}_{i}}x^{\top}Qx\right)^{2}

(A.5)

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\sum_{i\leqslant k}w_{i}\E_{\mathcal{D}_{i}}\left((x-\mu_{i})^{\top}Q(x-\mu_{i})-\E_{x\sim\mathcal{D}_{i}}(x-\mu_{i})^{\top}Q(x-\mu_{i})\right)^{2}+8\sum_{i\leqslant k}w_{i}\E_{\mathcal{D}_{i}}\left((x-\mu_{i})^{\top}Q\mu_{i}\right)^{2}

(A.6)

\displaystyle\leqslant 6\sum_{i}w_{i}C\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}^{1/2}Q\Sigma_{i}^{1/2}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}+8\sum_{i\leqslant k}w_{i}\E_{\mathcal{D}_{i}}\left((x-\mu_{i})^{\top}Q\mu_{i}\right)^{2}\Biggr\}\,.

(A.7)

For the first term, note that
\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant 1+\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-I\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant 1+H
.
Thus,
\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}^{1/2}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\sqrt{1+H}
. Thus, we have that
\Sigma_{i}^{1/2}\preceq I+(\Sigma_{i}^{1/2}-I)\preceq\sqrt{1+H}I
.
Using Lemma
2.25
with
A=(1+H)^{-1/2}\Sigma_{i}^{1/2}
and
B=Q\Sigma_{i}^{1/2}
,
we have:
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\left\{\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}^{1/2}Q\Sigma_{i}^{1/2}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leqslant(1+H)\left\lVert Q\Sigma_{i}^{1/2}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\right\}
.
By another application of Lemma
2.25
, we have:

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\left\{\left\lVert Q\Sigma_{i}^{1/2}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leqslant(1+H)\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\right\}
.
Thus, altogether, we have:
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\left\{\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}^{1/2}Q\Sigma_{i}^{1/2}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leqslant(1+H)^{2}\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\right\}
.
Using our assumption that
1<H
, we thus have:

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}

\displaystyle\Biggl\{\sum_{i}w_{i}C\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}^{1/2}Q\Sigma_{i}^{1/2}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leqslant C(1+H)^{2}\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leqslant 4CH^{2}\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\Biggr\}\,.

For the second term, first observe that the following equality of quadratic polynomials in indeterminate
Q
:

\left((x-\mu_{i})^{\top}Q\mu_{i}\right)^{2}=\left((\Sigma_{i}^{\dagger/2}(x-\mu_{i}))^{\top}\Sigma_{i}^{1/2}Q\mu_{i}\right)^{2}
.
Thus,
\E_{x\sim\mathcal{D}_{i}}\left((x-\mu_{i})^{\top}Q\mu_{i}\right)^{2}=\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}^{1/2}Q\mu_{i}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
.
Next, by the SoS Cauchy-Schwarz inequality (Fact
2.20
), we have that

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\left\{\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}^{1/2}Q\mu_{i}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\mathrm{tr}(\mu_{i}\mu_{i}^{\top}Q\Sigma_{i}Q)\leqslant H\mathrm{tr}(Q\Sigma_{i}Q)=H\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}^{1/2}Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\right\}\;.

Applying Lemma
2.25
with the observation above that
\Sigma_{i}^{1/2}\leqslant(1+H)^{1/2}I
yields:

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\left\{\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}^{1/2}Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leqslant(1+H)\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\right\}
.
Thus, altogether, we obtain:

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\left\{\E_{x\sim\mathcal{D}_{i}}(x-\mu_{i})^{\top}Q\mu_{i}\right\}^{2}\leqslant H(1+H)^{2}\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leqslant 4H^{3}\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}
. We thus have:

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}

\displaystyle\Biggl\{\sum_{i\leqslant k}w_{i}\E_{\mathcal{D}_{i}}\left((x-\mu_{i})^{\top}Q\mu_{i}\right)^{2}\leqslant 4H^{3}\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\Biggr\}\,.

Plugging in these bounds into (
A.7
) completes the proof.
∎

As an immediate corollary of Lemma
2.39
and Lemma
2.43
,
we obtain:

Lemma A.11

(Variance of Degree-
2
Polynomials of Mixtures of Gaussians, Lemma
2.44
restated)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
be a
k
-mixture of Gaussians with
w_{i}\geqslant\alpha
,
mean
\mu=\sum_{i}w_{i}\mu_{i}
and covariance
\Sigma=\sum_{i}w_{i}((\mu_{i}-\mu)(\mu_{i}-\mu)^{\top}+\Sigma_{i})
.
Suppose that for some
H>1
,
\left\lVert\Sigma^{\dagger/2}(\Sigma_{i}-I)\Sigma^{\dagger/2}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant H
for every
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
.
Let
Q
be a symmetric
d\times d
matrix-valued indeterminate. Then for
H^{\prime}=\max\{H,1/\alpha\}
,

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\left\{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{M}}\left[\left(x^{\top}Qx-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{M}}\left[x^{\top}Qx\right]\right)^{2}\right]\leqslant 100{H^{\prime}}^{2}\left\lVert\Sigma^{1/2}Q\Sigma^{1/2}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\right\}\,.

Proof.

Let
\Sigma=U\Lambda U^{\top}
be the covariance of the mixture
\mathcal{M}
along with its eigendecomposition.
We want to apply Lemma
2.43
and Lemma
2.39

with the linear transformation
x\rightarrow Ax
for
A=\Lambda^{\dagger/2}U^{\top}
.
For this, we need to check that the conditions of the Lemma
2.43
are met after this linear transformation.
The new component covariance is
\Sigma_{i}^{\prime}=A\Sigma_{i}A^{\top}
and the hypothesis implies that they are within
H
in Frobenius distance of the new mixture covariance
I^{\prime}=A\Sigma A^{\top}
(
I
in the range space of
\Sigma
).
The new means of the components after the linear transformation are
\mu_{i}^{\prime}=A\mu_{i}

and the new mixture mean is
\mu^{\prime}=A\mu
. Thus, noting that
I^{\prime}=\sum_{i}w_{i}(\mu_{i}^{\prime}-\mu^{\prime})(\mu^{\prime}-\mu^{\prime})^{\top}+\sum_{i}w_{i}\Sigma_{i}^{\prime}
,
and since each of the terms in the RHS of the preceding equality are PSD, we must have that

I^{\prime}\succeq w_{i}(\mu_{i}^{\prime}-\mu^{\prime})(\mu_{i}^{\prime}-\mu^{\prime})^{\top}
for every
i
. Thus,

1=\left\lVert I^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2}\geqslant w_{i}\left\lVert(\mu_{i}^{\prime}-\mu^{\prime})(\mu_{i}^{\prime}-\mu^{\prime})^{\top}\right\rVert_{2}=\left\lVert\mu_{i}^{\prime}-\mu^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
.
Rearranging yields that
\left\lVert\mu_{i}^{\prime}-\mu^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leqslant 1/w_{i}\leqslant 1/\alpha
. Thus, we can now apply
Lemma
2.43
to the linearly transformed mixture and the conclusion follows.
∎

A.4
Omitted Proofs from Section
2.4

Lemma A.12

(Lemma
2.49
restated)
.

If
X
satisfies Condition
2.47
with respect to
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})

with parameters
(\gamma,t)
, then if
w_{i}\geqslant\gamma
for all
i\in[k]
,
and if for some
B\geqslant 0
we have that
\left\lVert\mu_{i}\right\rVert_{2}^{2},\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant B
for all
i\in[k]
,
then for all
m\leqslant t
, we have that:

\left\lVert\E_{x\in_{u}X}[x^{\otimes m}]-\E_{x\sim M}[x^{\otimes m}]\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leqslant\gamma^{2}m^{O(m)}B^{m}d^{m}\;.

Proof.

We begin by noting that for any symmetric
m
-tensor
A
we have that

\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{F}^{2}{\leqslant}m^{O(m)}(\E_{v\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)}[\langle A,v^{\otimes m}\rangle^{2}]).

This is because, in the notation of
[
DKS18
]
,
the squared expectation of
\langle A,v^{\otimes m}\rangle
is

\E[\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(v)^{2}]\geqslant m!\E[h_{A}(v)^{2}]=m!\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{F}^{2}
, where the first inequality holds
because
\sqrt{m!}h_{A}(v)
is the degree-
m
harmonic part of
\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(v)
,
and the equality is by Claim 3.22. Therefore, to prove the lemma, it suffices to bound

\displaystyle\E_{v\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)}\left[\left(\E_{x\in_{u}X}[(v\cdot x)^{m}]-\E_{x\sim\mathcal{M}}[(v\cdot x)^{m}]\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\E_{v\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{x\in X_{i}}(v\cdot x)^{m}-w_{i}\E_{x\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})}(v\cdot x)^{m}\right)\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\E_{v\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=0}^{m}\binom{m}{j}\mu_{i}^{m-j}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{x\in X_{i}}(v\cdot(x-\mu_{i}))^{j}-w_{i}\E_{x\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})}(v\cdot(x-\mu_{i}))^{j}\right)\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\E_{v\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=0}^{m}\binom{m}{j}|v\cdot\mu_{i}|^{m-j}\left(w_{i}\gamma m!(v^{T}\Sigma v)^{j/2}\right)\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\gamma^{2}m^{O(m)}\E_{v\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(w_{i}(|v\cdot\mu_{i}|+(v^{T}\Sigma v)^{1/2})^{2m}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\gamma^{2}m^{O(m)}\E_{v\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)}\left[2B^{m}\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{2}^{2m}\right]

\displaystyle\gamma^{2}m^{O(m)}B^{m}d^{m}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma A.13

(Lemma
2.50
restated)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
. Let
S\subset[k]
with
\sum_{i\in S}w_{i}=w
, and let

\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\sum_{i\in S}(w_{i}/w)\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
. Then if
X
satisfies Condition
2.47
with respect to
\mathcal{M}
with parameters
(\gamma,t)
for some
\gamma<1/(2k)

with the corresponding partition being
X=X_{1}\cup X_{2}\cup\ldots\cup X_{k}
,
then
X^{\prime}=\bigcup_{i\in S}X_{i}
satisfies Condition
2.47

with respect to
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
with parameters
(O(k\gamma/w),t)
.

Proof.

After noting that
|X^{\prime}|=w|X|(1+O(k\gamma/w))
, the rest follows straightforwardly from the definitions using the partition
X^{\prime}=\bigcup_{i\in S}X_{i}
.
∎

Lemma A.14

(Lemma
2.51
restated)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
and let
n
be an integer at least
kt^{Ct}d^{t}/\gamma^{3}
,
for a sufficiently large universal constant
C>0
, some
\gamma>0
, and some
t\in\mathbb{N}
.
If
X
consists of
n
i.i.d. samples from
\mathcal{M}
, then
X
satisfies Condition
2.47

with respect to
\mathcal{M}
with parameters
(\gamma,t)
with high probability.

Proof.

We will show that Condition
2.47
holds with high probability
using that partition where
X_{i}
is the set of samples drawn from the
i
-th component of
\mathcal{M}
.
Note that the second part of Condition
2.47
holds with high probability,
so long as
n
is a sufficiently large multiple of
d/\gamma^{2}
by the VC-Theorem
[
DL01
]
.
In particular, if we think of samples as being drawn from
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[k]
,
where the second coordinate denotes the component that the sample was drawn from,
the second part of Condition
2.47
says that the empirical probability
of any event
H\times\{i\}
is correct to within additive error
\gamma
. It is easy to see and well-known
that the class of such events has VC-dimension
O(d)
, from which the desired bound follows.

For the first part of Condition
2.47
, we claim that it holds with high probability
so long as
n\geqslant kt^{Ct}d^{t}/\gamma^{3}
. To prove this, we show it separately for each
i
with
w_{i}\geqslant\gamma

(as otherwise there is nothing to prove) and take a union bound.
As Condition
2.47
is invariant under affine transformations,
we may perform an invertible affine transformation so that
\mu_{i}=0
and
\Sigma_{i}

is the projection onto the first
d^{\prime}
coordinates, for some
d^{\prime}
. It is clear that only the first
d^{\prime}
coordinates
of any element of
X_{i}
will be non-zero. We claim that the first part of our condition will follow for a given
m
,
so long as
\left||X_{i}|/n-w_{i}\right|\leqslant\gamma w_{i}
(which holds with high probability if
n\gg\log(k)/\gamma^{3}
),
and

\left\lVert\E_{x\in_{u}X_{i}}[x^{\otimes m}]-\E_{x\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d^{\prime}})}[x^{\otimes m}]\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leqslant\gamma^{2}\;,

(A.8)

as
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{x\in X_{i}}\langle v,x-\mu_{i}\rangle^{m}=w_{i}(1\pm\gamma)\langle\E_{x\in_{u}X_{i}}[x^{\otimes m}],v^{\otimes m}\rangle.

It is easy to see that each entry of the tensor on the left hand side of Equation (
A.8
)
has mean
0
and variance
m^{O(m)}/|X_{i}|
, and thus the expected size of the left hand side
is
m^{O(m)}d^{m}/|X_{i}|.
Then, when
n\geqslant k^{Ck}d^{4k}/\gamma^{3}
for a sufficiently large constant
C
,
all parts of our condition hold with high probability. This completes the proof.
∎

A.5
Omitted Proofs from Section
6

Lemma A.15

(Frobenius Distance to TV Distance, Lemma
6.5
, restated)
.

Suppose
\mathcal{N}(\mu_{1},\Sigma_{1}),\mathcal{N}(\mu_{2},\Sigma_{2})
are Gaussians with
\left\|\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}\right\|_{2}\leqslant\delta
and

\left\|\Sigma_{1}-\Sigma_{2}\right\|_{F}\leqslant\delta
. If the eigenvalues of
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
are at least
\lambda>0
, then

d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{N}(\mu_{1},\Sigma_{1}),\mathcal{N}(\mu_{2},\Sigma_{2}))=O(\delta/\lambda)\;.

Proof.

By Fact
2.1
, we have

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathcal{N}(\mu_{1},\Sigma_{1}),\mathcal{N}(\mu_{2},\Sigma_{2})\right)=\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\left((\mu_{1}-\mu_{2})^{\top}\Sigma_{1}^{-1}(\mu_{1}-\mu_{2})\right)^{1/2}+\|\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{2}\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}-I\|_{F})\right).

Then the first term is

\left\langle\mu_{1}-\mu_{2},\Sigma_{1}^{-1}(\mu_{1}-\mu_{2})\right\rangle^{1/2}\leqslant(\left\|\Sigma_{1}^{-1}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\left\|\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}\right\|_{2}^{2})^{1/2}\leqslant\delta/\sqrt{\lambda}
.
The second term is

\displaystyle\|\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{2}\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}-I\|^{2}_{F}

\displaystyle=\left\|\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}(\Sigma_{1}-\Sigma_{2})\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}\right\|^{2}_{F}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{tr}\left(\left(\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}(\Sigma_{1}-\Sigma_{2})\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathrm{tr}\left((\Sigma_{1}-\Sigma_{2})^{2}\right)(1/\lambda)^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant(\delta/\lambda)^{2}.

Thus,

d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{N}(\mu_{1},\Sigma_{1}),\mathcal{N}(\mu_{2},\Sigma_{2}))=O(\delta/\sqrt{\lambda}+\delta/\lambda)=O(\delta/\lambda)\;.

∎

Lemma A.16

(Component Moments to Mixture Moments, Lemma
6.6
restated)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i\in[k]}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
be a
k
-mixture such that
w_{i}\geqslant\alpha
,
for some
0<\alpha<1
, and
\mathcal{M}
has mean
\mu
and covariance
\Sigma
and for all
i\neq j\in[k]
,

\left\lVert\Sigma^{\dagger/2}\left(\Sigma_{i}-\Sigma_{j}\right)\Sigma^{\dagger/2}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant 1/\sqrt{\alpha}
.
Let
X
be a multiset of
n
samples satisfying Condition
2.47

with respect to
\mathcal{M}
with parameters
(\gamma,t)
, for
0<\gamma<(dk/\alpha)^{-ct}
,
for a sufficiently large constant
c
, and
t\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\mathcal{D}
be the uniform distribution over
X
.
Then,
\mathcal{D}
is
2t
-certifiably
(c/\alpha)
-hypercontractive and for
d\times d
-matrix-valued indeterminate
Q
,
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\left\{\E_{\mathcal{M}}\left(x^{\top}Qx-\E_{\mathcal{M}}x^{\top}Qx\right)^{2}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(1/\alpha\right)\left\lVert\Sigma^{1/2}Q\Sigma^{1/2}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\right\}
.

Proof.

First, since
\mathcal{M}
is a
k
-mixture of Gaussians with minimum mixing weight
w_{\min}\geqslant\alpha
, it follows from Corollary
2.35
that
\mathcal{M}
is
t
-certifiably
(4/\alpha)
hypercontractive. Further, since
X
satisfies Condition
2.47
with parameters
(\gamma,t)
, it follows from Lemma
2.48
that the set
X^{\prime}=\{\Sigma^{\dagger/2}(x_{i}-\mu)\}_{x_{i}\in X}
also satisfies Condition
2.47
with parameters
(\gamma,t)
w.r.t.
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\sum_{i\in[k]}w_{i}\mathcal{N}\left(\Sigma^{\dagger/2}(\mu_{i}-\mu),\Sigma^{\dagger/2}\Sigma_{i}\Sigma^{\dagger/2}\right)
. Since
\left\lVert\Sigma^{\dagger/2}\Sigma_{i}\Sigma^{\dagger/2}\right\rVert_{\textrm{op}}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(1/\alpha\right)
, it follows from Lemma
2.49
that for all
m\leqslant t
,
\left\lVert\E_{x\in_{u}X^{\prime}}[x^{\otimes m}]-\E_{x\sim M^{\prime}}[x^{\otimes m}]\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leqslant\gamma^{2}m^{O(m)}d^{m}(1/\alpha)^{m}
. Since
\gamma<(dk/\alpha)^{-O(t)}
, it follows from Fact
2.45
that
X
is
2t
-certifiably
(c/\alpha)
-hypercontractive.

By assumption, for all
i\neq j\in[k]
, we have that

\left\lVert\Sigma^{\dagger/2}\left(\Sigma_{i}-\Sigma_{j}\right)\Sigma^{\dagger/2}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant 1/\sqrt{\alpha}
.
We can now apply Lemma
2.44

to obtain

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\left\{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{M}}\left[\left(x^{\top}Qx-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\mathcal{M}}\left[x^{\top}Qx\right]\right)^{2}\right]\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(1/\alpha\right)\left\lVert\Sigma^{1/2}Q\Sigma^{1/2}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\right\}\,.

Therefore, it follows from Fact
2.45
that since
X
satisfies Condition
2.47
with parameters
(\gamma,t)
, the uniform distribution
\mathcal{D}_{X}
on
X
,
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{10.29771pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-10.29771pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[10.29771pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Q}$}}}$}}}\left\{\E_{x\sim\mathcal{D}_{X}}(x^{\top}Qx-\E_{x\sim\mathcal{D}_{X}}x^{\top}Qx)^{2}\leqslant\mathcal{O}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(1/\alpha\right)\left\lVert\Sigma^{1/2}Q\Sigma^{1/2}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\right\}
.
∎

Appendix B
Bit Complexity Analysis

Here we address numerical issues related to our computation. We begin wth the assumption that the eigenvalues of our covariance matrices are bounded below.

Lemma B.1
.

If
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}G_{i}
is a mixture of Gaussians
G_{i}
where each
G_{i}
has mean and covariance of norm at most
2^{b}
for some positive integer
b
and each
G_{i}
has covariance matrix whose eigenvalues are bounded below by some
\lambda>0
. Let
\mathcal{M^{\prime}}
be an
\varepsilon
-corruption of
\mathcal{M}
whose outputs are bounded by
2^{O(b)}
. Let
N
be a sufficiently large polynomial in
d^{k}/\varepsilon
and let
\eta
be
\lambda
divided by a sufficiently large polynomial in
2^{b}d/\varepsilon
(where sufficiently large is degree
O(1)
). Then if our algorithm is given
N
i.i.d. samples from
\mathcal{M^{\prime}}
with each of their coordinates rounded to a nearby multiple of
\eta
(by which we mean one of the two closest), then our algorithm runs in time
\poly(N,b,\log(1/\eta))
and with high probability returns a list of mixtures of Gaussians
X_{i}
with at least one of the
X_{i}

\poly_{k}(\varepsilon)
-close to
\mathcal{M}
in parameter distance.

Proof.

This follows from noting firstly that with high probability the any subset of the rounded samples will have moments
\lambda/\poly(d/\varepsilon)
-close to their moments before rounding. This means that with high probability these rounded samples will satisfy Condition
2.47
. This means that our algorithm satisfies the necessary correctness guarantees. Furthermore, given that our samples now all have bounded bit complexity, it is easy to see that the runtime of our algorithm is polynomial in
N
and the bit complexity.
∎

More generally, as long as the parameters of the components of our mixture can be expressed with bounded bit complexity, we can prove a similar result, without needing any lower bound on the covariances.

Theorem B.2
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}G_{i}
be a mixture of Gaussians where the
G_{i}
are Gaussians whose means and covariance matrices can all be written with coefficients given by rational numbers with bit complexity at most
b
for some integer
b
. Let
\mathcal{M^{\prime}}
be an
\varepsilon
-corruption of
\mathcal{M}
so that with probability
1
the returned points have size
2^{O(b)}
. Let
N
be a sufficiently large polynomial in
d^{k}/\varepsilon
. Then there exists an algorithm that given
b
bit-oracle access to these samples runs in time
\poly(N,b)
and with high probability returns a mixture of Gaussians
X
so that
d_{\mathsf{TV}}(X,\mathcal{M})<\poly_{k}(\varepsilon)
.

Proof.

We begin by showing that we can find a list of hypotheses at least one of which is close. It is then straightforward to show that we can run a tournament over these hypotheses to find a specific one that works. We also assume for simplicity that each
w_{i}
is at least
3\varepsilon
.

We begin by setting
\lambda
to be
2^{-b\cdot d^{kC}}
for a sufficiently large constant
C
. By adding each sample to a random sample from
N(0,\lambda I)
, we can produce samples from
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
, and
\varepsilon
-corruption of
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}\tilde{G_{i}}
where
\tilde{G_{i}}
is the convolution of
G_{i}
with
N(0,\lambda I)
. Note that
\tilde{G}_{i}
is a Gaussian whose covariance has eigenvalues at least
\lambda
. Furthermore, if the covariance matrix of
G_{i}
is non-singular, the smallest eigenvalue of the covariance matrix must be at least
2^{O(b\cdot d)}
, and therefore
d_{\mathsf{TV}}(G_{i},\tilde{G}_{i})<\varepsilon
.

Since the eigenvalues of the components of
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
are bounded below, we can apply Lemma
B.1
to our samples from
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
rounded to an appropriate accuracy
\eta
, and in
\poly(N,b)
-time obtain a list of hypothesis mixtures at least one of which is (with high probability) close to
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
in total variation distance.

If the covariances of all of the
G_{i}
with weights more than some sufficiently large
\poly_{k}(\varepsilon)
are all non-singular, then one of these hypotheses will be close to
\mathcal{M}
. Otherwise, there must be some
i
for which
w_{i}
is relatively large and for which
G_{i}
has singular covariance matrix. In particular, there must be an integer vector
v
with bit complexity
O(bd)
in the kernel of the covariance matrix of
G_{i}
. The hypothesis mixture
X
that is close to
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
in parameter distance must contain some component close to
\tilde{G}_{i}
. Since
\tilde{G}_{i}
has covariance matrix
\tilde{\Sigma}_{i}=\lambda I+\Sigma_{i}
where
\Sigma_{i}
is the covariance matrix of
G_{i}
. We note that
\tilde{\Sigma}_{i}
will have an eigenvalue of
\lambda
and that therefore, our close hypothesis will have an eigenvalue at most
2\lambda
.

If any of our returned hypotheses have any component with a covariance matrix
\Sigma
which has any eigenvalue less than
2\lambda
, we do the following. We consider the quadratic form on integer vectors
v
defined by

Q(v)=v^{T}\Sigma v+\sqrt{\lambda}|v|_{2}^{2}.

We note that if this
\Sigma
is close in parameter distance to a singular
\tilde{\Sigma}_{i}
where
\Sigma_{i}
had a null-vector
v
of norm
2^{O(bd)}
, then for that same value of
v
we will have that
Q(v)<\lambda^{1/4}.
Using the Lovász local lemma in
[
EL73
]
, we can find a
v
so that
Q(v)
is within a
2^{O(d)}
-factor of the minimum possible value over all non-zero, integer vectors
v
. If for this
v
,
Q(v)>2^{\Omega(d)}\lambda^{1/4}
, we know that the hypothesis in question is not close to
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
in parameter distance and can be ignored. On the other hand, any
v
with
Q(v)
this small must have
|v|<2^{O(d)}\lambda^{-1/2}
and
v^{T}\Sigma v<2^{O(d)}\lambda^{1/4}
. Note that the projection of
v
onto the
\ker(\Sigma_{i})^{\perp}
is either zero or has magnitude at least
2^{O(bd)}
. In the latter case, it would need to be the case that
Q(v)
is substantially larger. Thus, if such a hypothesis is close to
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
in parameter distance, then
v
is in the kernel of some
\Sigma_{i}
.

If our algorithm finds some
v
for some hypothesis, we then compute
v\cdot x
to error
\lambda
for each of our samples
x
. If
\mathcal{M}
really has a component with
v
in the kernel of its covariance matrix, all of the
x
’s taken from this component will have
v\cdot x
the same. This means that at least a
(3/2)\varepsilon
fraction of our samples
x
will have
v\cdot x
within
\lambda
of each other. Note that if
v
is not in the kernel of any covariance matrix of any
G_{i}
than
\mbox{\bf Var}[v\cdot G_{i}]
will be at least
2^{O(db)}
for each
i
, and with high probability we will not find this many close samples.

To summarize, if our algorithm applies this procedure to every component of every hypothesis and does not find such a
v
, then it cannot be the case that
\mathcal{M}
contains any components of weight more than
\poly_{k}(\varepsilon)
that are singular, and thus one of our original hypotheses must be close in total variational distance. We can then run a tournament to find a single one that is close. Otherwise, if we find such a
v
for which many points do have
v\cdot x
close by, then
v
must be a null vector of the covariance matrix of some
G_{i}
. Furthermore, all of the samples within
\lambda
of this common value of
v\cdot x
, with high probability are either errors or come from components contained in some lower dimensional subspace. We can determine what this subspace is by noting that it is defined by
v\cdot x=q
for some rational number
q
with bit-complexity at most
O(bd)
and using continued fractions on a good numerical approximation of
q
in order to determine its true value. Our algorithm can then recurse on the points in this subspace (a mixture of Gaussians in a lower dimensional space) and on the remaining points (which are from a mixture of fewer Gaussians), and return an appropriate mixture of the results.
